attached “to the right” of the geodesic path
\llbracket 0,p_{H}(t)\rrbracket
. We now introduce another measure-valued process closely related to the exploration process that informally codes at each time
t\geq 0
, the height of the subtrees attached “to the left” of the same geodesic path. This process will arise in our work when discussing spinal decompositions in Section
6
. Under
P
, we consider the measure-valued process
\eta:=(\eta_{t}:t\geq 0)
defined by

\eta_{t}({\mathrm{d}}r):=\beta\mathbbm{1}_{[0,H_{t}]}(r){\mathrm{d}}r+\mathop{\sum\limits_{0<s\leq t}}_{X_{s-}<I_{s,t}}(X_{s}-I_{s,t})\>\delta_{H_{s}}({\mathrm{d}}r),\quad t\geq 0.

(2.22)

The process
\eta
is also rcll with respect to the total variation distance of measures
[
17
, Corollary 3.1.6]
and takes values in
\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})

[
17
, Lemma 3.1.1]
. Further,
P
-a.s. we have
H(\eta_{t})=H(\rho_{t})
for every
t\geq 0
and the set
\{t\geq 0:\eta_{t}=0\}
coincides with (
2.17
). The pair
(\rho,\eta)
, is a strong Markov process
[
17
, Proposition 3.1.2]
. For a complete account on
(\eta_{t}:t\geq 0)
we refer to
[
17
, Section 3.1]
, see as well
[
30
]
for heuristics on
(\rho,\eta)
in terms of queuing systems. The argument we employed to define
H
under
N
can be easily extended to the pair

(\rho,\eta)
; it is plain that under
N
, we still have
H=H(\rho)=H(\eta)
and that properties (i)-(iii) still hold. As a straightforward consequence of our previous discussion and excursion theory for the reflected Lévy process
X-I
we deduce that, under
P
, if we write
(a_{i},b_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
for the connected component of
\{t\geq 0:X_{t}-I_{t}>0\}
, the random measure in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{2}
defined by

\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(-I_{a_{i}},\rho_{(a_{i}+\cdot)\wedge{b_{i}}},\eta_{(a_{i}+\cdot)\wedge{b_{i}}})}

(2.23)

is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(\ell){\mathrm{d}}\ell\,N({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\eta)
. Note that (iii) and our discussion on the process
\eta
immediately yields that under
P
, the measure
(0,0)\in\mathcal{M}_{f}^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
is regular and instantaneous for the Markov process
(\rho,\eta)
, and that
-I
is a local time. It then follows that the corresponding excursion measure away from
(0,0)
for the pair
(\rho,\eta)
is precisely
N({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\eta)
. The process
\eta
is often referred to as the dual of
\rho
. This terminology is justified by the following remarkable identity in distribution:

\big((\rho_{t},\eta_{t}):t\in[0,\sigma_{H(\rho)}]\big)\overset{(d)}{=}\big((\eta_{(\sigma_{H(\rho)}-t)-},\rho_{(\sigma_{H(\rho)}-t)-}):t\in[0,\sigma_{H(\rho)}]\big),\quad\text{under }N,

(2.24)

with the convention that
(\eta_{0-},\rho_{0-})=(\eta_{0},\rho_{0})
, we refer to
[
17
, Corollary 3.1.6]
for a proof.

Let us close this section with a discussion which concerns the reconstruction of
H,\rho,\eta
and
X
under
P
or
N
. Namely, we will prove that given any of these four processes, the remaining three can be recovered in a measurable. First, recall that
H,\rho
and
\eta
are functionals of
X
, and that
H=H(\rho)=H(\eta)
. Thus, it remains to check that one can recover
X
from the height process
H
. To this end, let us recall that by
[
17
, Lemma 1.3.2]
and the monotonicity of
t\mapsto I_{t}
we have:

\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}E\big[\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}\big|\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{0}^{s}{\mathrm{d}}r\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H_{r}<\varepsilon\}}+I_{s}\big|\big]=0,\quad\text{ for every }t\geq 0.

(2.25)

The convergence in the previous display yields that
-I
can also be thought of as the local time at
0
of
H
. In particular, by (
2.25
) we can find a decreasing subsequence
(\varepsilon_{k})_{k\geq 0}
of positive numbers converging to
0
along which
P
-a.s. we have

\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}\big|\varepsilon^{-1}_{k}\int_{0}^{s}{\mathrm{d}}r\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H_{r}<\varepsilon_{k}\}}+I_{s}\big|=0,\quad\text{ for every }t\geq 0.

(2.26)

From now on, we fix the subsequence
(\varepsilon_{k})_{k\geq 0}
. The following result is undoubtedly known, but since we have not been able to find a reference in the literature we give a quick proof.

Lemma 2.2
.

Under
P
and
N
and for each
t\geq 0
, the following convergence holds a.e.

X_{t}-I_{t}=\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\varepsilon^{-1}_{k}\int_{t}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}r\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<m_{H}(t,r)<H_{r}<m_{H}(t,r)+\varepsilon_{k}\}}.

(2.27)

By right-continuity of
X
, we deduce from the previous lemma and (
2.26
) that under
P
and
N
, we can recover the process
X
from
H
in a measurable way. We shall refer to the process
X
obtained from
H
under
P
(resp
N
) through (
2.26
) and (
2.27
) as the
\psi
-Lévy process (resp.
\psi
-Lévy excursion) associated with
H
.

Proof.

We only establish the lemma under
P
, the result under
N
follows by the same arguments. In this direction we fix
t\geq 0
and we start by expressing (
2.27
) solely in terms of
\rho
. To this end, recall from (
2.15
) the identity
X_{t}-I_{t}=\langle\rho_{t},1\rangle
and note that as a direct consequence of (
2.17
), the variable
\inf\{s\geq t:H_{s}=0\}
coincides with
\inf\{s\geq t:\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle=0\}
. Further, by the Markov property, the law of
(\rho_{s+t}:s\geq 0)
under
P
is
\textbf{P}_{\rho_{t}}
, where we recall that for
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
the measure
\textbf{P}_{\mu}
is the law under
P
of
\rho^{\mu}
as defined in (
2.19
). By our previous remarks, to obtain (
2.27
) it suffices to show that for every fixed
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, it holds that:

\langle\mu,1\rangle=\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\varepsilon^{-1}_{k}\int_{0}^{\inf\{s\geq 0:\rho^{\mu}_{s}=0\}}\hskip-22.76219pt\mathrm{d}r\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H(\rho^{\mu}_{r})\leq\min_{[0,r]}H(\rho^{\mu})+\varepsilon_{k}\}},\quad P\text{-a.s.}

(2.28)

By construction, under
P
we can write
H(\rho_{r}^{\mu})=H(\kappa_{-I_{r}}\mu)+H(\rho_{r})
as well as
\min_{[0,r]}H(\rho^{\mu})=H(\kappa_{-I_{r}}\mu)
for
r\geq 0
, and remark that
\inf\{s\geq 0:\rho^{\mu}_{s}=0\}
coincides with
T_{\langle\mu,1\rangle}=\inf\{s\geq 0:-I_{s}=\langle\mu,1\rangle\}
by (
2.19
). This gives that the convergence (
2.28
) can be written as

\langle\mu,1\rangle=\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\varepsilon^{-1}_{k}\int_{0}^{T_{\langle\mu,1\rangle}}\mathrm{d}r\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H_{r}\leq\varepsilon_{k}\}},\quad P\text{-a.s.}

However, the convergence in the last display clearly holds by (
2.26
). This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

In what follows, our analysis is carried over in terms of
\rho
, instead of
X
. In particular, we use the notation
H(\rho)
instead of
H
. This is due to the fact that the exploration process is better suited to study Lévy trees.

2.2.3
The Lévy snake

Fix a branching mechanism
\psi
as in Section
2.1.1
as well as the law of a continuous strong Markov process
\Pi=(\Pi_{y})_{y\in E}
with values in some fixed Polish space
(E,{\mathrm{d}}_{E})
. The goal of this section is to define the snake with spatial motion
\Pi
driven by the height process of a
\psi
-Lévy process. This will give rise to the notion of a
\Pi
-Markov process indexed by a
\psi
-Lévy tree. In this direction, recall that for
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
the measure
\mathbf{P}_{\mu}
stands for the law in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+}))
of the exploration process started from
\mu
. With a slight abuse of notation, it will be convenient to write
\rho=(\rho_{t}:t\geq 0)
for the canonical process in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+}))
. We shall argue that, for an appropriate subset of initial conditions
(\mu,{\mathrm{w}})\in\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E}
and pairs
(\psi,\Pi)
, the probability measure
Q_{\mathrm{w}}^{H(\rho)}({\mathrm{d}}W)
under
\mathbf{P}_{\mu}
is well defined in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{W}_{E})
and supported on
\mathcal{S}^{\circ}
. First, remark that it is imperative to consider initial conditions
(\mu,\mathrm{w})
for which
\zeta_{\mathrm{w}}=H(\mu)
, and ensuring the continuity of
H(\rho)
under
\mathbf{P}_{\mu}
. By the discussion following (
2.21
), it is clear that both conditions are verified for any pair
(\mu,{\mathrm{w}})
in the following subset:

\Theta:=\Big\{(\mu,{\mathrm{w}})\in\mathcal{M}_{f}^{0}\times\mathcal{W}_{E}:\penalty\ H(\mu)=\zeta_{{\mathrm{w}}}\Big\},

for
\mathcal{M}^{0}_{f}
defined as in (
2.21
). Next, recall that in Section
2.1.2
, we gave, for a continuous non-negative function
h
with
h(0)=\zeta_{\mathrm{w}}
, sufficient conditions [(i)-(ii) in Section
2.1.2
] on
(h,\Pi)
ensuring that
Q_{\mathrm{w}}^{h}
can be defined as a measure on
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{W}_{E})
with support on
\mathcal{S}^{\circ}
. We shall then enforce conditions on
\psi
and
\Pi
to ensure that
conditions (i)-(ii) from Section
2.1.2
are verified
\mathbf{P}_{\mu}
-a.s. by
(H(\rho),\Pi)
. To this end, we will always assume that
\psi
and
\Pi
verify
11
11

11

For instance, it can be checked that condition (

H
0

) is fulfilled if the Lévy tree has exponent
\psi(\lambda)=\lambda^{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in(1,2]
and
\Pi
is a linear Brownian motion.
the following hypothesis.

Hypothesis
(\textbf{H}_{0})
There exists a constant
C_{\Pi}>0
and two positive numbers
p,q>0
such that,

for every
y\in E
and
t\geq 0
, we have:

\hskip-42.67912pt\Pi_{y}\big(\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}{\mathrm{d}}_{E}(\xi_{s},y)^{p}\big)\leq C_{\Pi}\cdot t^{q},\hskip 19.91692pt\text{ and }\hskip 19.91692ptq\cdot(1-\Upsilon^{-1})>1,

(
\mathbf{H_{0}}
)

where
\Upsilon:=\sup\big\{r\geq 0:\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow\infty}\lambda^{-r}\psi(\lambda)=\infty\big\}.
12
12

12

Remark that the convexity of
\psi
ensures that
\Upsilon\geq 1
.

Let us explain the implications of these conditions.
Fix
(\mu,\mathrm{w})\in\Theta
and a pair
(p,q)
satisfying assumption (

H
0

). Note that in particular condition (i) from Section
2.1.2
is satisfied. By
[
17
, Theorem 1.4.4]
(see as well the discussion following assumption H
0
in page 18 of
[
37
]
), for every
\theta\in(0,1-1/\Upsilon)
, the height process
H(\rho)
is
\mathbf{P}_{\mu}
-a.s. locally
\theta
-Hölder continuous in the excursion intervals of
H(\rho)
over its running infimum. Thanks to the standing assumption
q\cdot(1-\Upsilon^{-1})>1
we can further impose on
\theta
that
q\theta>1
, so that condition (ii) from Section
2.1.2
is satisfied
\textbf{P}_{\mu}
–a.s. by
H(\rho)
for every such
\theta
. We are then in position to apply Proposition 2.2 in
[
37
]
which entails that
\textbf{P}_{\mu}
–a.s. the measure
Q_{{{\mathrm{w}}}}^{H(\rho)}
is well defined in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{W}_{E})
and is supported on
\mathcal{S}^{\circ}
. If, with a slight abuse of notation, we write
(\rho,W)
for the canonical process in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
, it now follows from our previous discussion that for every
(\mu,\text{w})\in\Theta
, we can define a probability measure in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
by setting

\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\text{w}}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,\>{\mathrm{d}}W):=\textbf{P}_{\mu}({\mathrm{d}}\rho)\>Q^{H(\rho)}_{\text{w}}({\mathrm{d}}W).

(2.29)

The process
(\rho,W)
under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,{\mathrm{w}}}
is called the
\psi
-Lévy snake with spatial motion
\Pi
started from
(\mu,{\mathrm{w}})
. The key reason to work with the pair
(\rho,W)
, instead of solely with
W
, is that the Lévy snake is a Markov process (while the process
W
is only Markovian when
\pi\neq 0
). Namely, if we write
\mathcal{F}:=(\mathcal{F}_{t}:t\in[0,\infty])
for its canonical filtration, by
[
17
, Theorem 4.1.2]
the family
((\rho,W),(\mathbb{P}_{\mu,{\mathrm{w}}}:(\mu,{\mathrm{w}})\in\Theta))
is a strong Markov process with respect to the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t+}:\penalty\ t\in[0,\infty])
. We stress that by construction, under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,{\mathrm{w}}}
for
(\mu,{\mathrm{w}})\in\Theta
and outside of a negligible set, the process
W
takes values in
\mathcal{S}^{\circ}
, and its lifetime process
\zeta
is continuous and indistinguishable from
H(\rho)
. These facts will be used frequently in the sequel.

Let us now introduce the notion of
snake path
which summarises the regularity properties of
(\rho,W)
that we shall need. We denote systematically the elements of the space
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
by:

(\uprho,\omega)=\big((\uprho_{s},\omega_{s}):\,s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\big).

In particular, by definition we have
(\rho_{s}(\uprho),W_{s}(\omega))=(\uprho_{s},\omega_{s})
for
s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Definition 2.3

(Snake paths)
.

Fix
(\mu,{\mathrm{w}})\in\Theta
. An element
(\uprho,\omega)\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
is called a snake path started from
(\mu,{\mathrm{w}})
if the following properties hold:

(S1)

(\uprho_{0},\omega_{0})=(\mu,{\mathrm{w}})
and
\omega\in\mathcal{S}^{\circ}_{\mathrm{w}}
;

(S2)

For every
s\geq 0
, we have
(\uprho_{s},\omega_{s})\in\Theta
; in particular
H(\uprho)=\zeta(\omega)
.

The family of snake paths started from some fixed
(\mu,{\mathrm{w}})\in\Theta
is denoted by
\mathcal{S}_{\mu,{{\mathrm{w}}}}
, and when
(\mu,{\mathrm{w}})
is of the form
(0,y)
for some
y\in E
, we simply write
\mathcal{S}_{y}
.
We set

\mathcal{S}:=\bigcup_{(\mu,{\mathrm{w}})\in\Theta}\mathcal{S}_{\mu,{\mathrm{w}}}

for the collection of snake paths.

When working with a snake path
(\uprho,\omega)
, the equivalent notations
\zeta_{s}(\omega)
and
H(\uprho_{s})
for
s\geq 0
will be used indifferently, and note that in particular
\sigma_{H(\uprho)}
and
\sigma(\omega)
coincide. The continuity of
H(\rho)
and
\zeta
under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,{\mathrm{w}}}
for
(\mu,{\mathrm{w}})\in\Theta
paired with the fact that
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,{\mathrm{w}}}
a.s. the process
W
belongs to
\mathcal{S}^{\circ}
, immediately gives by construction of the measure
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,{\mathrm{w}}}
that the process
((\rho,W),(\mathbb{P}_{\mu,{\mathrm{w}}}:(\mu,{\mathrm{w}})\in\Theta))
takes a.s. values in
\mathcal{S}
. Said otherwise, for each
(\mu,{\mathrm{w}})\in\Theta
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\text{w}}\text{-a.s.}
we have

\zeta_{s}=H(\rho_{s}),\>\>\text{ for every }s\geq 0,

and for any
t,t^{\prime}\geq 0
,

W_{t}(r)=W_{t^{\prime}}(r),\>\>\text{ for all }0\leq r\leq m_{H(\rho)}(t,t^{\prime}).

Recalling the discussion at the end of Section
2.1.2
, under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\text{w}}
and outside of a negligible set, we can consider the tree-indexed process
(\widehat{\omega}_{a}:\penalty\ a\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)})
and we call it the Markov process
\Pi
indexed by the
\psi
-Lévy tree
\mathcal{T}_{H(\rho)}
started from
(\mu,\text{w})
. It is straightforward to check from the definition that
\mathcal{S}
is a measurable subset of the space
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
.

The study of the Lévy snake and the geometric properties of the corresponding tree-indexed process is often performed by working with restrictions of
(\rho,W)
to different sub-intervals of interest. We now introduce a notion that is well suited for this purpose.

Subtrajectories.
Let us start by introducing some notation. First, for
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and
z\geq 0
, we let
\uptheta_{z}(\mu)
be the element of
\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
defined by the relation:

\langle\uptheta_{z}(\mu),f\rangle:=\int\mu({\mathrm{d}}r)f(r-z)\mathbbm{1}_{\{r>z\}},

(2.30)

where
f:\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is any measurable bounded function. Similarly, for

{\mathrm{w}}\in\mathcal{W}_{E}
and
z\geq 0
satisfying the additional condition
z\leq\zeta_{\mathrm{w}}
, we write
\uptheta_{z}({\mathrm{w}})
for the shifted path
{\mathrm{w}}(z+r),r\in[0,\zeta_{\mathrm{w}}-z]
.
Now, consider an arbitrary
(\uprho,\omega)\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
and fix
0\leq s_{1}<s_{2}\leq\infty
such that
\zeta_{s_{1}}(\omega)\leq\zeta_{t}(\omega)
for every
t\in[s_{1},s_{2}]
. The subtrajectory of
(\uprho,\omega)
associated with
[s_{1},s_{2}]
is the element of
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
defined by

\mathscr{T}_{s_{1},s_{2}}(\uprho,\omega):=\big(\uptheta_{\zeta_{s_{1}}(\omega)}(\uprho_{(s_{1}+t)\wedge s_{2}}),\uptheta_{\zeta_{s_{1}}(\omega)}(\omega_{(s_{1}+t)\wedge s_{2}})\big),\quad\text{ for }t\geq 0.

(2.31)

Let us briefly comment on the use of this definition in our main case of interest. Consider an arbitrary element
(\uprho,\omega)
from
\mathcal{S}
. Let
0\leq s_{1}<s_{2}\leq\sigma(\omega)
be as before and assume additionally that
\zeta_{s_{1}}(\omega)=\zeta_{s_{2}}(\omega)
. In this case, it follows by construction that
\mathscr{T}_{s_{1},s_{2}}(\uprho,\omega)
belongs to
\mathcal{S}_{\widehat{\omega}_{s_{1}}}
. One can think of
\mathscr{T}_{s_{1},s_{2}}(\uprho,\omega)
as coding the labels of the restricted process
(\widehat{\omega}_{v}:\penalty\ v\in p_{H(\uprho)}([s_{1},s_{2}]))
.

We conclude this section by introducing the excursion measure of the Lévy snake and by recalling some of its main properties. Identity (
2.17
) combined with the snake property yields that under
\mathbb{P}_{0,y}
for every
y\in{E}
, the point
(0,y)\in\mathcal{M}_{f}^{0}\times\mathcal{W}_{{E}}
is regular and instantaneous for
(\rho,{W})
and that
-I
is a local time for
(\rho,{W})
at
(0,y)
. We write
\mathbb{N}_{y}
for the corresponding excursion measure in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
. Let
(a_{i},b_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be the connected components of
\{t\geq 0:\rho_{t}\neq 0\}
and for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, write
(\rho^{i},{W}^{i})
for the subtrajectory associated with the excursion interval
(a_{i},b_{i})
. By excursion theory, under
\mathbb{P}_{0,y}
the measure

\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(-I_{a_{i}},\rho^{i},{W}^{i})}

(2.32)

is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(\ell){\mathrm{d}}\ell\,\mathbb{N}_{y}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}{W})
. Since the excursion measure of
\rho
under
\mathbb{P}_{0,y}
is
N({\mathrm{d}}\rho)
, it readily follows from the form of the conditional law of
{W}
given
\rho
that the measure
\mathbb{N}_{y}
writes:

\mathbb{N}_{y}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,\>{\mathrm{d}}{W})=N({\mathrm{d}}\rho)\>Q^{H(\rho)}_{y}({\mathrm{d}}{W}).

(2.33)

Recalling that
\eta
under
N({\mathrm{d}}\rho)
is a functional of
\rho
, it follows that
(\rho,\eta)
under
\mathbb{N}_{y}
is distributed as
(\rho,\eta)
under the excursion measure
N
and, conditionally on the pair
(\rho,\eta)
, the process
{W}
has the law of a snake driven by
H(\rho)
with spatial motion
\Pi_{y}
. Under
\mathbb{N}_{y}
we write
\sigma
for the lifetime of
\zeta
, which coincides with
\sigma_{H(\rho)}
. Let us briefly comment on the Markovian character of
\mathbb{N}_{y}
as well as on its properties under time-reversal. Starting with the latter, by the disintegration of
\mathbb{N}_{y}
given above and (
2.24
), under
\mathbb{N}_{y}
the following identity holds in distribution:

\big((\rho_{t},\eta_{t},{W}_{t}):t\in[0,\sigma]\big)\overset{(d)}{=}\big((\eta_{(\sigma-t)-},\rho_{(\sigma-t)-},{W}_{\sigma-t}):t\in[0,\sigma]\big).

(2.34)

We refer to the identity in the last display as the duality property of the Lévy snake. Further, the Lévy snake is still a strong Markov process under
\mathbb{N}_{y}
, and the strong Markov property under
\mathbb{N}_{y}
takes the following form. Let
T>0
be a
\mathcal{F}_{t+}
-stopping time and consider an arbitrary non-negative
\mathcal{F}_{T+}
-measurable function
\Phi
. By
[
17
, Theorem 4.1.2]
, for every nonegative measurable function
F
on
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
, we have
13
13

13

Let us mention that
[
17
, Theorem 4.1.2]
is stated for snakes taking values on a more general space than
\mathcal{W}_{{E}}
, namely the space of killed
E
-valued rcll paths endowed with a different metric than
d_{\mathcal{W}_{E}}
. It is however straightforward to check that the proof of
[
17
, Theorem 4.1.2]
still holds in our framework.

\mathbb{N}_{y}\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\{T<\infty\}}\Phi\cdot F(\rho_{T+s},{W}_{T+s}:s\geq 0)\big)=\mathbb{N}_{y}\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\{T<\infty\}}\Phi\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{T},{W}_{T}}^{\dagger}[F]\big)

where we write
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,{{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\dagger}
for the law of
(\rho,W)
stopped at time
\inf\{s\geq 0:\rho_{s}=0\}
under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,{\mathrm{w}}}
.

3
Setup for the excursion theory

In this section we fix the framework that we shall consider for the rest of this manuscript and under which we develop the excursion theory. We work under the same assumptions as in the companion paper
[
37
, Sections 4-5]
, these are restated here for the reader’s convenience.

Fix a Polish space
E
and the family of distributions
\Pi:=(\Pi_{y})_{y\in E}
on the canonical space
\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+},E)
of an
E
-valued continuous strong Markov process. As usual, we write
\xi=(\xi_{t}:t\geq 0)
for the canonical process on
\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+},E)
. We assume the existence of a reference point
x
in
E
satisfying the following conditions:

\bm{x}\>\>\textbf{is regular, instantaneous and recurrent for}\>\>{\xi},

\mathbf{(H_{1})}

and

{\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}t\>\mathbbm{1}_{\{\xi_{t}={x}\}}=0},\quad\quad\Pi_{x}-\text{a.s. }

\mathbf{(H_{2})}

The first two conditions in hypothesis

(

H
1

)

ensure the existence of a local time at
x
for the Markov process, and we denote it by
\mathcal{L}=(\mathcal{L}_{t}:t\geq 0)
. The recurrence
hypothesis is assumed for convenience, and we expect our results to hold without it up to minor modifications. The local time
\mathcal{L}
is unique up to a multiplicative constant, that we fix arbitrarily, and we write
\mathcal{N}
for the corresponding (infinite) excursion measure. We set
\uptau(\xi):=\inf\{s>0:\xi_{s}=x\}
for the return time to
x
of
\xi
, and to simplify notation when there is no risk of confusion we simply denote it by
\uptau
. If we write
(a_{i},b_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
for the excursion intervals away from
x
of
\xi
and we let
(\xi^{i,*})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be the corresponding excursions with respective duration
\uptau_{i}:=\uptau(\xi^{i,*})
, the point measure

\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(\mathcal{L}_{a_{i}},\xi^{i,*})}

(3.1)

under
\Pi_{x}
is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(t){\mathrm{d}}t\otimes\mathcal{N}
. Note that since
x
is recurrent, the duration of each excursion is finite
\Pi_{x}
-a.s. Since the family
(a_{i},b_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
are the connected components of
\mathbb{R}_{+}\setminus\{t\geq 0:\,\xi_{t}=x\}
, condition

(

H
2

)

ensures that for any non-negative measurable function
f:E\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
we have
\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}sf(\xi_{s})=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{0}^{\uptau_{i}}{\mathrm{d}}s\,f(\xi_{s}^{i,*}),\,\Pi_{x}
–a.s. This fact will be used frequently in our computations without further notice, but it is by no means the reason behind working under

(

H
2

)

. The main implication of

(

H
2

)

is postponed for now and discussed in (
3.4
) below, as it concerns the geometry of the corresponding tree indexed process. It will be crucial for us to not only work with the Markov process
\Pi
indexed by the underlying Lévy tree, but to keep track as well of the value of its local time along the branches of the tree. This can be easily solved by working instead with the pair
\overline{\xi}_{s}:=(\xi_{s},\mathcal{L}_{s})
,
s\geq 0
, under
\Pi_{y}
for
y\in E
. Namely, this is still a strong Markov process taking values in the Polish space
\overline{E}:=E\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
, that we equip with the product metric
d_{\overline{E}}
. We write
\Pi_{y,r}
for its law on
\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\overline{E})
started from an arbitrary point
(y,r)\in\overline{E}
and for convenience we assume that
\overline{\xi}
is the canonical process in
\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\overline{E})
. We still write
\overline{\xi}=(\xi,\mathcal{L})
for its first and second component and to simplify notation, we set
\overline{\Pi}:=({\Pi}_{y,r})_{(y,r)\in\overline{E}}
. Under
{\Pi}_{y,r}
, we also use the notation
\uptau
for
\inf\{t>0:\xi_{t}=x\}
.

We next introduce another family of measures, closely related to the excursion measure, that will play a crucial role in our work. For each
r\geq 0
we let
\mathcal{N}_{x,r}
be the law in
\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\overline{E})
of the path obtained by concatenating
(({\xi}_{t},r):0\leq t<\uptau)
under
\mathcal{N}
, with an independent process distributed according to
\Pi_{x,r}
. This somewhat informal description can be made precise by working in the product space
\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\overline{E})^{2}
under the product measure
\mathcal{N}\otimes\Pi_{x,r}
, and by considering a concatenation operation like the one described in
[
39
, Chapter II - 14]
. Since this procedure is standard we skip the details. Heuristically, the measure
\mathcal{N}_{x,r}
codes the law of the Markov process and its local time shifted at the beginning of an excursion away from
x
, when the value of the local time at the start of the excursion is given by
r\geq 0
. It is not difficult to check that the process
\overline{\xi}
under
\mathcal{N}_{x,r}
is still Markovian in the following sense. For any
t>0
and every non-negative
\sigma(\overline{\xi}_{s}:0\leq s\leq t)
- measurable function
\Phi
we have

\mathcal{N}_{x,r}\big(\Phi\cdot G(\bar{\xi}_{t+s}:s\geq 0)\big)=\mathcal{N}_{x,r}\big(\Phi\cdot{\Pi}_{\bar{\xi}_{t}}[G]\big),

(3.2)

where
G
is an arbitrary non-negative measurable function on
\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\overline{E})
. Let us mention that the collection
({\mathcal{N}}_{x,r}:r\geq 0)
fall in the much more general framework of exit systems introduced by Maisonneuve in
[
33
]
, and the heuristic description of the measure
\mathcal{N}_{x,r}
given above is justified by the so called exit formula
[
33
, Theorem 4.1]
. See as well
[
33
, Theorem 5.1]
for a proof of the Markov property in a much more general setting.

Let us now return to the setting of Lévy snakes. Recall that we assume that
(\psi,\Pi)
satisfies (

H
0

), and that we write
\mathbb{N}_{y}
,
y\in E
, and
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,{\mathrm{w}}}
,
(\mu,{\mathrm{w}})\in\Theta
, for the family of measures associated with the
(\psi,\Pi)
-Lévy snake introduced in Section
2.1.2
. In order to keep track of the local time along the branches of the tree, we will often need to work with the
(\psi,\overline{\Pi})
-Lévy snake, so that the spatial motion now consists in the Markov process paired with its local time
(\xi,\mathcal{L})
. This can be done as soon as additional regularity assumptions on the pair
(\psi,\overline{\Pi})
are satisfied. To this end, we shall henceforth assume that
(\psi,\overline{\Pi})
satisfies the following slightly stronger version of hypothesis (

H
0

).

Hypothesis
(\textbf{H}_{0}^{\prime})
. There exists a constant
C_{\overline{\Pi}}>0
and two positive numbers
p,q>0
such that,

for every
y\in E
and
t\geq 0
, we have:

\hskip-42.67912pt\Pi_{y,0}\Big(\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}d_{\overline{E}}\big((\xi_{s},\mathcal{L}_{s}),(y,0)\big)^{p}\Big)\leq C_{\overline{\Pi}}\cdot t^{q}\hskip 19.91692pt\text{ and }\hskip 19.91692ptq\cdot(1-\Upsilon^{-1})>1.

\mathbf{(H_{0}^{\prime})}

Hence, the framework that we have discussed in Section
2.2.3
can be applied to the motion
\overline{\Pi}
and it allows us to work, when required, with the
\psi
-Lévy snake with spatial motion
\overline{\Pi}
.

Let us briefly comment on some notations that will be used when working with the
(\psi,\overline{\Pi})
-Lévy snake. Every element of
\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}
can be written in the form
\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}=({\mathrm{w}},\ell)
for some
{\mathrm{w}}\in\mathcal{W}_{E}
and
\ell\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
with identical lifetimes. We use respectively the notation
\overline{\Theta},\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ},\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}},\overline{\mathcal{S}}
for the sets defined as
\Theta,\mathcal{S}^{\circ},\mathcal{S}_{\mu,{\mathrm{w}}},\mathcal{S}
but associated with the Polish space
\overline{E}
. For every
(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}
the law of the
(\psi,\overline{\Pi})
-Lévy snake started from
(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})
is denoted by
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}
, and for every
(y,r)\in\overline{E}
we let
\mathbb{N}_{y,r}
be its excursion measure away from
(y,r)
; note that these are now measures on
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
. Further, we write
(\rho,\overline{W})=(\rho,W,\Lambda)
for the canonical process in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
, where
W_{s}:[0,\zeta_{{s}}]\mapsto E
and
\Lambda_{s}:[0,\zeta_{{s}}]\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
for every
s\geq 0
, and we write
\overline{\mathcal{F}}:=(\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{t}:t\in[0,\infty])
for its canonical filtration.
Since the law of
(\rho,W)
under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}
and
\mathbb{N}_{y,r}
is respectively
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,{{\mathrm{w}}}}
and
\mathbb{N}_{y}
, when the local time of the motion is not needed in our reasoning we systematically work under the latter measures. Note from the definition of
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}
that for every
(y,r)\in\overline{E}
and under
\mathbb{P}_{0,y,r}
, for each fixed
s\geq 0
and conditionally on
\zeta_{s}
, the pair
(W_{s},\Lambda_{s})=\big((W_{s}(h),\Lambda_{s}(h)\big):h\in[0,\zeta_{s}]\big)
is distributed as
(\xi_{h},\mathcal{L}_{h})_{h\in[0,\zeta_{s}]}
under
\Pi_{y,r}
. In particular, the Lebesgue-Stieltjes measure induced by
\Lambda_{s}
is supported on the closure of
\{h\in[0,\zeta_{s}):W_{s}(h)=x\}
,
\mathbb{P}_{0,y,r}
–a.e. Note however that this property might fail if we work under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}
for an arbitrary
(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}
.
Therefore, it will be convenient for our purposes to impose further restrictions on the set of initial conditions
(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}
that we shall work with. In this direction, we let
\overline{\Theta}_{x}
be the subset of
\overline{\Theta}
conformed by pairs
(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})
, with
\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}=({\mathrm{w}},\ell)
,
satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

The measure
\mu
does not charge the set
\{h\in[0,\zeta_{\mathrm{w}}]:{\mathrm{w}}(h)=x\}
, i.e.

\int_{[0,\zeta_{{\mathrm{w}}}]}\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{{\mathrm{w}}(h)=x\}}=0.

(ii)

\ell
is a non-decreasing continuous function and the support of its Lebesgue-Stieltjes measure is

\overline{\big\{h\in[0,\zeta_{\mathrm{w}}):{\mathrm{w}}(h)=x\big\}}.

The following lemma taken from
[
37
]
justifies our definition for
\overline{\Theta}_{x}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

[
37
, Lemma 5]

For every
(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
and
(y,r)\in\overline{E}
, the process
(\rho,\overline{W})
under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}
and
\mathbb{N}_{y,r}
takes values in
\overline{\Theta}_{x}
a.e.

This holds for instance for initial conditions
(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})
of the form
(0,y,r)
for
(y,r)\in\overline{E}
, making the space
\overline{\Theta}_{x}
the natural subset of initial condition to work with.
With a slight abuse of notation, for every
(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
and
(y,r)\in\overline{E}
, under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}
and
\mathbb{N}_{y,r}
we write

\big((\widehat{W}_{a},\widehat{\Lambda}_{a}):a\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\rho)}\big)

(3.3)

for the labels induced on
\mathcal{T}_{H(\rho)}
by
(\widehat{W},\widehat{\Lambda})
in the sense of Section
2.1.2
. Finally, assumption

(

H
2

)

entails that, for every
(\mu,{\mathrm{w}})\in{\Theta}_{x}
and
y\in{E}
, under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,{{\mathrm{w}}}}
and
\mathbb{N}_{y}
, we have:

\big\{a\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\rho)}:\widehat{W}_{a}=x\big\}\cap\big\{a\in\text{Mult}_{3}(\mathcal{T}_{H(\rho)})\cup\text{Mult}_{\infty}(\mathcal{T}_{H(\rho)}):\,a\neq 0\big\}=\emptyset

(3.4)

and we refer to
[
37
, Proposition 4.4]
for a proof. In words, since branching points in a Lévy tree can only be of multiplicity
3
or infinite, the process
(\widehat{W}_{a}:a\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\rho)})
can not take the value
x
at a branching point in
\mathcal{T}_{H(\rho)}\setminus\{0\}
.

4
Excursions and trajectories stemming from
x

In this section we introduce, the notion of excursion away from
x
, the one of a trajectory stemming away from
x
, and related functionals crucial in their study. Our definitions are reminiscent from classical excursion theory of
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-indexed processes. First, we shall need a notion of trajectory
(\uprho,\omega)\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
stopped at its first return to
x
. To this end, we define in Section
4.1
a truncation operation on the elements of
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
which informally, prunes each
(\uprho_{s},\omega_{s})
, for
s\geq 0
, upon its first return to
x
. For functions in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with values in
E
, the interface between the function stopped at its first return to
x
and the piece of path coming afterwards consists of a single point. In our setting this scenario is more complex, since the interface consists of a family of points that typically are of fractal nature. Making use of the theory of exit local times, in Section
4.1
we introduce a functional well suited to study these interfaces. Section
4.2
is then devoted to the definition of excursions away from
x
, trajectories stemming from
x
, and to addressing some elementary geometric properties closely related to these objects.

4.1
Truncation and exit local times

We start defining the truncation operation of an arbitrary element
(\uprho,\omega)\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
, that we fix from now on. To this end we first need to introduce some notations. For
{\mathrm{w}}\in\mathcal{W}_{E}
, we write

\uptau({\mathrm{w}}):=\inf\{t>0:{\mathrm{w}}(t)=x\},

(4.1)

for the first return time to
x
of the path
{\mathrm{w}}
and we set

V_{t}(\uprho,\omega):=\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,{1}_{\{H(\uprho_{s})\leq\uptau(\omega_{s})\}},\quad t\geq 0.

(4.2)

When
V_{\sigma(\omega)}(\uprho,\omega)>0
, we denote the right-inverse of
(V_{t}(\uprho,\omega):t\geq 0)
by
(\Gamma_{t}(\uprho,\omega):t\geq 0)
, i.e.

\Gamma_{t}(\uprho,\omega)=\inf\big\{s\geq 0:V_{s}(\uprho,\omega)>t\big\},\quad\text{for }t\in[0,V_{\sigma(\omega)}(\uprho,\omega)),

(4.3)

with the convention that
\Gamma_{t}(\uprho,\omega)=\Gamma_{V_{\sigma}-}(\uprho,\omega)
, for
t\geq V_{\sigma(\omega)}(\uprho,\omega)
. For definiteness, when
V_{\sigma(\omega)}(\uprho,\omega)=0
we simply set
\Gamma_{t}(\uprho,\omega)=0
, for every
t\geq 0
. We shall write
\mathrm{tr}(\uprho,\omega)
for the element of
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
defined by the relation:

\mathrm{tr}(\uprho,\omega)_{t}:=(\uprho_{\Gamma_{t}(\uprho,\omega)},\omega_{\Gamma_{t}(\uprho,\omega)}),\quad\quad\text{ for }t\geq 0.

(4.4)

We write
\text{tr}(\uprho)
and
\text{tr}(\omega)
respectively for the first and second coordinate of
\text{tr}(\uprho,\omega)
. We stress that
\text{tr}(\uprho,\omega)
is rcll since
\uprho,\omega
and
\Gamma(\uprho,\omega)
are rcll processes. Observe that if
V_{\sigma(\omega)}(\uprho,\omega)=0
the truncation
\mathrm{tr}(\uprho,\omega)
only takes the value
(\uprho_{0},\omega_{0})
. Hence, this operation is only of interest when applied to elements
(\uprho,\omega)
for which
V_{\sigma(\omega)}(\uprho,\omega)
is non-null. This is for instance the case if
(\uprho,\omega)\in\mathcal{S}
and for some
t\geq 0
we have
\uptau(\omega_{t})>0
, and this is the scenario we are interested in. Roughly speaking, when applied to an element
(\uprho,\omega)
of
\mathcal{S}
, since
H(\uprho)=\zeta(\omega)
the truncation operation removes the trajectories
(\uprho_{s},\omega_{s})
from
(\uprho,\omega)
for which
\uptau(\omega_{s})<\zeta_{s}(\omega)
and glues the remaining endpoints. We also stress that when
(\uprho,\omega)
is an element of
\mathcal{S}
, the truncation
\mathrm{tr}(\uprho,\omega)
is still in
\mathcal{S}
since
\mathrm{tr}(\omega)
is a snake trajectory by
[
2
, Proposition 10]
, and the others conditions in Definition
2.3
are clearly satisfied.

The goal now is to define a function “counting” the time spent by
(\uprho,\omega)
at those values of
s\geq 0
at which
\uptau(\omega_{s})=H(\uprho_{s})
. To this end, we now introduce a notion of
exit local time
suited for this purpose; we refer to
[
17
, Section 4.3]
and
[
37
, Section 3.1]
for background and the general theory. Recalling the definition of the sequence
(\varepsilon_{k})_{k\geq 0}
introduced in (
2.26
), we consider the functional
L:\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
defined for every
t\geq 0
by the relation

L_{t}(\uprho,\omega):=\liminf_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(\omega_{s})<H(\uprho_{s})<\uptau(\omega_{s})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}.

(4.5)

When the previous convergence holds uniformly in compact intervals, with a limit instead of a liminf, the process
L(\uprho,\omega)=(L_{s}(\uprho,\omega):s\geq 0)
is continuous and non-decreasing. In particular, we can consider the associated Stieltjes measure
{\mathrm{d}}L(\uprho,\omega)
which by (
4.5
) is
supported on
\{s\geq 0:\penalty\ H(\uprho_{s})=\uptau(\omega_{s})\}
. The following lemma establishes that the previous discussion holds for a.e.
(\uprho,\omega)
under
\mathbb{P}_{0,y}
and
\mathbb{N}_{y}
, for
y\in{E}
, with
y\neq x
.

Lemma 4.1
.

For every
y\in{E}
with
y\neq x
, under
\mathbb{P}_{0,y}
and
\mathbb{N}_{y}
, a.e. for every
M\geq 0
we have

\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\sup_{t\leq M}\Big|\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\uptau(W_{s})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}-L_{t}(\rho,W)\Big|=0.

(4.6)

Proof.

We fix an arbitrary
y\in E
and we start by establishing that the convergence (
4.6
) holds under
\mathbb{P}_{0,y}
. For every
t\geq 0
, set

\sigma_{t}:=\inf\Big\{s\geq 0:\>\int_{0}^{s}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{H(\rho_{u})>\uptau(W_{u})\}}>t\Big\},

and we consider the process
(\rho^{\prime}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
taking values in
\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
defined, for any bounded measurable function
f:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, by the relation:

\langle\rho^{\prime}_{t},f\rangle:=\int\rho_{\sigma_{t}}({\mathrm{d}}h)f\big(h-\uptau(W_{\sigma_{t}})\big)\mathbbm{1}_{\{h>\uptau(W_{\sigma_{t}})\}}.

(4.7)

Then, by Proposition 4.3.1 in
[
17
]
,
\rho^{\prime}
and
\rho
have the same distribution under
\mathbb{P}_{0,y}
. In particular,
\langle\rho^{\prime},1\rangle
has the same law as the reflected Lévy process
X-I
under
P
and, by (
2.26
), there exists a continuous process
\ell^{\prime}=(\ell^{\prime}(t):t\geq 0)
such that for every
M\geq 0
, we have

\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{t\leq M}\Big|\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H(\rho^{\prime}_{s})<\varepsilon_{k}\}}-\ell^{\prime}(t)\Big|=0,\quad\mathbb{P}_{0,y}\text{ a.s.}

(4.8)

Now, we define a continuous non-decreasing function
L^{\prime}=(L^{\prime}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
by setting

L^{\prime}_{t}=\ell^{\prime}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{H(\rho_{s})>\uptau(W_{s})\}}\Big),\quad t\geq 0

and we next check that (
4.6
) holds with
L
replaced by
L^{\prime}
. To simplify notation, we write
\widetilde{V}_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{H(\rho_{s})>\uptau(W_{s})\}}
for
t\geq 0
and observe that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\leq M}\Big|\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\uptau(W_{s})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}-L^{\prime}_{t}\Big|

\displaystyle=\sup_{t\leq M}\Big|\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\int_{0}^{\widetilde{V}_{t}}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{\sigma_{s}})<H(\rho_{\sigma_{s}})<\uptau(W_{\sigma_{s}})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}-\ell^{\prime}(\widetilde{V}_{t})\Big|

\displaystyle=\sup_{t\leq\widetilde{V}_{M}}\Big|\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H(\rho^{\prime}_{s})<\varepsilon_{k}\}}-\ell^{\prime}(t)\Big|,

where in the last equality we used that if
\uptau(W_{\sigma_{s}})<H(\rho_{\sigma_{s}})
, we have
H(\uprho_{{\sigma_{s}}})=\uptau(W_{{\sigma_{s}}})+H(\rho^{\prime}_{s})
. Now, the expression in last display converges a.s. to
0
by (
4.8
), completing the proof. The statement under
\mathbb{N}_{y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\varepsilon>0)
now easily follows by working under
\mathbb{P}_{0,y}
and by considering the first excursion of
(\rho,W)
away from
(0,y)
with duration
\sigma>\varepsilon
and by applying the previous result, we skip the details.
∎

We conclude this section by observing that for
(\uprho,\omega)\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
, the process
L(\uprho,\omega)
is not defined in the time-scale of
\mathrm{tr}(\uprho,\omega)
, but rather in the one of
(\uprho,\omega)
. For this reason, we will sometimes work with the time-changed version
L_{\Gamma_{t}(\uprho,\omega)}(\uprho,\omega),

t\geq 0.

4.2
Debut points

In this section we work with a fixed deterministic element
(\uprho,\omega)\in\mathcal{S}_{x}
. We work within
\mathcal{S}_{x}
rather than in the general space
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
to avoid pathological cases that can only occur on sets of null
\mathbb{N}_{x}
and
\mathbb{P}_{0,x}
measure. For definiteness, the notions defined below will be extended to elements in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
which are not in
\mathcal{S}_{x}
in a trivial way. Recall that on
\mathcal{S}_{x}
, the process
\zeta(\omega)
is continuous, null at
0
, and we have
H(\uprho)=\zeta(\omega)
. Recall as well that we write
(\widehat{\omega}_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)}}
for the corresponding tree-indexed process.

Closely related versions of the results that we now present have already been discussed in
[
2
, Section 3]
and we rely on similar arguments.

Definition 4.2
.

A point
u\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)}
is called an excursion debut for
(\widehat{\omega}_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)}}
if the following properties hold:

(i)

We have
\widehat{\omega}_{u}=x
;

(ii)

We can find
v\succeq u
such that
\widehat{\omega}_{a}\neq x
for every
a
in
\rrbracket u,v\llbracket
.

We denote the collection of excursion debuts by
\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega)
. For every
u\in\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega)
, we write
\mathcal{C}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)
for the subset of points
v\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)}
fulfilling that
v\succeq u
with
\widehat{\omega}_{a}\neq x
for every
a\in\,\rrbracket u,v\llbracket
and we set

\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{u}(\uprho,\omega):=\mathcal{C}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)\cap\{a\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)}:\widehat{\omega}_{a}\neq x\}.

For definiteness, when
(\uprho,\omega)
is not in
\mathcal{S}_{x}
we set
\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega)
as
\emptyset
and to avoid degenerate cases we henceforth assume that
\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega)
is non-empty. Let us briefly discuss some basic properties of the components
\mathcal{C}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)
, for
u\in\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega)
. First, note that by definition, if
u
is a debut point, then it belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)
. Moreover, since for every point
a\in\mathcal{C}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)
we have that
\llbracket u,a\rrbracket\subset\mathcal{C}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)
, the subset
\mathcal{C}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)
is path-wise connected. Hence,
\mathcal{C}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)
is a tree on its own right that we root at
u
, and it is not difficult to check from our definitions that it is a closed subset of
\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)}
. Using that the mapping
a\mapsto\widehat{\omega}_{a}
is continuous, it is easy to check that
\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega)
is countable, and that for every
u\in\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega)
the sets
\{a\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)}:\widehat{\omega}_{a}\neq x\}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)
are open. Remark however that in general,
\text{Int}(\mathcal{C}_{u}(\uprho,\omega))
and
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)
may differ. Indeed, the existence of an isolated point
b
of the set
\mathcal{C}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)\cap\{a\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)}:\widehat{\omega}_{a}=x\}
satisfying
b\in\text{Mult}_{1}(\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)})
would yield both that
b\in\text{Int}(\mathcal{C}_{u}(\uprho,\omega))
and
\widehat{\omega}_{b}=x
. Note that
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)
is not necessarily path connected (consider a component
\mathcal{C}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)
for an element
u\in\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega)
which is a branching point). However, as the next lemma shows, this property does hold as soon as this scenario has been ruled out.

Lemma 4.3
.

Suppose that
(\widehat{\omega}_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)}}
does not take the value
x
at branching points. Then, the family
(\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{u}(\uprho,\omega))_{u\in\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega)}
are the connected components of the open set

\{a\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)}:\widehat{\omega}_{a}\neq x\}
.

Proof.

Let us first prove that

\cup_{u\in\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega)}\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)=\{a\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)}:\widehat{\omega}_{a}\neq x\}.

The inclusion
\cup_{u\in\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega)}\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)\subset\{a\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)}:\widehat{\omega}_{a}\neq x\}
follows by definition, and to obtain the reverse one let us fix an arbitrary
v\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)}
such that
\widehat{\omega}_{v}\neq x
. Since the set
\llbracket 0,v\rrbracket\cap\{a\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)}:\widehat{\omega}_{a}\neq x\}
is non-empty, the continuity of
(\widehat{\omega}_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)}}
, combined with (
2.11
) and the fact that
\widehat{\omega}_{0}=x
, ensure that there exists a unique
u\in\llbracket 0,v\rrbracket
with
\widehat{\omega}_{u}=x
satisfying that
\widehat{\omega}_{a}\neq x
for every
a\in\rrbracket u,v\rrbracket
. The continuity of
(\widehat{\omega}_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)}}
in
\llbracket 0,v\rrbracket
and the fact that
\widehat{\omega}_{0}=x
then ensure that there exists a unique
u\in\llbracket 0,v\rrbracket
with
\widehat{\omega}_{u}=x
satisfying that
\widehat{\omega}_{a}\neq x
for every
a\in\rrbracket u,v\rrbracket
. It now follows from our definitions that
u
is an excursion debut and that
v\in\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)
, which proves the reverse inclusion. It remains to prove that the sets
(\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)
,
u\in\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega))
, are disjoint and that each
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)
is connected. For the latter, let us argue that for any
v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)
, we have

\llbracket v_{1},v_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket v_{1}\curlywedge v_{2},v_{1}\rrbracket\cup\llbracket v_{1}\curlywedge v_{2},v_{2}\rrbracket\subset\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{u}(\uprho,\omega),

where we recall that the notation
v_{1}\curlywedge v_{2}
stands for the common ancestor. The fact that both
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are descendents of
u
guarantees that
v_{1}\curlywedge v_{2}\in\llbracket u,v_{1}\rrbracket\cap\llbracket u,v_{2}\rrbracket
, and the equality
u=v_{1}\curlywedge v_{2}
is ruled out since
u
can not be a branching point by assumption. This ensures that
v_{1}\curlywedge v_{2}
is a strict descendent of
u
and since there is no point with label
x
in
\rrbracket u,u_{1}\curlywedge u_{2}\rrbracket
, we get that
u_{1}\curlywedge u_{2}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{u}^{\circ}(\uprho,\omega)
. The contention in the previous display now follows by definition of
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)
. Finally, let us check that if
u,u^{\prime}
are distinct debut points, then
\mathcal{C}_{u}^{\circ}(\uprho,\omega)
and
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{u^{\prime}}(\uprho,\omega)
are disjoint. Arguing by contradiction, if we had some
v\in\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)\cap\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{u^{\prime}}(\uprho,\omega)
, then it must hold that
u\preceq u^{\prime}\preceq v
or
u^{\prime}\preceq u\preceq v
. In any case, we get respectively that
u^{\prime}\in\,\rrbracket u,v\llbracket
with
\widehat{\omega}_{u^{\prime}}=x
and
u\in\,\rrbracket u^{\prime},v\llbracket
with
\widehat{\omega}_{u}=x
, in contradiction with the fact that
v
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)\cap\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{u^{\prime}}(\uprho,\omega)
.
∎

For every
u\in\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega)
, we refer to
(\widehat{\omega}_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{C}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)}
as the excursion away from
x
of
(\widehat{\omega}_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)}}
associated with the component
\mathcal{C}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)
. Each excursion away from
x
is a tree indexed process on its own right, and we shall now argue that it can be coded by a snake path. In order to be more precise, we recall the notation
\mathfrak{g}(u)
and
\mathfrak{d}(u)
from (
2.4
) for respectively the first time and the last time at which the exploration
p_{H(\uprho)}
visits the component
\mathcal{C}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)
. Then, we set:

(\uprho^{u},\omega^{u}):=\mathscr{T}_{\mathfrak{g}(u),\mathfrak{d}(u)}(\uprho,\omega)

(4.9)

for the subtrajectory
associated with the interval
[\mathfrak{g}(u),\mathfrak{d}(u)]
, where we recall the notation
\mathscr{T}_{\mathfrak{g}(u),\mathfrak{d}(u)}(\uprho,\omega)
from (
2.31
). Informally, the snake path
(\uprho^{u},\omega^{u})
encodes the restriction of
(\widehat{\omega}_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)}}
to
\{v\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\uprho)}:\penalty\ u\preceq v\}
, the subtree stemming from
u
. For this reason, we refer to
(\uprho^{u},\omega^{u})
as the subtrajectory stemming from
u
. The snake path coding the excursion
(\widehat{\omega}_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{C}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)}
is then obtained by pruning the paths of
(\uprho^{u},\omega^{u})
at its first return time to
x
, namely

(\uprho^{u,*},\omega^{u,*}):=\mathrm{tr}(\uprho^{u},\omega^{u}).

(4.10)

The process
(\uprho^{u,*},\omega^{u,*})
is an element of
\mathcal{S}_{x}
with (strictly) positive duration
\sigma(\omega^{u,*})
which encodes
(\widehat{\omega}_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{C}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)}
, in the sense that by construction the tree coded by
\zeta(\omega^{u,*})=H(\uprho^{u,*})
is isometric to the subset
\mathcal{C}_{u}(\uprho,\omega)
by an isometry preserving the root and the labels.

Definition 4.4
.

The family
((\uprho^{u,*},\omega^{u,*}):u\in\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega))
is referred to as the family of excursions away from
x
of the snake path
(\uprho,{\omega})
.

Recalling the functional
L
introduced in (
4.5
), we interpret
L_{\infty}(\uprho^{u},\omega^{u})
as the boundary size of
\mathcal{C}_{u}
. We conclude this section with a discussion under the measures
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
and
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
. We start by introducing some notations that will be used throughout this work. Under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
and
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, the process
(\rho,\overline{W}):=(\rho,W,\Lambda)
belongs a.e. to
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{x}
. Therefore,
(\rho,W)
is in
\mathcal{S}_{x}
a.e. and we can then consider, outside of a
negligible set, the sets
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{C}_{u},\mathcal{C}_{u}^{\circ}
for
u\in\mathcal{D}
taken at
(\rho,W)
. As usual, we omitted the dependence on
(\rho,W)
to simplify notation. We write

(\rho^{u},W^{u})_{u\in\mathcal{D}}\quad\text{ and }\quad(\rho^{u,*},W^{u,*})_{u\in\mathcal{D}}

for the corresponding family of subtrajectories stemming from
u\in\mathcal{D}
, as well as the family of excursions. Note that for
u\in\mathcal{D}
we have
p_{H(\rho)}^{-1}(\{u\})=\{\mathfrak{g}(u),\mathfrak{d}(u)\}
since
u
is not a branching point by (
3.4
); in particular, the statement of Lemma
4.3
holds under
\mathbb{N}_{x}
and
\mathbb{P}_{0,x}
. The next lemma shows in particular that under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
and
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, the local time process
\Lambda
can be used to index
the family of excursions away from
x
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
and
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, for every
u\in\mathcal{D}
the process
(\widehat{\Lambda}_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\rho)}}
is constant on
\mathcal{C}_{u}
and its value is given by
\widehat{\Lambda}_{u}
. Moreover, if
u^{\prime}
is another arbitrary element of
\mathcal{D}
with
u\neq u^{\prime}
, we have
\widehat{\Lambda}_{u}\neq\widehat{\Lambda}_{u^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

We only prove the result under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, the arguments under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
are identical. To simplify notation we write
H
for the height process instead of
H(\rho)
. We start proving the first claim and in this direction, recall from Lemma
3.1
that
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
-a.e., the Lévy snake
(\rho,\overline{W})
takes values in
\overline{\Theta}_{x}
. Therefore, we can consider a measurable subset
\Omega_{0}
of full
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
measure at which the process
(\rho_{t},\overline{W}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
stays in
\overline{\Theta}_{x}
, and for the rest of the argument we work on
\Omega_{0}
. Fix
u\in\mathcal{D}
, an arbitrary element
a\in\mathcal{C}_{u}\setminus\{u\}
, and let us prove that
\widehat{\Lambda}_{u}=\widehat{\Lambda}_{a}
. Recall that by definition, we have
\widehat{W}_{v}\neq x
for every
v\in\rrbracket u,a\llbracket
. The condition
u\preceq a
ensures that
H_{\mathfrak{g}(u)}=\min_{[\mathfrak{g}(u),\mathfrak{g}(a)]}H\leq H_{\mathfrak{g}(a)}
, and by (
2.11
) we get that

W_{\mathfrak{g}(a)}(h)\neq x
for every
h\in(H_{\mathfrak{g}(u)},H_{\mathfrak{g}(a)})
. Now, from the fact that
(\rho_{\mathfrak{g}(a)},\overline{W}_{\mathfrak{g}(a)})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
and the snake property we infer
\widehat{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{g}(a)}=\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}(a)}(H_{\mathfrak{g}(u)})=\widehat{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{g}(u)}
. This entails that
\widehat{\Lambda}_{u}=\widehat{\Lambda}_{a}
, and since
a
is arbitrary, we conclude that
\widehat{\Lambda}_{a}
,
a\in\mathcal{C}_{u}
, is identically equal to
\widehat{\Lambda}_{u}
.

The proof of the second claim of the lemma relies on the following elementary fact. Under
\Pi_{y,r}
for
(y,r)\in E\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
consider an independent copy
\xi^{\prime}
of
\xi
and write
\tau^{\prime}=\inf\{t>0:\xi^{\prime}_{t}=x\}
. Then, the excursion process of
(\xi_{\uptau+t})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\xi^{\prime}_{\uptau^{\prime}+t})_{t\geq 0}
are independent Poisson point measures with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{\{\ell>r\}}\mathrm{d}\ell\penalty\ \mathcal{N}
and therefore, the first entries in the atoms of these two measures are a.s. all pairwise distinct. Let us infer from this the second statement of the lemma. To this end, fix two arbitrary rational numbers
0<t<t^{\prime}
, write
t\curlywedge t^{\prime}
for an arbitrary element of
p_{H}^{-1}(p_{H}(t)\curlywedge p_{H}(t^{\prime}))
and denote the connected components of
\{h\in[H_{t\curlywedge t^{\prime}},H_{t}]:\penalty\ W_{t}(h)\neq x\}
(resp.
\{h\in[H_{t\curlywedge t^{\prime}},H_{t^{\prime}}]:\penalty\ W_{t^{\prime}}(h)\neq x\}
) that do not contain
H_{t\curlywedge t^{\prime}}
by
(a_{i},b_{i})_{i\in I}
(resp.
(a_{i}^{\prime},b_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in I^{\prime}}
). Note that both of these collections are empty only when
p_{H}(t)
and
p_{H}(t^{\prime})
belong to the same excursion component. Conditionally on
H
and
\overline{W}_{t}(H_{t\curlywedge t^{\prime}})
, the processes
(\overline{W}_{t}(H_{t\curlywedge t^{\prime}}+h))_{h\leq H_{t}-H_{t\curlywedge t^{\prime}}}
and
(\overline{W}_{t^{\prime}}(H_{t\curlywedge t^{\prime}}+h))_{h\leq H_{t^{\prime}}-H_{t\curlywedge t^{\prime}}}
are independent and distributed as
(\xi,\mathcal{L})
under
\Pi_{\overline{W}_{t}(H_{t\curlywedge t^{\prime}})}
stopped respectively at time
H_{t}-H_{t\curlywedge t^{\prime}}
and
H_{t^{\prime}}-H_{t\curlywedge t^{\prime}}
. From the elementary observation given above we get that the quantities
(\Lambda_{t}(a_{i}))_{i\in I}
and
(\Lambda_{t^{\prime}}(a_{i}^{\prime}))_{i\in I^{\prime}}
are all distinct when
I,I^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
. Since by Lemma
4.3
the sets
\mathcal{C}_{u}^{\circ}
for
u\in\mathcal{D}
are open, it is plain by (
2.11
) and the definition of debut points that this ensures
\widehat{\Lambda}_{u}
,
\widehat{\Lambda}_{u^{\prime}}
a.s. differ if
u,u^{\prime}
are distinct debut points.
∎

For every
u\in\mathcal{D}
, the local time process
\widehat{\Lambda}
is constant in the connected components
\mathcal{C}_{u}
, but this is no longer the case if we consider instead its restriction to the corresponding stemming subtree

\{v\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\rho)}:\penalty\ u\preceq v\}
containing
\mathcal{C}_{u}
. It will be in fact crucial for our purposes to enrich the family
(\rho^{u},W^{u})_{u\in\mathcal{D}}
by including the corresponding restriction of the local time
\Lambda
.

Definition 4.6
.

Under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
and
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, for every
u\in\mathcal{D}
, we shall write
(\rho^{u},\overline{W}^{u})
for the subtrajectory of
(\rho,\overline{W})
associated with the interval
[\mathfrak{g}(u),\mathfrak{d}(u)]
.

Note that under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
and
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, the lifetime of
W^{u}
is finite and given by
\mathfrak{d}(u)-\mathfrak{g}(u)
.

5
The measures
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
and
\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}

Now that we have defined the notion of an excursion away from
x
and the corresponding subtrajectory stemming from
x
, the next step is to construct candidates for the corresponding (infinite) measures. This task requires some preliminary constructions and notations that we will now introduce.

We consider the family of measures
\bar{R}_{a,b}(\overline{{\mathrm{w}}},{\mathrm{d}}\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}^{\prime})
for
\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}
,
0\leq a\leq\zeta_{\mathrm{w}}
and
b\geq a
introduced in Section
2.1.2
associated with
\overline{\Pi}:=({\Pi}_{y,r})_{(y,r)\in\overline{E}}
. In this section we work with a fixed
r\geq 0
and we recall the notation
\mathcal{N}_{x,r}
for the measure introduced in Section
3
. For every continuous function
h:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
t\geq 0
, we write
\nu^{h}_{t}({\mathrm{d}}\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})
for the law of
(({\xi}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{s}):0\leq s\leq h(t))
under
\mathcal{N}_{x,r}
. Note that
\nu_{t}^{h}
is a measure on
\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}
; the dependence on
r\geq 0
was only omitted to simplify notation. The interest in the family
(\nu_{t}^{h}:t\geq 0)
arises from the following result.

Proposition 5.1
.

Fix a continuous function
h:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
\sigma_{h}\in(0,\infty)
and
h(0)=h(\sigma_{h})=0
. There exists a unique measure
\mathbf{Q}^{h}_{x,r}({\mathrm{d}}\overline{W})
on
\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}^{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
such that, for every
n\geq 1
,
A_{0},A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
measurable subsets of
\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}
and
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<t_{2}\dots<t_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}^{h}_{x,r}\Big(

\displaystyle\overline{W}_{t_{0}}\in A_{0},\,\overline{W}_{t_{1}}\in A_{1},\dots,\overline{W}_{t_{n}}\in A_{n}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}_{A_{0}}(x,r)\int_{A_{1}}\nu_{t_{1}}^{h}({\mathrm{d}}\overline{\mathrm{w}}_{1})\int_{A_{2}\times\dots\times A_{n}}\bar{R}_{m_{h}(t_{1},t_{2}),h(t_{2})}(\overline{\mathrm{w}}_{1},{\mathrm{d}}\overline{\mathrm{w}}_{2})\dots\bar{R}_{m_{h}(t_{n-1},t_{n}),h(t_{n})}(\overline{\mathrm{w}}_{n-1},{\mathrm{d}}\overline{\mathrm{w}}_{n}).

(5.1)

Proof.

First, remark that for every
0<s<t
and every bounded measurable function
f:\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we have

\int_{\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}}\nu_{s}^{h}({\mathrm{d}}\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})\int_{\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}}\bar{R}_{m_{h}(s,t),h(t)}(\overline{{\mathrm{w}}},{\mathrm{d}}\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}^{\prime})f(\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}^{\prime})=\mathcal{N}_{x,r}\big(f(\overline{\xi}_{u}:0\leq u\leq h(t))\big)=\nu^{h}_{t}(f),

where in the first equality we applied the Markov property (
3.2
) at time
m_{h}(s,t)
. In other terms, it holds that:

\nu^{h}_{t}\big(\mathrm{d}\overline{\mathrm{w}}^{\prime}\big)=\int_{\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}}\nu^{h}_{s}\big(\mathrm{d}\overline{\mathrm{w}}\big)\bar{R}_{m_{h}(s,t),h(t)}(\overline{{\mathrm{w}}},\mathrm{d}\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}^{\prime}).

If we write
\mathfrak{Q}_{t_{0},t_{1},\dots t_{n}}
for the measure in
\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}^{n+1}
defined by the right-hand side of (
5.1
), our previous observation entails that the family of measures

\mathfrak{Q}_{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{n}}
, for
n\geq 1
and
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{n}
satisfies Kolmogorov’s consistency criterion. The statement of the proposition will now follow by a straightforward argument which involves Kolmogorov’s extension theorem. Note that since the measures
\mathfrak{Q}_{t_{0},t_{1},\dots t_{n}}
are infinite, we must proceed with some care. In this direction, fix
s>0
such that
h(s)>0
as well as an arbitrary
\delta>0
. For any collection of times
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{n}
, we let
\mathfrak{Q}_{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{n}}^{\delta}
be the measure in
\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}^{n+1}
defined at each measurable set
A_{0}\times A_{1}\times\dots\times A_{n}
as:

\mathfrak{Q}_{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{j},\dots,t_{n}}\big(A_{0}\times A_{1},\times\dots\times\big(A_{j}\cap\{\uptau({\mathrm{w}})>\delta\}\big)\times\dots A_{n}\big)

if
s=t_{j}
for some
1\leq j\leq n
, as

\mathfrak{Q}_{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{j},s,t_{j+1},\dots,t_{n}}\big(A_{0}\times A_{1},\times\dots A_{j}\times\{\uptau({\mathrm{w}})>\delta\}\times A_{j+1}\times\dots\times A_{n}\big)

if
t_{j}<s<t_{j+1}
for some
0\leq j\leq n-1
, and as

\mathfrak{Q}_{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{n},s}\big(A_{0}\times A_{1},\times\dots A_{n}\times\{\uptau({\mathrm{w}})>\delta\}\big)

if
t_{n}<s
. The family
\mathfrak{Q}_{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{n}}^{\delta}
for
n\geq 1
and
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{n}

still satisfies Kolmogorov’s consistency criterion, and each one of these measures has finite total mass given by
\nu_{s}^{h}(\uptau({\mathrm{w}})>\delta)\leq\mathcal{N}_{x,r}(\uptau(\xi)>\delta)<\infty
. Therefore, Kolmogorov’s extension theorem ensures the existence of a unique measure
\mathbf{Q}^{h,\delta}_{x,r}
in the product space
\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}^{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
with total mass
\nu_{s}^{h}(\uptau({\mathrm{w}})>\delta)
and having for finite-dimensional marginal distributions the measures
\mathfrak{Q}_{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{n}}^{\delta}
, for
n\geq 1
and
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{n}
. By construction, for every
0<\delta_{1}<\delta_{2}
, the measure
\mathbf{Q}_{x,r}^{h,\delta_{2}}
is the restriction of
\mathbf{Q}_{x,r}^{h,\delta_{1}}
to
\{\uptau(W_{s})>\delta_{2}\}
. Hence, we can consider the measure
\mathbf{Q}^{h}_{x,r}
in
\mathcal{W}^{\mathbb{R}_{+}}_{\overline{E}}
defined at every measurable subset
\mathscr{C}
by

\mathbf{Q}^{h}_{x,r}(\mathscr{C}):=\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\mathbf{Q}^{h,\delta}_{x,r}(\mathscr{C}).

Using that
\nu_{s}^{h}(\uptau({\mathrm{w}})=0)=0
, we infer by monotone convergence that
\mathbf{Q}^{h}_{x,r}
satisfies (
5.1
). Finally, the uniqueness comes from the fact that if
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}
also verifies (
5.1
), by uniqueness of
\mathbf{Q}^{h,\delta}_{x,r}
, for each
\delta>0
we get that the restriction of
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}
to
\{\uptau(W_{s})>\delta\}
must be
\mathbf{Q}^{h,\delta}_{x,r}
. Since
\{\uptau(W_{s})=0\}
is still a
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}
-null set, it now follows by monotone convergence
14
14

14

The uniqueness follows also easily from the monotone class theorem.
that
\mathbf{Q}^{h}_{x,r}=\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}
.
∎

By construction, for any fixed time
t\geq 0
, the variable
\overline{W}_{t}
under
{\textbf{Q}}^{h}_{x,r}
is distributed as
\overline{\xi}
under
\mathcal{N}_{x,r}
restricted to
[0,h(t)]
. For this reason, the canonical process
\overline{W}
under
\textbf{Q}_{x,r}^{h}
can be interpreted as the snake driven by
h
with spatial motion distributed according to
\mathcal{N}_{x,r}
. We stress that, in contrast to the framework presented in Section
2.1.2
, the measure
\mathcal{N}_{x,r}
is an infinite measure, and as a result,
\mathbf{Q}^{h}_{x,r}
is as well an infinite measure.

Lemma 5.2
.

Fix a continuous function
h:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
satisfying
\sigma_{h}\in(0,\infty)
, with
h(0)=h(\sigma_{h})=0
and set
h_{\sigma_{h}\shortminus\hskip 0.56905pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}:=(h((\sigma_{h}-t)\vee 0):t\geq 0)
. The following properties hold:

(i)

The distribution of
(\overline{W}_{(\sigma_{h}-t)\vee 0}:t\geq 0)
under
\mathbf{Q}^{h}_{x,r}
coincides with the law of

(\overline{W}_{t}:t\geq 0)
under
\mathbf{Q}^{h_{\sigma_{h}\shortminus\hskip 0.56905pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}}_{x,r}
.

(ii)

Let
q>0
be as in

(

H
0

′

)

. If
h
is
\theta
-Hölder-continuous with
\theta q>1
, the canonical process
\overline{W}
under
\mathbf{Q}_{x,r}^{h}
possesses a continuous modification.

Proof.

For
0\leq s<t\leq\sigma_{h}
, we shall write
P^{h}_{s,t}
and
P_{s,t}^{h_{\sigma_{h}\shortminus\hskip 0.56905pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}}
for the transition semigroup from time
s
to time
t
of the snake with spatial motion
\overline{\Pi}
with driving function
h
and
h_{\sigma_{h}\shortminus\hskip 0.56905pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}
respectively.
Note that its explicit expressions can be easily derived from (
2.8
). Starting with (i), observe that the result will follow as soon as we establish that

\nu_{\sigma_{h}-t}^{h}f_{2}P_{\sigma_{h}-t,\sigma_{h}-s}^{h}f_{1}=\nu_{s}^{h_{\sigma_{h}\shortminus\hskip 0.56905pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}}f_{1}P_{s,t}^{h_{\sigma_{h}\shortminus\hskip 0.56905pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}}f_{2}

(5.2)

for every
0<s<t<\sigma_{h}
and non-negative measurable functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
on
\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}
.
Indeed, if the previous identity holds, by inductively applying (
5.2
) and noting that
\nu_{t}^{h_{\sigma_{h}\shortminus\hskip 0.56905pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}}=\nu_{\sigma_{h}-t}^{h}
, we infer that for every
0<t_{1}<\dots<t_{k}<\sigma_{h}
and non-negative measurable functions
f_{1},\dots,f_{k}
on
\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}
,

\displaystyle\nu^{h}_{\sigma_{h}-t_{k}}f_{k}P_{\sigma_{h}-t_{k},\sigma_{h}-t_{k-1}}^{h}f_{k-1}\dots P_{\sigma_{h}-t_{2},\sigma_{h}-t_{1}}^{h}f_{1}=\nu^{h_{\sigma_{h}\shortminus\hskip 0.56905pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}}_{t_{1}}f_{1}P_{t_{1},t_{2}}^{h_{\sigma_{h}\shortminus\hskip 0.56905pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}}f_{2}\dots P_{t_{k-1},t_{k}}^{h_{\sigma_{h}\shortminus\hskip 0.56905pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}}f_{k}.

Since for
t\geq\sigma_{h}
we plainly have
W_{0}=W_{t}=(x,r)
, the equality in the last display yields that
(\overline{W}_{(\sigma_{h}-t)\vee 0}:t\geq 0)
under
\textbf{Q}^{h}_{x,r}
and

(\overline{W}_{t}:t\geq 0)
under
\textbf{Q}^{h_{\sigma_{h}\shortminus\hskip 0.56905pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}}_{x,r}
have the same finite-dimensional distributions, proving (i). Let us then establish (
5.2
). In this direction, note that the left-hand side of (
5.2
) is given by

\displaystyle\nu_{\sigma_{h}-t}^{h}f_{2}P_{\sigma_{h}-t,\sigma_{h}-s}^{h}f_{1}=\int_{\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}}\nu_{\sigma_{h}-t}^{h}({\mathrm{d}}\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})f_{2}(\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})\int_{\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}}\bar{R}_{m_{h}(\sigma_{h}-t,\sigma_{h}-s),h(\sigma_{h}-s)}(\overline{{\mathrm{w}}},{\mathrm{d}}\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}^{\prime})f_{1}(\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}^{\prime}).

Said otherwise, the law of the pair
(\overline{W}_{\sigma_{h}-t},\overline{W}_{\sigma_{h}-s})
under
\textbf{Q}^{h}_{x,r}
is characterised by the following two properties:

•

Up to time
m_{h}(\sigma_{h}-t,\sigma_{h}-s)
, the paths
\overline{W}_{\sigma_{h}-t}
and
\overline{W}_{\sigma_{h}-s}
coincide and are distributed as the canonical process
(\xi,\mathcal{L})
under
\mathcal{N}_{x,r}
restricted to the time-interval
[0,m_{h}(\sigma_{h}-t,\sigma_{h}-s)]
.

•

Conditionally on
\big(\overline{W}_{\sigma_{h}-t}(u):u\in[0,m_{h}(\sigma_{h}-t,\sigma_{h}-s)]\big)
, the restrictions

\overline{W}_{\sigma_{h}-t}\big(m_{h}(\sigma_{h}-t,\sigma_{h}-s)+u\big),\quad u\in\big[0,h(\sigma_{h}-t)-m_{h}(\sigma_{h}-t,\sigma_{h}-s)\big],

and

\overline{W}_{\sigma_{h}-s}\big(m_{h}(\sigma_{h}-t,\sigma_{h}-s)+u\big),\quad u\in\big[0,h(\sigma_{h}-s)-m_{h}(\sigma_{h}-t,\sigma_{h}-s)\big],

are independent with respective distributions
(\xi,\mathcal{L})
under
\overline{\Pi}_{y}
taken at
y={\overline{W}_{\sigma_{h}-t}(m_{h}(\sigma_{h}-t,\sigma_{h}-s))}
, and stopped at time
h(\sigma_{h}-t)-m_{h}(\sigma_{h}-t,\sigma_{h}-s)
and
h(\sigma_{h}-s)-m_{h}(\sigma_{h}-t,\sigma_{h}-s)
respectively.

The first identity in distribution in the last point is a consequence of the Markov property (
3.2
) under
\mathcal{N}_{x,r}
while the second follows by definition of the measures
(\bar{R}_{a,b}(\overline{{\mathrm{w}}},{\mathrm{d}}\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}^{\prime}):\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}},0\leq a\leq\zeta_{\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}\text{ and }b\geq a)
. A similar inspection to the right-hand side of (
5.2
) gives that this is precisely the law of
(\overline{W}_{s},\overline{W}_{t})
under
\textbf{Q}_{x,r}^{h_{\sigma_{h}\shortminus\hskip 0.56905pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}}
, and concludes the proof of (i).

Let us now turn our attention to (ii). Recall that we are working under

(

H
0

′

)

and that
(h,\overline{\Pi})
satisfies the regularity conditions (i) and (ii) of Section
2.1.2
. In particular, the shifted function
h^{\prime}:=(h_{\sigma_{h}/3\,+t}:t\geq 0)
is still
\theta
-Hölder continuous and the pair
(h^{\prime},\overline{\Pi})
still satisfies conditions (i) and (ii) of Section
2.1.2
. For
\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}
, let us write
Q_{\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}^{h^{\prime}}
for the law of the snake driven by
h^{\prime}
with spatial motion
\overline{\Pi}
and recall that under our assumptions, the process
\overline{W}
under
Q_{\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}^{h^{\prime}}
possesses a continuous modification. Noting from (
5.1
) that conditionally on

, the distribution of
(\overline{W}_{\sigma_{h}/3+t}:t\geq 0)
is
{Q}^{h^{\prime}}_{\overline{W}_{\sigma_{h}/3}}
, we infer that under
\textbf{Q}_{x,r}^{h}
the process
\overline{W}
possesses a continuous modification on
(\sigma_{h}/3,\infty)
. To extend the modification to the non-negative real line we make use of (i). More precisely, by (i) we have the equality in distribution

\Big((\overline{W}_{s}:\sigma_{h}/3\leq s\leq\sigma_{h})\text{ under }\textbf{Q}^{h_{\sigma_{h}\shortminus\hskip 0.56905pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}}_{x,r}\Big)\overset{(d)}{=}\Big((\overline{W}_{\sigma_{h}-s}:\sigma_{h}/3\leq s\leq\sigma_{h})\text{ under }\textbf{Q}_{x,r}^{h}\Big),

and applying the previous argument to the process in the left hand side yields that
\overline{W}
under
\textbf{Q}_{x,r}^{h}
possesses as well a continuous modification in
[0,2\sigma_{h}/3)
, and therefore in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
∎

Now, we randomise the driving function
h
by setting:

\mathbf{N}_{x,r}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W}):=N({\mathrm{d}}\rho)\textbf{Q}^{H(\rho)}_{x,r}({\mathrm{d}}\overline{W}).

Since under our standing assumption

(

H
0

′

)

the process

H(\rho)
under
N({\mathrm{d}}\rho)
is a.e.
\theta
-Hölder continuous for some
\theta
satisfying
\theta q>1
, by Lemma
5.2
-(ii) the process
\overline{W}
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
possesses a continuous modification. Therefore, arguing as in Section
2.2.3
we infer that the measure
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
is well defined in the canonical space
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{W}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
and supported on
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{x,r}
. From now on, it will be implicitly assumed that
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
is a measure on
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{W}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
. The canonical process
(\rho,\overline{W})
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
can be interpreted as the
\psi
-Lévy snake with spatial motion distributed according to
\mathcal{N}_{x,r}
, where we recall that this description is informal since
\mathcal{N}_{x,r}
is an infinite measure. Since for any
r\geq 0
, the process
(\rho,W,\Lambda-r)
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
is distributed
\textbf{N}_{x,0}
, it is often only necessary to prove statements holding under
\textbf{N}_{x,r}
in the case
r=0
, this will be used frequently in the sequel. Note that
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
is a sigma-finite measure. For example, it gives finite mass to events of the form
\{\uptau(W_{\varepsilon})\wedge H(\rho_{\varepsilon})>\varepsilon\}
for
\varepsilon>0
, since by construction:

N\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\{H(\rho_{\varepsilon})>\varepsilon\}}\mathbf{Q}^{H(\rho)}_{x,r}(\uptau(W_{\varepsilon})>\varepsilon)\big)=N(H(\rho_{\varepsilon})>\varepsilon)\mathcal{N}_{x,r}(\uptau(\xi)>\varepsilon)<\infty.

Let us now establish some important properties of
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
. First, note that since under
N
the process
\eta
is a functional of
\rho
, we can consider the triplet
(\rho,\eta,\overline{W})
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
. The definition of
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
, paired with Lemma
5.2
-(i) and (
2.24
), allows us to recover the duality property (
2.34
) of the Lévy snake under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
.

Corollary 5.3
.

Under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
, the processes
((\rho_{s},\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s}):0\leq s\leq\sigma)
and
((\eta_{(\sigma-s)-},\rho_{(\sigma-s)-},\overline{W}_{\sigma-s}):0\leq s\leq\sigma)
have the same distribution.

It is not difficult to check from our definitions that
(\rho,\overline{W})
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
satisfies the following simple Markov property. For every
t>0
and conditionally on
\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
, the shifted process
(\rho_{t+s},\overline{W}_{t+s})_{s\geq 0}
is distributed according to
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{t},\overline{W}_{t}}^{{\dagger}}
. In the following proposition, we strengthen this result by proving that
(\rho,\overline{W})
satisfies the strong Markov property under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
T
be a
(\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{t+})
-stopping time such that
T>0
,
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
-a.e. For every non-negative
\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{T+}
-measurable function
\Phi
and non-negative measurable function
F
on
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
, we have

\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\{T<\infty\}}\Phi\cdot F\big((\rho_{T+s},\overline{W}_{T+s}:s\geq 0)\big)\big)=\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\{T<\infty\}}\Phi\cdot\mathbb{E}^{{\dagger}}_{\rho_{T},\overline{W}_{T}}[F]\big).

(5.3)

Proof.

Our arguments are taken from Section 4.1.3 of
[
17
]
. First, notice that
it suffices to prove the result for an arbitrary bounded stopping time
T
taking values in
(0,\infty)
, that we fix from now on. For
t\geq 0
, we let
\lceil t\rceil
be the smallest integer satisfying
t<\lceil t\rceil
and for every
n\geq 1
, we set
T^{(n)}:=\lceil Tn\rceil/n
. We write
d_{\text{P}}
for the Prokhorov metric on
\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. Next, consider
f:\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
a bounded non-negative Lipschitz-continuous function with respect to the product metric
d_{\text{P}}\wedge 1+d_{\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}}\wedge 1
as well as a bounded non-negative
\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{T+}
-measurable function
\Phi
, non-null on a set with finite
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
measure. If we let
(\texttt{P}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be the transition
semigroup of the
(\psi,\overline{\Pi})
-Lévy snake, the statement of the proposition reduces to show that

\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\big(\Phi\cdot f(\rho_{T+t},\overline{W}_{T+t})\big)=\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\big(\Phi\cdot\texttt{P}_{t}f(\rho_{T},\overline{W}_{T})\big).

In this direction, for every
n\geq 1
, by the simple Markov property we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}_{x}(\Phi\cdot f(\rho_{T^{(n)}+t},\overline{W}_{T^{(n)}+t}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbf{N}_{x,r}(\Phi\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\{T^{(n)}=\frac{k}{n}\}}f(\rho_{T^{(n)}+t},\overline{W}_{T^{(n)}+t}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\big(\Phi\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\{T^{(n)}=\frac{k}{n}\}}\texttt{P}_{t}f(\rho_{\frac{k}{n}},\overline{W}_{\frac{k}{n}})\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\big(\Phi\cdot\texttt{P}_{t}f(\rho_{T^{(n)}},\overline{W}_{T^{(n)}})\big).

(5.4)

Since
(\rho,\overline{W})
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
is right-continuous, it holds that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}f(\rho_{T^{(n)}+t},\overline{W}_{T^{(n)}+t})=f(\rho_{T+t},\overline{W}_{T+t})
, and to conclude it suffices to prove
\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}|\texttt{P}_{t}f(\rho_{T},\overline{W}_{T})-\texttt{P}_{t}f(\rho_{T^{(n)}},\overline{W}_{T^{(n)}})|=0
,
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
-a.e. To this end, for every
\varepsilon>0
and
(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}
, set

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}):=

\displaystyle\Big\{(\mu^{\prime},\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}^{\prime})\in\overline{\Theta}:\,\exists\,\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{1}\in[0,\varepsilon)\text{ such that }\kappa_{\varepsilon_{0}}\mu=\kappa_{\varepsilon_{1}}\mu^{\prime},
