\displaystyle\quad\text{ and }\big(\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}(h):0\leq h\leq H(\kappa_{\varepsilon_{0}}\mu)\big)=\big(\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}^{\prime}(h):0\leq h\leq H(\kappa_{\varepsilon_{1}}\mu^{\prime})\big)\Big\},

(5.5)

where we recall that
\kappa_{a}
for
a\geq 1
is the pruning operation defined in (
2.18
). We introduce this set since for every
\varepsilon>0
, it is plain that
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
-a.e., the pair
(\rho_{T+s},\overline{W}_{T+s})
belongs to
\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}(\rho_{T},\overline{W}_{T})
for
s
small enough. Therefore,
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
-a.e.,

\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\big|\texttt{P}_{t}f(\rho_{T},\overline{W}_{T})-\texttt{P}_{t}f(\rho_{T^{(n)}},\overline{W}_{T^{(n)}})\big|\leq\sup_{(\mu^{\prime},\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}(\rho_{T},\overline{W}_{T})}\big|\texttt{P}_{t}f(\rho_{T},\overline{W}_{T})-\texttt{P}_{t}f(\mu^{\prime},\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}^{\prime})\big|.

To conclude, we rely on results from
[
17
]
. Namely, it was established in the proof of
[
17
, Lemma 4.1.3]
by means of a coupling argument
15
15

15

Lemma 4.1.3 in
[
17
]
works on a more general space than
\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}
, namely the space of killed rcll paths. Despite this difference, the same coupling argument applies, leading to the conclusion in (
5.6
).
that for every
(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}
, we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\sup_{(\mu^{\prime},\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})}|\texttt{P}_{t}f(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})-\texttt{P}_{t}f(\mu^{\prime},\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}^{\prime})|=0.

(5.6)

This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

We conclude this section by introducing our candidate measure for the law of the excursions away from
x
.

Definition 5.5
.

We write
\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}
for the law in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
of
\mathrm{tr}(\rho,W)\text{ under }\mathbf{N}_{x,r}.

Observe that this definition of
\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}
does not depend on
r
. Therefore, the measure
\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}
is well-defined, and we refer to it as the excursion measure
16
16

16

The terminology might be slightly misleading, the measure
\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}
should not be confused with
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
.
away from
x
of the
\Pi
-Markov process indexed by the
\psi
-Lévy tree. Note that by construction, under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
, the pair
(\rho,\overline{W})
belongs to
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{x}
, whereas under
\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}
we have
(\rho,W)\in\mathcal{S}_{x}
. In particular, in both cases we can consider the corresponding tree-indexed process. We stress that, while under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
the process
\rho
can be understood
17
17

17

Under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
, the conditional distribution of
W
given
\rho
is an infinite measure, and consequently this statement must be handled with care
as the exploration process of a Lévy tree (and the corresponding tree
\mathcal{T}_{H(\rho)}
as a Lévy tree), this is no longer the case under
\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}
. This makes the measure
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
much simpler to work with, as one can naturally extend classical results on Lévy snakes under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
by making use of the machinery developed in
[
17
,
37
]
. In order to study
\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}
, it is often simpler to start working under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
, since one can then extend the analysis to
\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}
through the identity
\mathbf{N}^{*}_{x}=\mathbf{N}_{x,r}(\text{tr}(\rho,W)\in\,\cdot\,)
.

6
Spinal decompositions

On the forthcoming sections, the study of spatial properties of the Lévy snake and its excursions away from
x
rely in a precise description of the law of
(\rho,\eta,\overline{W})
at typical times taken under different measures. In order to state our results we first need to introduce some notations. In this section we still work with an arbitrary fixed
r\geq 0
.

We start by introducing a pointed version of
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}
. Namely, let
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
be the measure on
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
defined by
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}:=\mathbb{N}_{x,r}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W}){\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{s\leq\sigma\}}
. Recall that under
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}
the process
\eta
is a functional of
\rho
, so that
\eta
is well defined under

\mathbb{N}^{\bullet}_{x,r}
. If we write
U:\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
for the identity function
U(s)=s
for
s\geq 0
, the law of
(\rho,\eta,\overline{W},U)
under
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
is then characterised by the relation

\mathbb{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}\big(\Phi(\rho,\eta,\overline{W},U)\big)=\mathbb{N}_{x,r}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\Phi(\rho,\eta,\overline{W},s)\Big).

Roughly speaking
U
is a point taken, conditionally on
(\rho,\eta,\overline{W})
, uniformly at random with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
[0,\sigma]
. Our goal now is to describe the law of
(\rho_{U},\eta_{U},\overline{W}_{U})
under
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
. To this end, in an auxiliary probability space
(\Omega_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0},P^{0})
, we consider a
2
-dimensional subordinator
(U^{(1)},U^{(2)})
with Laplace exponent given by:

-\log E^{0}\Big[\exp\big(-\lambda_{1}U^{(1)}_{1}-\lambda_{2}U^{(2)}_{1}\big)\Big]:=\begin{cases}\frac{\psi(\lambda_{1})-\psi(\lambda_{2})}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}-\alpha\quad\quad\text{if }\lambda_{1}\neq\lambda_{2},\\
\psi^{\prime}(\lambda_{1})-\alpha\quad\quad\hskip 22.76219pt\text{if }\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}.\end{cases}

(6.1)

In particular, we have the identity in law
(U^{(1)},U^{(2)})\overset{(d)}{=}(U^{(2)},U^{(1)})
and note that both subordinators
U^{(1)}
and
U^{(2)}
have Laplace exponent
\psi(\lambda)/\lambda-\alpha
, for
\lambda\geq 0
. Still under
P^{0}
and for
a\in(0,\infty]
, we write
(\mathcal{J}_{a},\widecheck{\mathcal{J}}_{a})
for the measure in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
defined by the relation

(\mathcal{J}_{a},\widecheck{\mathcal{J}}_{a}):=\big(\mathbbm{1}_{[0,a]}(t)\penalty\ {\mathrm{d}}U^{(1)}_{t},\mathbbm{1}_{[0,a]}(t)\penalty\ {\mathrm{d}}U^{(2)}_{t}\big)

with the usual convention
[0,\infty]:=[0,\infty)
. Our interest behind these measures comes from the fact that, by formula (18) in
[
17
]
, we have:

N\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\Phi\big(\rho_{s},\eta_{s}\big)\Big)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}a\,\exp\big(-\alpha a\big)\cdot E^{0}\big(\Phi(\mathcal{J}_{a},\widecheck{\mathcal{J}}_{a})\big),

(6.2)

where
\Phi
is a non-negative measurable function on
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}^{2}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+}))
, and where we recall from Section
2.2
that
N
is the excursion measure of the underlying reflected Lévy process. The previous formula can be easily enriched with the process
\overline{W}
and takes the form:

\mathbb{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}\big(\Phi\big(\rho_{U},\eta_{U},\overline{W}_{U}\big)\big)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}a\,\exp\big(-\alpha a\big)\cdot E^{0}\otimes\Pi_{x,r}\Big(\Phi\big(\mathcal{J}_{a},\widecheck{\mathcal{J}}_{a},(\overline{\xi}_{t}:t\leq a)\big)\Big),

(6.3)

we refer to
[
37
, Lemma 2.4]
for a proof.

We will now apply the same treatment to the Lévy snake under pointed versions of the measures
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
. In this direction, we introduce the pointed measure

\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}_{x,r}:=\mathbf{N}_{x,r}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W}){\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{s\leq\sigma\}},

(6.4)

and recall the definition of the measure

\mathcal{N}_{x,r}
given in Section
3
.

Lemma 6.1
.

For every non-negative measurable function
\Phi
on
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}
we have:

\mathbf{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}\big(\Phi\big(\rho_{U},\eta_{U},\overline{W}_{U}\big)\big)=E^{0}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{x,r}\Big(\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}a\,\exp(-\alpha a)\cdot\Phi\big(\mathcal{J}_{a},\widecheck{\mathcal{J}}_{a},(\overline{\xi}_{t}:t\leq a)\big)\Big).

Proof.

Fix a non-negative measurable function

\Phi
on
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}
.
By definition of
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
, for every fixed
s\geq 0
, under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
and conditionally on
(\rho,\eta)
, the variable
\overline{W}_{s}
is distributed as
(\xi_{t},\mathcal{L}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
under
\mathcal{N}_{x,r}
restricted to
[0,H(\rho_{s})]
.
Hence,

\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{\{s\leq\sigma\}}\Phi\big(\rho_{s},\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s}\big)\Big)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}s\,N\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{\{s\leq\sigma\}}\mathcal{N}_{x,r}\Big[\Phi\big(\rho_{s},\eta_{s},\big(\overline{\xi}_{t}:t\leq H(\rho_{s})\big)\big)\Big]\Big),

where now
\mathcal{N}_{x,r}\big[\Phi\big(\rho_{s},\eta_{s},(\overline{\xi}_{t}:t\leq H(\rho_{s}))\big)\big]
is a function of
(\rho_{s},\eta_{s})
. An application of Tonelli’s theorem combined with (
6.2
) now entails the statement of the lemma, noting that for
a>0
, we have
H(J_{a})=a
. This identity immediately follows from the fact the Lévy measure of
\psi(\lambda)/\lambda-\alpha
is the measure in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
given by
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(\ell){\mathrm{d}}\ell\,\pi([\ell,\infty))
, and therefore it has infinite mass. We refer to
[
17
, Section 1.1.2]
for details.
∎

The formulations in (
6.3
) and Lemma
6.1
are not strong enough for our purposes. Namely, it will be crucial for the sequel to also keep track of the distribution of the pieces of trajectory before and after time
U
, in both (
6.3
) and Lemma
6.1
. To this end, for each
(\uprho,\overline{\omega})\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
, we introduce for every
s\geq 0
, the notation

\big(\uprho,\overline{\omega}\big)^{s,\leftarrow}:=(\uprho_{(0\vee(s-t))-},\overline{\omega}_{(0\vee(s-t))-})_{t\geq 0}\quad\text{ and }\quad\big(\uprho,\overline{\omega}\big)^{s,\rightarrow}:=(\uprho_{s+t},\overline{\omega}_{s+t})_{t\geq 0},

for the pieces of path coming before and after time
s
.
As usual, we use the convention
(\uprho_{0-},\overline{\omega}_{0-}):=(\uprho_{0},\overline{\omega}_{0})
. For
s\geq 0
, we shall refer to the pair

\big(\big(\uprho,\overline{\omega}\big)^{s,\leftarrow},\big(\uprho,\overline{\omega}\big)^{s,\rightarrow}\big)

(6.5)

as the spinal decomposition of
(\uprho,\overline{\omega})
at time
s
.
Observe that for every fixed
s\geq 0
, the process
(\uprho,\overline{\omega})
can be easily recovered from (
6.5
) by time reversing
\big(\uprho,\overline{\omega}\big)^{s,\leftarrow}

and concatenating the resulting piece of path with
\big(\uprho,\overline{\omega}\big)^{s,\rightarrow}
.

Our goal now is to characterise the law of
(\rho,\overline{W})^{U,\leftarrow}
and
(\rho,\overline{W})^{U,\rightarrow}
under the measures
\mathbb{N}^{\bullet}_{x,r}
and
\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}_{x,r}
. In this direction, recall that
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{\mathrm{w}}}
stands for the law of
(\rho,\overline{W})
started from
(\mu,\overline{\mathrm{w}})
. Characterising the distribution of
(\rho,\overline{W})^{U,\rightarrow}
in terms of the measures
(\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{\mathrm{w}}},(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x})
can be easily done by an application of the Markov property, but the one of
(\rho,\overline{W})^{U,\leftarrow}
is more delicate. Indeed, the latter relies on the duality property (
2.34
), and
in a description of the law of
(\rho,\eta,\overline{W})
started from an arbitrary element
(\mu,\nu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})
of
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}
. Since the conditional distribution of
\overline{W}
given
(\rho,\eta)
only depends on
H(\rho)
, we only need to introduce the law of the pair
(\rho,\eta)
started from
(\mu,\nu)
- we recall that by
[
17
, Proposition 3.1.2.]
the pair
(\rho,\eta)
is a Markov process. To do so, we first extend the pruning and concatenation operations, introduced in Section

2.2.2
for elements of
\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, to arbitrary elements
(\mu,\nu)
in
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. Our presentation follows
[
17
, Section 3.1]
. First, for every
a\geq 0
, we write:

\kappa_{a}(\mu,\nu):=(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\nu}),

for
\tilde{\mu}:=\kappa_{a}\mu
, and where the measure
\tilde{\nu}
is the unique element of
\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that
(\mu+\nu)|_{[0,H(\tilde{\mu})]}=\tilde{\mu}+\tilde{\nu}
. Notice that
\tilde{\nu}
and
\nu|_{[0,H(\tilde{\mu})]}
may only differ at the point
H(\tilde{\mu})
. Next, we define the concatenation of two elements
(\mu_{1},\nu_{1}),(\mu_{2},\nu_{2})\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, with
H(\mu_{1})<\infty
, by setting:

[(\mu_{1},\nu_{1}),(\mu_{2},\nu_{2})]:=\big([\mu_{1},\mu_{2}],\theta\big)

where
\theta
is the element of
\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
defined by
\langle\theta,f\rangle:=\int\nu_{1}(\mathrm{d}s)\mathbbm{1}_{[0,H(\mu_{1})]}(s)f(s)+\int\nu_{2}(\mathrm{d}s)f(H(\mu_{1})+s)
, for any non-negative measurable function
f
in
\mathbb{R_{+}}
. Note that in general
\theta
does not coincide with
[\mu_{2},\nu_{2}]
.
Then, for every
(\mu,\nu)\in\mathcal{M}_{f}^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, we let
\mathbf{P}_{\mu,\nu}
be the distribution under
\mathbf{P}_{0}
in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+}))
of the process

\big[\kappa_{-I_{t}}(\mu,\nu),(\rho_{t},\eta_{t})\big],\quad\text{ for }{t\geq 0}.

(6.6)

The probability measure
\mathbf{P}_{\mu,\nu}
is the law of the strong Markov process
(\rho,\eta)
started from
(\mu,\nu)
, we refer to
[
17
, Section 3.1]
for a detailed account. Since under
\mathbf{P}_{0}
the processes
\eta
and
(I_{t})_{t\geq 0}
are functionals of
\rho
, it follows that
\eta
is also a functional of
\rho
under
\mathbf{P}_{\mu,\nu}
. For
(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}
and
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, we define a probability measure in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}^{2}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
by setting

\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\nu,\overline{\text{w}}}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\eta,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W}):=\textbf{P}_{\mu,\nu}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\eta)\>Q^{H(\rho)}_{\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}({\mathrm{d}}\overline{W}).

(6.7)

We write
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}_{\mu,\nu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}
for the law of
(\rho,\eta,\overline{W})
under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\nu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}
stopped at time
\inf\{t\geq 0:\penalty\ \langle\rho_{t},1\rangle=0\}
.

Proposition 6.2
.

Under
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
, conditionally on
(\rho_{U},\eta_{U},\overline{W}_{U})
, the
processes
(\rho,\overline{W})^{U,\leftarrow}
and
(\rho,\overline{W})^{U,\rightarrow}
are independent and their
respective conditional laws are as follows:

•

the process
(\rho,\overline{W})^{U,\leftarrow}
is distributed as
(\eta,\overline{W})
under
\mathbb{P}_{\eta_{U},\rho_{U},\overline{W}_{U}}^{\dagger}
;

•

the process
(\rho,\overline{W})^{U,\rightarrow}
is distributed as
(\rho,\overline{W})
under
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{U},\overline{W}_{U}}^{\dagger}
.

Observe that (
6.3
) and Lemma
6.1
paired with Proposition
6.2
completely characterise the law of the spinal decomposition at time
U
of
(\rho,\overline{W})
under
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
.

Proof.

The proof is a straightforward consequence of the Markov property and the duality (
2.34
). We start proving the result under
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
. We will then briefly explain how to extend the proof to the measure
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
. Let
\Phi_{1}
be a non-negative measurable function on
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}
and
\Phi_{2},\Phi_{3}
two non-negative measurable functions on
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
. The statement of the proposition is equivalent to proving that

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}\Big(\Phi_{1}\big(\rho_{U},\eta_{U},\overline{W}_{U}\big)\Phi_{2}((\rho,\overline{W})^{U,\leftarrow})\Phi_{3}((\rho,\overline{W})^{U,\rightarrow})\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}\Big(\Phi_{1}\big(\rho_{U},\eta_{U},\overline{W}_{U}\big)\mathbb{E}_{\eta_{U},\rho_{U},\overline{W}_{U}}^{\dagger}\big[\Phi_{2}(\eta,\overline{W})\big]\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{U},\overline{W}_{U}}^{\dagger}\big[\Phi_{3}(\rho,\overline{W})\big]\Big).

(6.8)

In this direction, notice that the left hand side in the last display is given by

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}s\penalty\ \mathbb{N}_{x,r}\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{\{s\leq\sigma\}}\,\Phi_{1}\big(\rho_{s},\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s}\big)\Phi_{2}((\rho,\overline{W})^{s,\leftarrow})\Phi_{3}((\rho,\overline{W})^{s,\rightarrow})\Big).

The simple Markov property under
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}
at time
s
followed by the change of variable
s\mapsfrom\sigma-s
gives that the previous display writes

\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}s\penalty\ \mathbb{N}_{x,r}\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{\{s\leq\sigma\}}\,\Phi_{1}\big(\rho_{\sigma-s},\eta_{\sigma-s},\overline{W}_{\sigma-s}\big)\Phi_{2}((\rho,\overline{W})^{\sigma-s,\leftarrow})\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\sigma-s},\overline{W}_{\sigma-s}}^{\dagger}\big[\Phi_{3}(\rho,\overline{W})\big]\Big).

By an application of the duality identity (
2.34
) the last display equals

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}s\penalty\ \mathbb{N}_{x,r}\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{\{s\leq\sigma\}}\,\Phi_{1}\big(\eta_{s},\rho_{s},\overline{W}_{s}\big)\Phi_{2}\big((\eta_{s+t},\overline{W}_{s+t}:\penalty\ t\geq 0)\big)\mathbb{E}_{\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s}}^{\dagger}\big[\Phi_{3}(\rho,\overline{W})\big]\Big),

and we can now use again the simple Markov property to get by analogous arguments that the previous expression is given by

\mathbb{N}_{x,r}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}\mathrm{d}s\penalty\ \Phi_{1}\big(\eta_{s},\rho_{s},\overline{W}_{s}\big)\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{s},\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s}}^{\dagger}\big[\Phi_{2}\big(\eta,\overline{W}\big)\big]\mathbb{E}_{\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s}}^{\dagger}\big[\Phi_{3}(\rho,\overline{W})\big]\Big).

When applying (
2.34
) we used the fact that, since
(\rho,\eta)
under
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}
is a rcll process, the set of its jump-times has null Lebesgue measure
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}
-a.e. Finally, the change of variable
s\mapsfrom\sigma-s
and the duality property applied as before yields (
6.8
), which proves the desired result under
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
. The same arguments apply under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
, making use of Corollary
5.3
and the Markov property (
5.3
) instead. We skip the details.
∎

7
The excursion theory

In this section we present the main results of the excursion theory. It is divided in tree sections. We start by recalling in Section
7.1
some properties of the local time at
x
of
(\widehat{W}_{t}:t\geq 0)
that will be used frequently in the upcoming sections. Section
7.2
is devoted to the proofs of the exit formulas , and finally, in Section
7.3
we prove that the excursion process of the Lévy snake is a Poisson point measure.

7.1
The local time at
x

In this section, we recall some important properties of an additive functional
(A_{t})_{t\geq 0}
of the Lévy snake called the local time at
x
of
(\widehat{W}_{t}:\penalty\ t\geq 0)
. This process was recently introduced in
[
37
]
and plays a role analogous to that of the classical local time for
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-indexed processes. Roughly speaking, at each time
t\geq 0
, the variable
A_{t}
measures how much time
\widehat{W}
has spent at
x
up to time
t
. Let us stress that, although closely related (see (
7.3
) below), the processes
(A_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\Lambda_{t})_{t\geq 0}
are drastically different. Recall that vaguely, for each fixed
t\geq 0
, the path
\Lambda_{t}
codes the local time at
x
of the Markov process
\Pi
along the ancestral path of
p_{H(\rho)}(t)
in the tree
\mathcal{T}_{H(\rho)}
.

For each
z\geq 0
and
\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}
, we set:

\tau_{z}(\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}):=\inf\{h\geq 0:\penalty\ \overline{{\mathrm{w}}}(h)=(x,z)\},

with the usual convention
\inf\emptyset=\infty
. Under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
and
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, the continuous, non-decreasing process
A=(A_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
is defined by the relation

A_{t}=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}u\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathrm{d}}z\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{z}(\overline{W}_{u})<H(\rho_{u})<\tau_{z}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon\}},\quad t\geq 0,

(7.1)

the convergence holding uniformly on compact intervals in measure under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
and
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(\,\cdot\,\cap\{\sigma>\delta\})
for every
\delta>0
. As a consequence, we can find a decreasing sequence

(\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime})_{k\geq 0}
of positive real numbers converging to
0
along which the convergence (
7.1
) holds uniformly in compact intervals under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
and
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
outside of a
negligible set. It will be convenient to define
A_{t}(\uprho,\overline{\omega})
for every
(\uprho,\overline{\omega})\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
at
t\geq 0
by setting

A_{t}(\uprho,\overline{\omega})=\liminf_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}u\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathrm{d}}z\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{z}(\overline{\omega}_{u})<H(\uprho_{u})<\tau_{z}(\overline{\omega}_{u})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}.

(7.2)

This definition is consistent with (
7.1
) under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
and
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
up to an negligible set. We shall now argue that one can make use of this process to index the excursions away from
x
, and that this indexing is compatible with the order induced by time
t\geq 0
. To this end, we recall that Theorem 4.19 in
[
37
]
states that the support of the Stieltjes measure
{\mathrm{d}}A
, denoted by
\text{supp }{\mathrm{d}}A
, satisfies:

\text{supp }{\mathrm{d}}A=[0,\sigma]\setminus\mathcal{C}^{*}\subset\{t\in[0,\sigma]:\widehat{W}_{t}=x\},\quad\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}\text{ and }\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\text{ a.e.}\penalty\ ,

(7.3)

where we write
\mathcal{C}^{*}
for the subset of all times
t\geq 0
at
which
\widehat{\Lambda}
is constant on some open neighborhood of
t
.
The following decomposition in excursions of the functional
A
will be used frequently in our computations. Under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, let
(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be the subtrajectories associated to the connected components of
\{t\geq 0:H(\rho_{t})>0\}
. Then,

A_{\sigma}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}A_{\sigma_{i}}(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i}),\quad\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}\text{-a.s.}

(7.4)

We refer to the end of Section 4.2 in
[
37
]
for other related results.
Recalling that by Lemma
4.5
, the variables
\widehat{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{g}(u)}
,
u\in\mathcal{D}
under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
and
\mathbb{\mathbb{P}}_{0,x,0}
are a.e. distinct, it follows
from (
7.3
) and the continuity of the map
t\mapsto\widehat{\Lambda}_{t}
that:

Corollary 7.1
.

Under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
and
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, all the values
A_{\mathfrak{g}(u)}
,
u\in\mathcal{D}
, are distinct.

It might be worth mentioning that, in stark contrast with
(A_{\mathfrak{g}(u)},u\in\mathcal{D})
, the ordering induced by
(\widehat{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{g}(u)}:u\in\mathcal{D})
in
\mathcal{D}
is not compatible with the order induced by time since
(\widehat{\Lambda}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is not monotone.

We conclude this short section establishing a spinal decomposition in the same vein as in Section
6
but at a point sampled according to the measure
{\mathrm{d}}A
. This description will be used when establishing
the relationship between the
(\psi,\overline{\Pi})
-Lévy snake under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0},\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, and the measures
(\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
,
r\geq 0)
in Section
7.2
. To this end,
we introduce the pointed measure

\mathbb{N}^{\bullet,A}_{x,r}:=\mathbb{N}_{x,r}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W}){\mathrm{d}}A_{s}

in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
With a slight abuse of notation, we extend the definition of
\tau_{z}
,
z\geq 0
, under
\Pi_{x,0}
by setting
\tau_{z}(\overline{\xi}):=\inf\{h\geq 0:\penalty\ \overline{\xi}_{h}=(x,z)\}
, which coincides with the first time at which the local time
\mathcal{L}
takes the value
z
. When there is no risk of confusion we will drop the dependence on
\overline{\xi}
, and to simplify notation we write
\bar{\xi}^{\tau_{z}}:=\big(\bar{\xi}_{t}:\penalty\ t\in[0,\tau_{z}]\big)
.

Proposition 7.2
.

Fix
r\geq 0
. For every non-negative measurable function
\Phi
on
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet,A}\big(\Phi\big(\rho_{U},\eta_{U},\overline{W}_{U}\big)\big)=E^{0}\otimes\Pi_{x,r}\Big(\int_{r}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}a\,\exp\big(-\alpha\tau_{a}\big)\Phi(\mathcal{J}_{\tau_{a}},\widecheck{\mathcal{J}}_{\tau_{a}},\bar{\xi}^{\tau_{a}})\Big).

(7.5)

Furthermore, under
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet,A}
, conditionally on
(\rho_{U},\eta_{U},\overline{W}_{U})
, the
processes
(\rho,W)^{U,\leftarrow}
and
(\rho,W)^{U,\rightarrow}
are independent and their conditional laws are as follows:

•

the process
(\rho,W)^{U,\leftarrow}
is distributed as
(\eta,\overline{W})
under
\mathbb{P}_{\eta_{U},\rho_{U},\overline{W}_{U}}^{\dagger}
;

•

the process
(\rho,W)^{U,\rightarrow}
is distributed as
(\rho,\overline{W})
under
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{U},\overline{W}_{U}}^{\dagger}
.

In particular, the only difference with Proposition
6.2
lies in the distribution of
(\rho_{U},\eta_{U},\overline{W}_{U})
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one of Proposition
6.2
so we will be brief. First, by
[
37
, Lemma 4.11]
, we already know that:

\mathbb{N}_{x,r}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}A_{s}\,\Phi\big(\rho_{s},\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s}\big)\Big)=E^{0}\otimes\Pi_{x,r}\Big(\int_{r}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}a\,\exp\big(-\alpha\tau_{a}\big)\Phi\big(\mathcal{J}_{\tau_{a}},\widecheck{\mathcal{J}}_{\tau_{a}},\bar{\xi}^{\tau_{a}}\big)\Big).

(7.6)

Furthermore, if
\Phi_{2},\Phi_{3}
are non-negative measurable functions on
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
, by strong Markov property we get that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}_{x,r}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}A_{s}\,\Phi\big(\rho_{s},\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s}\big)\Phi_{2}((\rho,\overline{W})^{s,\leftarrow})\Phi_{3}((\rho,\overline{W})^{s,\rightarrow})\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{N}_{x,r}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}A_{s}\,\Phi\big(\rho_{s},\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s}\big)\Phi_{2}((\rho,\overline{W})^{s,\leftarrow})\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{s},\overline{W}_{s}}^{\dagger}\big[\Phi_{3}(\rho,\overline{W})\big]\Big).

(7.7)

We can now use the duality identity (
2.34
) paired with (
7.1
) to infer that

\Big(\big(\rho_{t},\eta_{t},\overline{W}_{t},A_{t}\big):t\in[0,\sigma]\Big)\overset{(d)}{=}\Big(\big(\eta_{(\sigma-t)-},\rho_{(\sigma-t)-},\overline{W}_{\sigma-t},A_{\sigma}-A_{\sigma-t}\big):t\in[0,\sigma]\Big),\quad\text{ under }\mathbb{N}_{x,r}.

Therefore, as in the proof of Proposition
6.2
, by performing the change of variable
s\mapsfrom\sigma-s
and applying again the strong Markov property, we obtain that (
7.7
) equals:

\mathbb{N}_{x,r}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}A_{s}\,\Phi\big(\rho_{s},\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s}\big)\mathbb{E}_{\eta_{s},\rho_{s},\overline{W}_{s}}^{\dagger}\big[\Phi_{2}(\eta,\overline{W})\big]\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{s},\overline{W}_{s}}^{\dagger}\big[\Phi_{3}(\rho,\overline{W})\big]\Big).

The statement of the proposition follows by combining the previous display with (
7.6
).
∎

7.2
The exit formula

In this section we establish the connection between the family of measures
(\mathbf{N}_{x,r})_{r\geq 0}
introduced in Section
5
and the
(\psi,\overline{\Pi})
-Lévy snake, through the
exit formula
stated in Theorem
7.3
. Let us first introduce some notation. Under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
and
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, for each
u\in\mathcal{D}
we write
(\mathrm{T}^{u}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
for the right-inverse of the functional

\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{s\,\in\,(\mathfrak{g}(u),\mathfrak{d}(u))},\quad t\geq 0.

(7.8)

It is plain that the time-changed process
\texttt{T}_{u}(\rho,\overline{W}):=(\rho_{\mathrm{T}_{t}^{u}},W_{\mathrm{T}_{t}^{u}})_{t\geq 0}
is an element of
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{x}
which roughly speaking, is obtained from gluing the two pieces of path
((\rho_{t},\overline{W}_{t}):0\leq t<\mathfrak{g}(u))
and
((\rho_{t},\overline{W}_{t}):\mathfrak{d}(u)\leq t)
so that the subtrajectory
(\rho^{u},\overline{W}^{u})
has been “trimmed” from
(\rho,\overline{W})
. Recall that since the image under
p_{H(\rho)}
of
[\mathfrak{g}(u),\mathfrak{d}(u)]
is the subtree stemming from
u
, in terms of labelled trees, the subtrajectory
(\rho^{u},\overline{W}^{u})
can be thought of as coding the labels on
\{v\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\rho)}:u\preceq v\}
and therefore
\texttt{T}_{u}(\rho,\overline{W})
as coding the labels on its complement
\mathcal{T}_{H(\rho)}\setminus\{v\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\rho)}:u\preceq v\}
.

Theorem 7.3
.

For every non-negative measurable functions
F,G
on
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})\times\mathbb{R}
and

\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
, we have

\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(\sum_{u\in\mathcal{D}}F\big(\,\emph{\texttt{T}}_{u}(\rho,\overline{W}),\mathfrak{g}(u)\big)\cdot G(\rho^{u},\overline{W}^{u})\Big)=\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}\mathrm{d}A_{s}\penalty\ F(\rho,\overline{W},s)\cdot\mathbf{N}_{x,\widehat{\Lambda}_{s}}\big(G(\rho,\overline{W})\big)\Big).

(7.9)

This formula shares strong similarities with the so-called exit formula of Maisonneuve
[
33
, Theorem 4.1]
and, with a slight abuse of terminology, we shall refer to it in this way. Most of this section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
7.3
. We will then derive a version of this result holding under the probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
that will be needed in Section
7.3
. It will be used at multiple instances in our arguments the fact that by

(

H
2

)

and (
6.3
), it holds that

\text{Leb}\big(\{s\geq 0:\widehat{W}_{s}=x\}\big)=0,\quad\mathbb{N}_{x,0}-\text{a.e.}

(7.10)

Proof.

The proof can be divided in two main steps. We first start by rewriting the left and right-hand side of (
7.9
) in terms of three spinal decompositions. We will then check that the two sides coincide by making use of (
6.3
), and of Propositions
6.2
and
7.2
. Let us start with the former task. In this direction, remark that by a simple continuity argument, under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
and for every
u\in\mathcal{D}
, we have
0<\sigma(\mathrm{tr}(W^{u}))<\infty
a.e., and that
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,0}
, we have
0<\sigma(\mathrm{tr}(W))<\infty
a.e.
Therefore, by replacing the functions
F(\uprho,\overline{\omega}),G(\uprho,\overline{\omega})
by
F(\uprho,\overline{\omega})/\sigma(\mathrm{tr}(\omega))
and
G(\uprho,\overline{\omega})/\sigma(\mathrm{tr}(\omega))
respectively, we get that to obtain (
7.9
) it suffices to prove

\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(\sum_{u\in\mathcal{D}}F\big(\,\texttt{T}_{u}(\rho,\overline{W}),\mathfrak{g}(u)\big)\sigma(\mathrm{tr}(W^{u}))G\big(\rho^{u},\overline{W}^{u}\big)\Big)=\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}\mathrm{d}A_{s}\penalty\ F\big(\rho,\overline{W},s\big)\cdot\mathbf{N}_{x,\widehat{\Lambda}_{s}}\big(\sigma(\mathrm{tr}(W))G\big(\rho,\overline{W})\big)\Big).

(7.11)

Let us now rewrite the right-hand side of (
7.11
). First, recalling the definition of
\uptau
given in (
4.1
) and the form of the time change
\Gamma
appearing in the definition of
\text{tr}(\uprho,\omega)
in (
4.4
), the right-hand side of (
7.11
) can be written in the following form:

\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}\mathrm{d}A_{s}\penalty\ F\big(\rho,\overline{W},s\big)\cdot\mathbf{N}_{x,\widehat{\Lambda}_{s}}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma(W)}{\mathrm{d}}t\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{t})=\infty\}}G\big(\rho,\overline{W})\Big)\Big).

(7.12)

Next, under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
and
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
for
r\geq 0
, we introduce the notation:

\texttt{Sp}_{s}(\rho,\overline{W}):=\big((\rho,\overline{W})^{s,\leftarrow},(\rho,\overline{W})^{s,\rightarrow}\big)

for every
s\geq 0
. Observe that for any
s\in(0,\sigma(W))
, we can recover the process
(\rho,\overline{W})
by considering
(\rho,\overline{W})^{s,\leftarrow}
run backwards in time, shifted at the last time at which it takes the value
(0,x,0)\in\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}
, and by concatenating the resulting process with
(\rho,\overline{W})^{s,\rightarrow}
. This operation on the pair
\texttt{Sp}_{s}(\rho,\overline{W})
does not depend on
s
, and we denote it by
\mathfrak{R}
. With this notation in hand, we get that (
7.12
) equals:

\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}\mathrm{d}A_{s}\penalty\ F\big(\mathfrak{R}\circ\texttt{Sp}_{s}(\rho,\overline{W}),s\big)\cdot\mathbf{N}_{x,\widehat{\Lambda}_{s}}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma(W)}{\mathrm{d}}t\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{t})=\infty\}}G\big(\mathfrak{R}\circ\texttt{Sp}_{t}(\rho,\overline{W})\big)\Big)\Big).

(7.13)

Let us now apply a similar treatment to the left-hand side of (
7.11
). In this direction we notice that, under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, for each
u\in\mathcal{D}
the positive variable
\sigma(\mathrm{tr}(W^{u}))
is precisely the Lebesgue measure of
\{s\in[0,\sigma(W)]:\penalty\ p_{H(\rho)}(s)\in\mathcal{C}_{u}\}
. It now follows that the left-hand side of (
7.11
) writes

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(\sum_{u\in\mathcal{D}}\int_{0}^{\sigma(W)}{\mathrm{d}}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{p_{H}(s)\in\,\mathcal{C}_{u}\}}F\big(\,\texttt{T}_{u}(\rho,\overline{W}),\mathfrak{g}(u)\big)G(\rho^{u},\overline{W}^{u})\Big).

By Lemma
4.3
and (
7.10
), for Lebesgue almost every
s\in[0,\sigma(W)]
, there is a unique
u(s)\in\mathcal{D}
such that
p_{H(\rho)}(s)
belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{u(s)}
, and by convention we set
u(s)=0
when the latter fails. Recalling (
7.10
), we can then rewrite the previous display in the following form

\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma(W)}{\mathrm{d}}s\penalty\ F\big(\,\texttt{T}_{u(s)}(\rho,\overline{W}),\mathfrak{g}(u(s))\big)\cdot G(\rho^{u(s)},\overline{W}^{u(s)})\Big).

(7.14)

In the same vein as before, the idea now is to express
\mathrm{T}_{u(s)}(\rho,\overline{W})
,
\mathfrak{g}(u(s))
and
(\rho^{u(s)},\overline{W}^{u(s)})
in terms of
\texttt{Sp}_{s}
and
\mathfrak{R}
. To this end, setting
T^{\rightarrow}_{s}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\penalty\ H(\rho_{s+t})=\varsigma_{x}(W_{s})\}
with
\varsigma_{x}({\mathrm{w}})=\sup\{0\leq s\leq\zeta_{\mathrm{w}}:{\mathrm{w}}(s)=x\}
, we “cut”
\big(\rho,\overline{W}\big)^{\rightarrow,s}
in the two following processes:

\displaystyle\big(\rho,\overline{W}\big)^{\rightarrow,s,\prime}_{t}

\displaystyle:=\big(\rho_{s+T_{s}^{\rightarrow}+t},\overline{W}_{s+T_{s}^{\rightarrow}+t}\big),\quad t\geq 0,

\displaystyle\big(\rho,\overline{W}\big)^{\rightarrow,s,\prime\prime}_{t}

\displaystyle:=\uptheta_{\varsigma_{x}(W_{s})}\big(\rho_{(s+t)\wedge(s+T_{s}^{\rightarrow})},\overline{W}_{(s+t)\wedge(s+T_{s}^{\rightarrow})}\big),\quad t\geq 0,

where
\uptheta
is the operation defined in (
2.30
). In the last display and for the remainder of the proof, when
\uptheta
is applied to a tuple, we apply it coordinate-wise. We now proceed similarly with the process
\big(\rho,\overline{W}\big)^{\leftarrow,s}
. Namely, we set
T^{\leftarrow}_{s}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\penalty\ H(\rho_{0\vee(s-t)})=\varsigma_{x}(W_{s})\}
and we consider the following pair

\displaystyle\big(\rho,\overline{W}\big)^{\leftarrow,s,\prime}_{t}

\displaystyle:=\big(\rho_{{\,(0\vee}(s-T_{s}^{\leftarrow}-t))-},\overline{W}_{{(0\vee}(s-T_{s}^{\leftarrow}-t))-}\big),\quad t\geq 0,

\displaystyle\big(\rho,\overline{W}\big)^{\leftarrow,s,\prime\prime}_{t}

\displaystyle:=\uptheta_{\varsigma_{x}(W_{s})}\big(\rho_{((s-t)\vee(s-T_{s}^{\leftarrow}))-},\overline{W}_{((s-t)\vee(s-T_{s}^{\leftarrow}))-}\big),\quad t\geq 0.

To simplify notation, for every
s\geq 0
, we set

\texttt{Sp}^{\prime}_{s}(\rho,\overline{W}):=\big((\rho,\overline{W})^{s,\leftarrow,\prime},(\rho,\overline{W})^{s,\rightarrow,\prime}\big)\quad\text{ and }\quad\texttt{Sp}^{\prime\prime}_{s}(\rho,\overline{W}):=\big((\rho,\overline{W})^{s,\leftarrow,\prime\prime},(\rho,\overline{W})^{s,\rightarrow,\prime\prime}\big).

Let us also denote by
\Sigma_{s}^{\prime}
the first time at which
\big(\rho,\overline{W}\big)^{\leftarrow,s,\prime}
takes the value
(0,x,0)
. The key now is that by (
7.10
), under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, a.e. for Lebesgue-almost every
s\in[0,\sigma(W)]
we have

\mathfrak{R}\circ\texttt{Sp}^{\prime}_{s}(\rho,\overline{W})=\texttt{T}_{u(s)}(\rho,\overline{W}),\quad\quad\mathfrak{R}\circ\texttt{Sp}^{\prime\prime}_{s}(\rho,\overline{W})=(\rho^{u(s)},\overline{W}^{u(s)})

and
\Sigma_{s}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{g}(u(s))
. The formula in (
7.14
) can now be written in terms of these identities, and recalling that the right-hand side of (
7.11
) is given by (
7.13
), we deduce that the desired identity (
7.11
) can be re-written as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma(W)}{\mathrm{d}}s\penalty\ F\big(\mathfrak{R}\circ\texttt{Sp}^{\prime}_{s}(\rho,\overline{W}),\Sigma_{s}^{\prime}\big)\cdot G\big(\mathfrak{R}\circ\texttt{Sp}^{\prime\prime}_{s}(\rho,\overline{W})\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma(W)}\mathrm{d}A_{s}\penalty\ F\big(\mathfrak{R}\circ\texttt{Sp}_{s}(\rho,\overline{W}),s\big)\cdot\mathbf{N}_{x,\widehat{\Lambda}_{s}}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma(W)}{\mathrm{d}}t\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{t})=\infty\}}G\big(\mathfrak{R}\circ\texttt{Sp}_{t}(\rho,\overline{W})\big)\Big)\Big).

(7.15)

Observe that both
\Sigma_{s}^{\prime}
and
s
are just the hitting time of
(0,x,0)
of respectively the first element in
\texttt{Sp}^{\prime}_{s}(\rho,\overline{W})
and
\texttt{Sp}_{s}(\rho,\overline{W})
. Hence, to establish (
7.15
) it suffices to prove that for every non-negative measurable functions
\Phi,\Psi
on triples, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma(W)}{\mathrm{d}}s\penalty\ \Phi\big(\texttt{Sp}^{\prime}_{s}(\rho,\overline{W})\big)\cdot\Psi\big(\texttt{Sp}^{\prime\prime}_{s}(\rho,\overline{W})\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma(W)}\mathrm{d}A_{s}\penalty\ \Phi\big(\texttt{Sp}_{s}(\rho,\overline{W})\big)\cdot\mathbf{N}_{x,\widehat{\Lambda}_{s}}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma(W)}{\mathrm{d}}t\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{t})=\infty\}}\Psi\big(\texttt{Sp}_{t}(\rho,\overline{W})\big)\Big)\Big).

(7.16)

We will now simplify this identity by exploiting the simple nature of the conditional law of the spine at time
U
under the pointed measures
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}^{\bullet}
,
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}^{\bullet,A}
and
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
. First, under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
and for every
s\in[0,\sigma(W)]
, set

\displaystyle\big(\rho^{\prime}_{s},\eta^{\prime}_{s},\overline{W}_{s}^{\prime}\big):=\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{\{h\leq\varsigma_{x}(W_{s})\}}\rho_{s}(\mathrm{d}h),\mathbbm{1}_{\{h\leq\varsigma_{x}(W_{s})\}}\eta_{s}(\mathrm{d}h),\big(\overline{W}_{s}(t):\penalty\ t\in[0,\varsigma_{x}(W_{s})]\big)\Big),

\displaystyle\big(\rho^{\prime\prime}_{s},\eta^{\prime\prime}_{s},\overline{W}_{s}^{\prime\prime}\big):=\uptheta_{\varsigma_{x}(W_{s})}\big(\rho_{s},\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s}\big).

where again,
\uptheta
is applied coordinatewise. Recall from Proposition
6.2
that, under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}^{\bullet}
and conditionally on
(\rho_{U},\eta_{U},\overline{W}_{U})
, the processes
(\rho,\overline{W})^{\leftarrow,U}
and
(\rho,\overline{W})^{\rightarrow,U}
are distributed according to
\mathbb{P}_{\eta_{U},\rho_{U},\overline{W}_{U}}^{\dagger}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{U},\overline{W}_{U}}^{\dagger}
respectively. It readily follows from the previous construction, the Markov property, and the definition of the Lévy snake that, under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}^{\bullet}
and conditionally on
(\rho_{U},\eta_{U},\overline{W}_{U})
, the processes
\big(\rho,\overline{W}\big)^{\leftarrow,U,\prime}
,
\big(\rho,\overline{W}\big)^{\rightarrow,U,\prime}
,
\big(\rho,\overline{W}\big)^{\leftarrow,U,\prime\prime}
and
\big(\rho,\overline{W}\big)^{\rightarrow,U,\prime\prime}
are independent and with respective distributions
\mathbb{P}_{\eta_{U}^{\prime},\rho_{U}^{\prime},\overline{W}_{U}^{\prime}}^{\dagger}
,
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{U}^{\prime},\overline{W}_{U}^{\prime}}^{\dagger}
,
\mathbb{P}_{\eta_{U}^{\prime\prime},\rho_{U}^{\prime\prime},\overline{W}_{U}^{\prime\prime}}^{\dagger}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{U}^{\prime\prime},\overline{W}_{U}^{\prime\prime}}^{\dagger}
. This fact applied to the left-hand side of (
7.16
), paired with an application of Propositions
6.2
and
7.2

to the right-hand side of (
7.16
) yields that the proof of (
7.16
) can be reduced to establishing that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma(W)}{\mathrm{d}}s\penalty\ \Phi\big(\rho_{s}^{\prime},\eta_{s}^{\prime},\overline{W}_{s}^{\prime}\big)\cdot\Psi\big(\rho_{s}^{\prime\prime},\eta_{s}^{\prime\prime},\overline{W}_{s}^{\prime\prime}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma(W)}\mathrm{d}A_{s}\penalty\ \Phi\big(\rho_{s},\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s}\big)\cdot\mathbf{N}_{x,\widehat{\Lambda}_{s}}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma(W)}{\mathrm{d}}t\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{t})=\infty\}}\Psi(\rho_{t},\eta_{t},\overline{W}_{t})\Big)\Big),

(7.17)

where now
\Phi,\Psi
are non-negative measurable functions on
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}
.
By (
6.3
), the left-hand side in the last display is given by

\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}a\,\exp(-\alpha a)\,\Pi_{x,0}\otimes E^{0}\Big(\Phi\big(\mathcal{J}_{\varsigma_{x}(\xi^{a})},\widecheck{\mathcal{J}}_{\varsigma_{x}(\xi^{a})},(\overline{\xi}_{t}:0\leq t\leq{\varsigma_{x}(\xi^{a})})\big)\cdot\Psi\big(\uptheta_{\varsigma_{x}(\xi^{a})}(\mathcal{J}_{a},\widecheck{\mathcal{J}}_{a},\overline{\xi^{a}})\big)\Big)

(7.18)

where to simplify notation, we write
\xi^{a},\overline{\xi^{a}}
for
(\xi_{t}:0\leq t\leq a)
and
(\overline{\xi}_{t}:0\leq t\leq a)
respectively. This expression can now be simplified by making use of excursion theory for
\overline{\xi}
under
\Pi_{x,0}
. Under
\Pi_{x,0}
and for
r\geq 0
, let
\tau_{r}^{+}
be the right limit at
r\geq 0
of the process
(\tau_{t})_{t\geq 0}
defined before Proposition
7.2
. In particular,
(\tau_{t}^{+})_{t\geq 0}
is a subordinator and it is the right-inverse of
\mathcal{L}
. We write
(r_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
for the jump-times of
(\tau_{t}^{+})_{t\geq 0}
. If for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
we let
\xi^{j}
be the excursion away from
x
of
\xi
corresponding to the excursion interval
(\tau_{r_{j}},\tau_{r_{j}}^{+})
, the measure
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(r_{j},\xi^{j})}
is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(t){\mathrm{d}}t\otimes\mathcal{N}
. Since
\mathcal{L}
is constant and identically equal to
r_{j}
on the excursion intervals
(\tau_{r_{j}},\tau^{+}_{r_{j}})
, we can express (
7.18
) as follows:

\displaystyle\Pi_{x,0}\otimes E^{0}\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\exp\big(-\alpha\sum_{r_{i}<r_{j}}\uptau(\xi^{i})\big)\Phi\big(\mathcal{J}_{\tau_{r_{j}}},\widecheck{\mathcal{J}}_{\tau_{r_{j}}},\overline{\xi^{\,\tau_{r_{j}}}}\,\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\cdot\int_{0}^{\uptau(\xi^{j})}{\mathrm{d}}a\,\exp\big(-\alpha a\big)\Psi\Big(\uptheta_{\tau_{r_{j}}}\big(\mathcal{J}_{\tau_{r_{j}}+a},\widecheck{\mathcal{J}}_{\tau_{r_{j}}+a}\big),\big((\xi_{t}^{j},r_{j}):0\leq t\leq a)\big)\Big).

An application of the compensation formula entails that the previous display writes:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}r\,E^{0}\otimes\Pi_{x,0}\Big(\exp\big(-\alpha\tau_{r}\big)\Phi\big(\mathcal{J}_{\tau_{r}},\widecheck{\mathcal{J}}_{\tau_{r}},\overline{\xi^{\tau_{r}}}\big)\Big)E^{0}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{x,r}\Big(\int_{0}^{\uptau(\xi)}{\mathrm{d}}a\,\exp(-\alpha a)\Psi(\mathcal{J}_{a},\widecheck{\mathcal{J}}_{a},\overline{\xi^{a}})\Big).

Making now use of Proposition
7.2
and Lemma
6.1
, we infer that the formula in the previous display coincides with the right-hand side of (
7.17
). This concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

We conclude the section deriving a version of Theorem
7.3
under the probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
.

Corollary 7.4
.

For every non-negative measurable functions
F,G
on
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})\times\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
we have

\mathbb{E}_{0,x,0}\Big[\sum_{u\in\mathcal{D}}F\big(\,\emph{\texttt{T}}_{u}(\rho,\overline{W}),\mathfrak{g}(u)\big)G(\rho^{u},\overline{W}^{u})\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{0,x,0}\Big[\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}A_{s}\penalty\ F(\rho,\overline{W},s)\mathbf{N}_{x,\widehat{\Lambda}_{s}}\big(G(\rho,\overline{W})\big)\Big].

(7.19)

Proof.

This corollary is a consequence of Theorem
7.9
and standard Poissonian calculus. In this proof we argue under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
and for simplicity we replace the function
F
in the statement by a non-negative measurable function of the form
F(\uprho,\overline{\omega})f(t)
for
(\uprho,\overline{\omega},t)\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Recall that
(0,x,0)
is regular and instantaneous for
(\rho,\overline{W})
and that the reflected running infimum
-I
of the underlying Lévy process is a local time for
(\rho,\overline{W})
at
(0,x,0)
. Let
(a_{i},b_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be the connected components of the complement of
\{t\geq 0:(\rho_{t},\overline{W}_{t})=(0,x,0)\}
and for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, write
(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})
for the subtrajectory associated with the interval
[a_{i},b_{i}]
. Setting
\ell_{i}:=-I_{a_{i}}
for
i\in\mathbb{N}
, as discussed in (
2.32
) the measure

\mathcal{N}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(\ell_{i},\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})}

is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(\ell){\mathrm{d}}\ell\,\mathbb{N}_{x,0}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W})
, and it is classic that we can recover
(\rho,\overline{W})
from
\mathcal{N}
. Let us be more precise. Consider the process

\Sigma_{\ell}:=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N},\,\ell_{i}\leq\ell}\sigma(W^{i}),\quad\text{ for }\ell\geq 0,

which is clearly nondecreasing and right-continuous. Note that if
\ell
is a discontinuity time for
\Sigma
, there exists a unique
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\ell_{i}=\ell
and
\Sigma_{\ell_{i}}-\Sigma_{\ell_{i}-}=\sigma(\overline{W}^{i})
. Since
\Sigma_{\ell}\to\infty
, as
\ell\to\infty
, for every fixed
t\geq 0
there exists a unique
\ell\geq 0
such that
\Sigma_{\ell-}\leq t\leq\Sigma_{\ell}
. Still for
t
and
\ell
as before, if there exists
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\ell_{i}=\ell
we can write:

(\rho_{t},\overline{W}_{t})=\big(\rho^{i}_{t-\Sigma_{\ell_{i}-}},\overline{W}^{i}_{t-\Sigma_{\ell_{i}-}}\big),\quad\quad A_{t}(\rho,\overline{W})=\sum_{\ell_{j}<\ell_{i}}A_{\sigma(\overline{W}^{j})}(\rho^{j},\overline{W}^{j})+A_{t-\Sigma_{\ell_{i}-}}(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i}),

and otherwise, we have
(\rho_{t},\overline{W}_{t})=(0,x,0)
as well as
A_{t}=\sum_{\ell_{j}<-I_{t}}A_{\sigma(\overline{W}^{i})}(\rho^{j},\overline{W}^{j})
. We stress that we used the fact that the measure
{\mathrm{d}}A
does not charge the set
\{t\geq 0:(\rho,\overline{W})=(0,x,0)\}
by (
7.4
) as well as its additive property.
Let us now infer from these facts identity (
7.19
). First, observe that by (
7.4
) we can rewrite the right-hand side of (
7.19
) in the following form:

\mathbb{E}_{0,x,0}\Big[\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{0}^{\sigma(W^{i})}\mathrm{d}A^{i}_{s}\penalty\ F(\rho,\overline{W})f(\Sigma_{\ell_{i}-}+s)\cdot\mathbf{N}_{x,\widehat{\Lambda}^{i}_{s}}\big(G(\rho,\overline{W})\big)\Big],

(7.20)

where
A^{i}
stands for the process
A(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})
. Next, remark that for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we can recover
(\rho,\overline{W})
by concatenating consecutively the three following paths

\Big(\big(\rho,\overline{W}\big)_{\cdot\wedge\Sigma_{\ell_{i}-}},(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i}),(\rho,\overline{W})_{\,\cdot+\Sigma_{\ell_{i}}}\Big)

(7.21)

where at each step we concatenate at the first time at which the initial process remains identically equal to
(0,x,0)
. This operations does not depend on
i\in\mathbb{N}
and for simplicity we denote it by
\mathfrak{G}
. With this notation in hand, we get that (
7.20
) equals

\mathbb{E}_{0,x,0}\Big[\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{0}^{\sigma(W^{i})}\mathrm{d}A_{s}^{i}\penalty\ F\circ\mathfrak{G}\big((\rho,\overline{W}\big)_{\cdot\wedge\Sigma_{\ell_{i}-}},(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i}),(\rho,\overline{W})_{\cdot+\Sigma_{\ell_{i}}}\big)f(\Sigma_{\ell_{i}-}+s)\cdot\mathbf{N}_{x,\widehat{\Lambda}^{i}_{s}}\big(G(\rho,\overline{W})\big)\Big].

For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, the first and third processes in (
7.21
) can be expressed in terms of the point measure
\mathcal{N}\setminus(\ell_{i},\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})
and the atom
(\ell_{i},\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})
. More precisely, they can be recovered respectively from the pair of point measures
\mathbbm{1}_{(0,\ell_{i})}(\ell)\mathcal{N}(\mathrm{d}\ell,\mathrm{d}\uprho,\mathrm{d}\omega)
, and
\mathbbm{1}_{(\ell_{i},\infty)}(\ell)\mathcal{N}(\mathrm{d}\ell,\mathrm{d}\uprho,\mathrm{d}\omega)
shifted by
-\ell_{i}
on its first coordinate, by making use of the construction recalled above. Thus, Mecke’s formula and Theorem
7.3
yield that the previous display is given by:

\mathbb{E}_{0,x,0}\Big[\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\sum\limits_{u\in\mathcal{D}(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})}\penalty\ F\circ\mathfrak{G}\big((\rho,\overline{W})_{\cdot\wedge\Sigma_{\ell_{i}-}},\texttt{T}_{u}(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i}),(\rho,\overline{W})_{\cdot+\Sigma_{\ell_{i}}}\big)f\big(\Sigma_{\ell_{i}-}+\mathfrak{g}_{i}(u)\big)\cdot G(\rho^{i,u},\overline{W}^{i,u})\Big],

(7.22)

where for every
u\in\mathcal{D}(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})
,
\texttt{T}_{u}(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})
and
(\rho^{i,u},\overline{W}^{i,u})
stand respectively for the subtrajectory stemming from
u
and the excursion of
(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})
corresponding with such debut, while

\mathfrak{g}_{i}(u)
is the first time at which
(p_{H(\rho^{i})}(t):t\geq 0)
visits the debut
u
. Finally, notice that for every element
u
of

\cup_{i\geq 1}\mathcal{D}(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})
we can canonically associate a unique debut
u^{\prime}
of
(\rho,\overline{W})
in such a way that

\mathfrak{g}(u^{\prime})=\Sigma_{\ell_{i}-}+\mathfrak{g}_{i}(u),\quad\text{ and }\quad(\rho^{u^{\prime}},\overline{W}^{u^{\prime}})=(\rho^{i,u},\overline{W}^{i,u}).

The correspondence
u\leftrightarrow u^{\prime}
is clearly one-to-one, and for any pair
u,u^{\prime}
in correspondence through this bijection we have

\texttt{T}_{u^{\prime}}(\rho,\overline{W})=\mathfrak{G}\big((\rho,\overline{W})_{\cdot\wedge\Sigma_{\ell_{i}-}},\texttt{T}_{u}(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i}),(\rho,\overline{W})_{\cdot+\Sigma_{\ell_{i}}}\big).

We infer from these identities that (
7.22
) and the left-hand side of (
7.19
) coincide,
which gives the desired result.
∎

7.3
The excursion process

For every element
(\uprho,\overline{\omega}):=(\uprho,\omega,\resizebox{559403}{466169}{$\curlywedge$})
in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
, recall the notation
\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega)
and
(\uprho^{u,*},\omega^{u,*})
for
u\in\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega)
for respectively the set of debuts of
(\uprho,\omega)
and the family of excursion away from
x
in the sense of Section
4.2
. For every such element of
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
we define a point measure in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
by setting

\mathcal{E}(\uprho,\overline{\omega}):=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega)}\delta_{(A_{\mathfrak{g}(u)}(\uprho,\overline{\omega}),(\uprho^{u,*},\omega^{u,*}))}.

Recall that by convention, if
(\uprho,\omega)
is not in
\mathcal{S}_{x}
, the set
\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega)
is empty and therefore
\mathcal{E}(\uprho,\overline{\omega})
is the null measure. In analogy to the nomenclature used in classical excursion theory, the measure in the last display will be referred to as the excursion process of
(\uprho,\overline{\omega})
. When working under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
and when there is no risk of confusion, we simply write
\mathcal{E}
for
\mathcal{E}(\rho,\overline{W})
. The section is devoted to proving the following theorem.

Theorem 7.5
.

Under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, the measure
\mathcal{E}
is a Poisson measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(t){\mathrm{d}}t\otimes\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}({\mathrm{d}}\uprho,{\mathrm{d}}\omega)
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}
is a sigma-finite measure and
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
is a Polish space that we equip with its Borel sigma-field
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{D})
, by standard characterisation results on Poisson point measures it suffices to show that:

(i)

For every
\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{D})
such that
\mathbf{N}^{*}_{x}(\mathcal{U})<\infty
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{0,x,0}\big[\mathcal{E}\big([0,1]\times\mathcal{U}\big)\big]=\mathbf{N}^{*}_{x}(\mathcal{U}).

(ii)

\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
-a.s., for every
\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{D})
and
t\geq 0
, it holds that
\mathcal{E}\big(\{t\}\times\mathcal{U}\big)\in\{0,1\}
and
\mathcal{E}\big(\{0\}\times\mathcal{U}\big)=0
.

(iii)

For every
n\geq 1
and
\mathcal{U}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{U}_{n}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{D})
satisfying

\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}(\mathcal{U}_{j})<\infty
for every
1\leq j\leq n
, the process

\big(\mathcal{E}\big([0,t]\times\mathcal{U}_{1}\big),\dots,\mathcal{E}\big([0,t]\times\mathcal{U}_{n}\big)\big),\quad\text{ for }t\geq 0,

has stationary and independent increments.

Let us start by deducing point (i) from Corollary
7.4
. First, note that for each
u\in\mathcal{D}
, the process
\texttt{T}_{u}(\rho,\overline{W})
restricted to the interval
[0,\mathfrak{g}(u)]
is just
\big((\rho_{t},\overline{W}_{t}),0\leq t\leq\mathfrak{g}(u)\big)
. Hence, using the approximation (
7.1
) we infer that
A_{\mathfrak{g(u)}}
is a measurable function of the pair
\texttt{T}_{u}(\rho,\overline{W})
,
\mathfrak{g}(u)
. It now readily follows from this fact and Corollary
7.19
that

\mathbb{E}_{0,x,0}\Big[\sum_{u\in\mathcal{D}}f(A_{\mathfrak{g}(u)})\cdot G\circ\mathrm{tr}(\rho^{u},{W}^{u})\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{0,x,0}\Big[\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}s\penalty\ f(s)\cdot\mathbf{N}_{x,\widehat{\Lambda}_{A^{-1}_{s}}}\big(G\circ\mathrm{tr}(\rho,{W})\big)\Big],

for every non-negative measurable functions
f,G
on
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
respectively. Since the distribution of
\mathrm{tr}(\rho,W)
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
for any
r\geq 0
is precisely
\mathbf{N}^{*}_{x}
, point (i) follows.

The first claim of point (ii) is an immediate consequence of Corollary
7.1
, which states that under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, the variables
A_{\mathfrak{g}(u)}
, for
u\in\mathcal{D}
, are distinct. Further, the measure
\mathcal{E}
can not possess an atom having
0
as first coordinate since under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, the point
0
belongs to the support of
{\mathrm{d}}A

[
37
, Lemma 4.15]
, and it is easy to check that
p_{H(\rho)}(0)
is not a debut. This proves the second claim in point (ii).

It remains to establish point (iii) and to do so, we rephrase the problem in a slightly different form. Under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
and for an arbitrary
r\geq 0
that we fix for the rest of the proof, we consider the following pair of measures

\mathbbm{1}_{(0,r]}\mathcal{E}:=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{D},A_{\mathfrak{g}(u)}\leq r}\delta_{(A_{\mathfrak{g}(u)},(\rho^{u,*},W^{u,*}))}\quad\quad\text{and }\quad\quad\mathcal{E}^{\prime}:=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{D},A_{\mathfrak{g}(u)}>r}\delta_{(A_{\mathfrak{g}(u)}-r,(\rho^{u,*},W^{u,*}))}.

Noting that for any
\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{D})
, with
\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}(\mathcal{U})<\infty
, we can write

\mathcal{E}\big((0,r+h]\times\mathcal{U}\big)-\mathcal{E}\big((0,r]\times\mathcal{U}\big)=\mathcal{E}\big((r,r+h]\times\mathcal{U}\big)=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\big([0,h]\times\mathcal{U}\big),\quad\text{ for }h\geq 0,

it is clear that to obtain point (iii) it suffices to establish that:

(iii’)

The pair of measures
\mathbbm{1}_{(0,r]}\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}

are independent, and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
is distributed as
\mathcal{E}
under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
.

In short, the idea is to encode both measures
\mathbbm{1}_{(0,r]}\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
in terms of pieces of path of
(\rho,\overline{W})
that are independent and of more tractable nature.

Starting with the measure
\mathbbm{1}_{(0,r]}\mathcal{E}
, we consider
(\mathscr{I}_{s}:\penalty\ s\geq A_{r}^{-1})
, the running infimum of

\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle-\langle\rho_{A_{r}^{-1}},1\rangle,\quad\text{ for }s\geq A_{r}^{-1},

(7.23)

and we set
T:=\inf\{t\geq A^{-1}_{r}:\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle=0\}
. Denote the excursion intervals of (
7.23
) over its running infimum occurring before time
T
by
(a_{i},b_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
and write
(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
for the the corresponding subtrajectories. Now, we claim that
\mathbbm{1}_{(0,r]}\mathcal{E}
can be recovered from the following pair

\big((\rho_{t},\overline{W}_{t}),\,0\leq t\leq A_{r}^{-1}\big)\quad\quad\text{ and }\quad\quad\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\delta_{(-\mathscr{I}_{a_{i}},\mathrm{tr}(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i}))},

(7.24)

where the truncation operator (
4.4
) is defined on elements of
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
in an obvious way. This essentially follows from our definitions but let us sketch a short argument. Fix an arbitrary atom
(A_{\mathfrak{g}(u)},\rho^{u,*},W^{u,*})
of
\mathbbm{1}_{(0,r]}\mathcal{E}
and note that the condition
A_{\mathfrak{g}(u)}\leq r
ensures that
\mathfrak{g}(u)<A^{-1}_{r}
, the inequality being strict since
A^{-1}_{r}
can not coincide with
\mathfrak{g}(u)
a.s. (indeed, noting that
\rho_{A^{-1}_{r}}(\{H(\rho_{A^{-1}_{r}})\})=0
by Lemma
3.1
, the fact that the point
0
is regular and instantaneous for the underlying Lévy process and the strong Markov property of the exploration process gives that a.s. the point
A^{-1}_{r}
is not a point of right-increase for
H(\rho)
). If it further holds that
\mathfrak{d}(u)\leq A^{-1}_{r}
, it is plain that the triple
(A_{\mathfrak{g}(u)},\rho^{u,*},W^{u,*})
only depends on
((\rho_{t},\overline{W}_{t}):0\leq t\leq A^{-1}_{r})
. On the other hand, if
\mathfrak{g}(u)<A^{-1}_{r}<\mathfrak{d}(u)
, the excursion
(\rho^{u,*},W^{u,*})
can be recovered from the piece of path
((\rho_{t},W_{t}):0\leq t\leq A^{-1}_{r})
and the atoms of the point measure in (
7.24
) satisfying
\Lambda^{i}_{0}=\widehat{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{g}(u)}
, by making use of the snake property and the definition of subtrajectory. Here, we use the fact that the value of
\widehat{\Lambda}
on each excursion component is a.s. distinct by Lemma
4.5
. We leave the details to the reader.

The previous discussion yields that proving the independence between
\mathbbm{1}_{(0,r]}\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
can be reduced to showing that
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
is independent from the pair (
7.24
).
To this end, we next show that
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
is the excursion process of a Lévy snake distributed
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
which is independent from the pair (
7.24
). In this direction and with the same notations as before, we set

\mathfrak{L}_{t}:=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}}{L}_{t\wedge b_{i}-t\wedge a_{i}}(\rho^{i},{W}^{i}),\quad t\geq 0.

(7.25)

The process
(\mathfrak{L}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
can be used to index the family of subtrajectories escaping
\overline{E}\setminus(\{x\}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
of the collection
(\rho^{i},W^{i},\Lambda^{i}-\Lambda^{i}_{0})
, for
i\in\mathcal{I}
. Specifically, for each
i\in\mathcal{I}
, denote the connected components of the open set
\big\{t\geq 0:\>\uptau(W_{t}^{i})<\zeta_{t}(W^{i})\big\}
by
\big((s_{k},t_{k}):k\in\mathcal{K}_{i}\big)
,
where
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is an indexing set that might be empty, and for
k\in\mathcal{K}_{i}
let
(\rho^{i,k},W^{i,k},\Lambda^{i,k})
be the subtrajectory of
(\rho^{i},W^{i},\Lambda^{i})
associated with the interval
[s_{k},t_{k}]
in the sense of Section
2.2.3
, translated by
-(0,0,\Lambda^{i}_{0})
. In particular, every such subtrajectory is an element of
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{x}
with
\Lambda^{i,k}_{0}=0
. Note that in the time scale of
((\rho_{t},\overline{W}_{t}):t\geq 0)
, the triple
(\rho^{i,k},W^{i,k},\Lambda^{i,k}+\Lambda_{0}^{i})
is the subtrajectory associated with the interval
[a_{i,k},b_{i,k}]
,
where
a_{i,k}:=a_{i}+s_{k}
and
b_{i,k}:=b_{i}+t_{k}
. The strong Markov property at time
A^{-1}_{r}
followed by the special Markov property of Lévy snakes established in
[
37
, Theorem 3.7]
entails that, conditionally on
\mathfrak{L}_{\infty}
, the point measure

\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I},k\in\mathcal{K}_{i}}\delta_{(\mathfrak{L}_{s_{k}},\rho^{i,k},\overline{W}^{i,k})}

(7.26)

is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\mathfrak{L}_{\infty}]}(\ell){\mathrm{d}}\ell\penalty\ \mathbb{N}_{x,0}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W})
independent of the pair (
7.24
). Observe that
\mathfrak{L}_{\infty}<\infty
a.s. since otherwise,
T=\inf\{t\geq A^{-1}_{r}:\langle\rho_{t},1\rangle=0\}
would be infinite with positive probability. Further, by the strong Markov property, the process
((\rho_{T+t},W_{T+t}):t\geq 0)
is distributed
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
and is independent from
\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{T}
; and in particular, it is independent of (
7.24
) and from the measure in the previous display. If we write
(c_{j},d_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
for the excursion intervals away from
0
of
(\rho_{t},\,t\geq T)
, we let
(\rho^{j},\overline{W}^{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
be the associated subtrajectories and
(\ell_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
the corresponding value of the local time of
(\rho_{T+t},\,t\geq 0)
at
0
at the start of each excursion, it follows from our previous discussion that the measure

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I},k\in\mathcal{K}_{i}}\delta_{(\mathfrak{L}_{s_{k}},\rho^{i,k},\overline{W}^{i,k})}+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\delta_{(\mathfrak{L}_{\infty}+\ell_{j},\rho^{j},\overline{W}^{j})}

(7.27)

is a Poisson point measure, independent of (
7.24
), and with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(\ell){\mathrm{d}}\ell\otimes\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
. Since
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
is precisely the excursion measure away from
(0,x,0)
of
(\rho,\overline{W})
, by the same classic arguments detailed in the proof of Corollary
7.4
, we can concatenate the atoms of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
according to the order induced by
(\mathfrak{L}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
to obtain a process
(\rho^{\prime},\overline{W}^{\prime})
independent from (
7.24
) and distributed
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
.

Let us now argue that
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
is precisely the excursion point process of
(\rho^{\prime},\overline{W}^{\prime})
. This would show both, that
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
is distributed as
\mathcal{E}
under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, and by our previous discussion that it is independent from the pair (
7.24
). Note that this will conclude the proof of point (iii’). Let
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{D}(\rho^{\prime},W^{\prime})
be the collection of debut times of
(\rho^{\prime},\overline{W}^{\prime})
, and write
(\rho^{\prime u^{\prime}},W^{\prime u^{\prime}})_{u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}
for the corresponding family of excursions. We set

\mathcal{E}(\rho^{\prime},\overline{W}^{\prime})=\sum_{u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}\delta_{(A_{g(u^{\prime})}(\rho^{\prime},W^{\prime}),\rho^{\prime u^{\prime}},W^{\prime u^{\prime}})}

for the associated excursion process. Since any debut such that
\mathfrak{g}(u)>A^{-1}_{r}
is necessarily an excursion away from
x
for either, a subtrajectory
(\rho^{i,k},W^{i,k})
for some
i\in\mathcal{I}
,
k\in\mathcal{K}_{i}
, or for some
(\rho^{j},W^{j})
for some
j\in\mathcal{J}
, from our construction of the process
(\rho^{\prime},\overline{W}^{\prime})
it follows that there exists a bijection between
\{u\in\mathcal{D}:\mathfrak{g}(u)>A^{-1}_{r}\}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. More precisely, for every debut point
u
in the set
\{u\in\mathcal{D}:\mathfrak{g}(u)>A^{-1}_{r}\}
, we can find
u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
with
W^{u}=W^{\prime u^{\prime}}
. Moreover, as we shall now briefly sketch, for such a pair
u,u^{\prime}
it holds that

A_{\mathfrak{g}(u)}(\rho,\overline{W})-r=A_{\mathfrak{g}(u^{\prime})}(\rho^{\prime},\overline{W}^{\prime}),

(7.28)

proving that
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
is precisely
\mathcal{E}(\rho^{\prime},\overline{W}^{\prime})
.
We stress however that the processes
(A_{A^{-1}_{r}+t}-r:t\geq 0)
and
A(\rho^{\prime},\overline{W}^{\prime})
do differ. Let us briefly explain why the identity in the last display holds. On one hand, by equations (4.29) and equation (4.30) in
[
37
]
we can write

A_{t}(\rho,\overline{W})-r=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I},k\in\mathcal{K}_{i}}A_{t\wedge b_{i,k}-t\wedge a_{i,k}}(\rho^{i,k},{W}^{i,k})+\sum\limits_{j\in\mathcal{J}}A_{t\wedge d_{j}-t\wedge c_{j}}(\rho^{i},{W}^{i}),\quad\text{ for }t\geq A^{-1}_{r}.

(7.29)

The process
A(\rho^{\prime},\overline{W}^{\prime})
can be decomposed as well in a similar form. More precisely, since by constriction the collection
(\rho^{i,k},\overline{W}^{i,k})_{i\in\mathcal{I},k\in\mathcal{K}_{i}}
,
(\rho^{j},\overline{W}^{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
are precisely the excursions away from
(0,x,0)
of
(\rho^{\prime},\overline{W}^{\prime})
, by making use of (
7.4
) we can as well write an analogous decomposition for
A(\rho^{\prime},\overline{W}^{\prime})
in terms of this family of subtrajectories. A direct comparison between the latter and (
7.29
) readily
yields identity (
7.28
).
∎

8
The exit local time under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
and
\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}

We work with an arbitrary fixed
r\geq 0
. In this section we aim to develop a theory analogous to that of exit local times under the measures
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
and
\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}
. We start in Section
8.1
by studying the process
L
introduced in Section
4.1
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
, and we establish a special Markov property, similar to the one proved in Theorem 3.7 of
[
37
]
. In Section
8.2
, we extend this theory to the excursion measure
\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}
. Finally, in Section
8.3
we derive a spinal decomposition under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
with respect to a point sampled uniformly according to the measure
{\mathrm{d}}L
. As an application, we identify the distribution of
L_{\sigma}
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
.

Let us start by introducing some notation and by recalling standard results on Lévy snakes that will be used frequently in our reasoning. In this direction, remark that for every
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, by property (iii) of Section
2.2.2
and by the definition of
\mathbf{P}_{\mu}
given in (
2.20
), we have:

\text{Leb}\big(\{s\geq 0:H(\rho_{s})=\inf_{[0,s]}H(\rho)\}\big)=0,\quad\mathbf{P}_{\mu}-\text{a.s.}

(8.1)

Next, fix an arbitrary element
(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})
of
\overline{\Theta}_{x}
. Under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\dagger}
, write
(a_{i},b_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
for the connected components of
\{s\geq 0:H(\rho_{s})-\inf_{[0,s]}H(\rho)>0\}
and for every
i\in\mathcal{I}
, let
(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i}):=(\rho^{i},W^{i},\Lambda^{i})
be the subtrajectory associated with the interval
[a_{i},b_{i}]
. It was shown in
[
17
, Lemma 4.2.4]
that the measure

\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\delta_{(H(\rho_{a_{i}}),\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})}

(8.2)

is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\mathbb{N}_{\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}(h)}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W})
. By elementary properties of Poisson point measures, the condition
(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
ensures that
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\dagger}
-a.s. we have
W_{0}^{i}\neq x
, for every
i\in\mathcal{I}
.
Finally, for latter use, we note that equation (4.11) in
[
17
]
and Lemma
4.1
entail that, under
\mathbb{N}_{y}
for
y\neq x
, the process
L
is the exit local time of
(\rho,W)
from the domain
E\setminus\{x\}
. In particular, the first moment formula
[
17
, Proposition 4.3.2]
gives:

\mathbb{N}_{y}(L_{\sigma})=\Pi_{y}\big(\exp(-\alpha\uptau)\big),\quad\text{ for all }y\in E\setminus\{x\}.

(8.3)

8.1
The special Markov property

Recall
the notation
(\varepsilon_{k})_{k\geq 0}
for the sequence used in the definition (
4.5
) of the functional
L
. The goal of the section is to prove the following result.

Proposition 8.1
.

Under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
, the following convergence holds

\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{t\geq 0}\Big|L_{t}(\rho,W)-\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\uptau(W_{s})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}\Big|=0,\quad\text{a.e.}

(8.4)

Furthermore, if under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
we write
(s_{i},t_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
for the connected components of
\{H(\rho_{s})>\uptau(W_{s}):\penalty\ s\geq 0\}
and we let
(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
be the associated subtrajectories, conditionally on
L_{\sigma}(\rho,W)
, the point measure

\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\delta_{(L_{s_{i}}(\rho,W),\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})}

(8.5)

is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{[0,L_{\sigma}(\rho,W)]}(\ell){\mathrm{d}}\ell\,\mathbb{N}_{x,0}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W})
, independent of
\mathrm{tr}(\rho,W)
.

The first statement of the proposition is an extension of Lemma
4.1
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
. We refer to the second statement as the special Markov property under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
, by analogy with the special Markov property of Lévy snakes
[
37
, Theorem 3.7]
. In Section
8.2
, we will strengthen this result by showing that
L_{\sigma}(\rho,W)
is a measurable function of
\mathrm{tr}(\rho,W)
; its proof is postponed to Section
8.2
since it requires additional estimates.

Note that Theorem
7.3
combined with Lemma
3.1
ensures that under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
, the process
(\rho,\overline{W})
takes values in
\overline{\Theta}_{x}
. Proposition
8.1
will easily follow by combining this remark with the following technical lemma.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}):=(\mu,{\mathrm{w}},\resizebox{559403}{466169}{$\curlywedge$})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
. Under
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}_{\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}
, the convergence (
8.4
) holds. Moreover, if under
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}_{\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}
we consider
(a_{i},b_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
the connected components of
\{s\geq 0:H(\rho_{s})-\inf_{[0,s]}H(\rho)>0\}
and we write
(\rho^{i},W^{i},\Lambda^{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
for the associated subtrajectories, then a.s. we have:

L_{t}(\rho,W)=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbbm{1}_{H(\rho_{a_{i}})<\uptau({\mathrm{w}})}L_{t\wedge b_{i}-t\wedge a_{i}}(\rho^{i},W^{i}),\quad t\geq 0.

(8.6)

Proof.

Fix an initial condition
(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}):=(\mu,{\mathrm{w}},\resizebox{559403}{466169}{$\curlywedge$})
in
\overline{\Theta}_{x}
and let
T=\inf\{t\geq 0:H(\rho_{t})<\uptau({\mathrm{w}})\}
, with the usual convention
\inf\emptyset=\infty
. The statement of the lemma will immediately follow from establishing that, under
\mathbb{P}^{{\dagger}}_{\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}
, the two following convergences hold a.s.

\displaystyle\mathrm{(i)}\,\lim_{k\to\infty}\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}\int_{0}^{T}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\uptau(W_{s})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}=0;

\displaystyle\mathrm{(ii)}\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{t\geq 0}\Big|\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}\int_{T}^{t\wedge T}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\uptau(W_{s})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}-\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbbm{1}_{H(\rho_{a_{i}})<\uptau({\mathrm{w}})}L_{t\wedge b_{i}-t\wedge a_{i}}(\rho^{i},W^{i})\Big|=0.

We start with some simplifications, and in this direction the following fact will be used repeatedly in our arguments. If
\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\delta_{\rho^{j}}
is a Poisson point measure with intensity
C\cdot N({\mathrm{d}}\rho)
for some constant
C\geq 0
, then

\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}t\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H(\rho^{j}_{t})<\varepsilon_{k}\}}=C,\quad\text{a.s.}

(8.7)

This convergence is an simple consequence of (
2.26
) and (
2.32
).
In what follows, we argue under
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}_{\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}

and we start with some simplifications. First remark that, outside of a negligible set, by (
8.1
) we can write

\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\uptau(W_{s})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\int_{a_{i}\wedge t}^{b_{i}\wedge t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\uptau(W_{s})+\varepsilon_{k}\}},\quad\text{ for }t\geq 0,

and notice that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}:=\{i\in\mathcal{I}:\penalty\ H(\rho_{a_{i}})<\uptau({\mathrm{w}})\}
coincides with
\{i\in\mathcal{I}:\penalty\ a_{i}>T\}
. For any
i\in\mathcal{I}
and
s\in[a_{i},b_{i}]
, it holds that
H(\rho_{s})=H(\rho_{a_{i}})+H(\rho^{i}_{s-a_{i}})
and since the process
(\rho,\overline{W})
is a.s. in
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
, we have as well

\uptau(W_{s})=\uptau({\mathrm{w}}),\text{ if }i\in\mathcal{I}\setminus\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\quad\text{ and }\quad\uptau(W_{s})=H(\rho_{a_{i}})+\uptau(W_{s-a_{i}}^{i}),\text{ if }i\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}.

With these remarks in hand, it is plain that in order to derive the points (i) and (ii) it suffices to prove respectively that a.s. we have

\displaystyle\text{({i}')}\,\lim_{k\to\infty}\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}\setminus\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\mathbbm{1}_{H(\rho_{a_{i}})\leq\uptau({\mathrm{w}})+\varepsilon_{k}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H(\rho_{s}^{i})<\varepsilon_{k}\}}=0;

\displaystyle\text{({ii}')}\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\sup_{t\geq 0}\Big|L_{t}(\rho^{i},W^{i})-\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{s}^{i})<H(\rho^{i}_{s})<\uptau(W_{s}^{i})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}\Big|=0.

We stress that
\mathcal{I}\setminus\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
or
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
might be empty depending on the choice of the initial condition
(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})
. Let us start by proving (i’). Fix
\delta>0
and note that, since (
8.2
) is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\mathbb{N}_{\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}(h)}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}W)
, an application of (
8.7
) gives

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\to\infty}\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}\setminus\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\mathbbm{1}_{H(\rho_{a_{i}})\leq\uptau({\mathrm{w}})+\varepsilon_{k}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H(\rho_{s}^{i})<\varepsilon_{k}\}}

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{k\to\infty}\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}\setminus\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\mathbbm{1}_{H(\rho_{a_{i}})\leq\uptau({\mathrm{w}})+\delta}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H(\rho_{s}^{i})<\varepsilon_{k}\}}=\mu([\uptau({\mathrm{w}}),\uptau({\mathrm{w}})+\delta]),\quad\text{a.s.}

with the convention that
[\uptau({\mathrm{w}}),\uptau({\mathrm{w}})+\delta]
is empty if
\uptau({\mathrm{w}})=\infty
. The condition
(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
ensures
\mu(\{\uptau({\mathrm{w}})\})=0
, and (i’) follows by taking the limit as
\delta\to 0
. We now turn our attention to (ii’). Observe that by Lemma
4.1
, we have the a.s. convergence:

\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{t\geq 0}\Big|L_{t}(\rho^{i},W^{i})-\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{s}^{i})<H(\rho^{i}_{s})<\uptau(W_{s}^{i})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}\Big|=0,\quad\text{ for }i\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime},

(8.8)

and to deduce (ii’) we need a domination argument. To this end, remark that:

\sup_{t\geq 0}\Big|L_{t}(\rho^{i},W^{i})-\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{s}^{i})<H(\rho^{i}_{s})<\uptau(W_{s}^{i})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}\Big|\leq L_{\infty}(\rho^{i},W^{i})+\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{s}^{i})<H(\rho^{i}_{s})<\uptau(W_{s}^{i})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}.

(8.9)

Recalling that the intensity measure of the Poisson point measure (
8.2
) is
\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\mathbb{N}_{\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}(h)}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,\,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W})
, using (
8.3
) we obtain the bound
\mathbb{E}_{\mu,{\mathrm{w}}}^{\dagger}\big[\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}L_{\infty}(\rho^{i},W^{i})\big]\leq\langle\mu,1\rangle<\infty
. Hence, the variable
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}L_{\infty}(\rho^{i},W^{i})
is finite a.s. and we claim that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{s}^{i})<H(\rho^{i}_{s})<\uptau(W_{s}^{i})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}L_{\infty}(\rho^{i},W^{i}),\quad\text{ a.s.}

(8.10)

Note that if this convergence holds, the points (
8.8
), (
8.9
), (
8.10
) and an application of the general dominated convergence theorem
[
38
, Theorem 19, Chapter 4]
immediately give (ii’). Let us then prove (
8.10
). For every
i\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
, denote the connected components of the open set
\big\{t\geq 0:\>\uptau(W_{t}^{i})<H(\rho^{i}_{t})\big\}
by
\big((s_{j},t_{j}):j\in\mathcal{K}_{i}\big)
,
where
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is an indexing set that might be empty. For
j\in\mathcal{K}_{i}
let
(\rho^{i,j},W^{i,j},\Lambda^{i,j})
be the subtrajectory of
(\rho^{i},W^{i},\Lambda^{i})
associated with the interval
[s_{j},t_{j}]
in the sense of Section
2.2.3
, translated by
-(0,0,\Lambda^{i}_{0})
. If we let
\mathfrak{L}_{\infty}^{\prime}
be the right-hand side of (
8.10
), Theorem 3.7 in
[
37
]
entails that, conditionally on
\mathfrak{L}_{\infty}^{\prime}
, the point measure

\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime},j\in\mathcal{K}_{i}}\delta_{(\rho^{i,j},\overline{W}^{i,j})}

(8.11)

is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathfrak{L}_{\infty}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbb{N}_{x,0}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W})
. By similar arguments as before, we can write

\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{s}^{i})<H(\rho^{i}_{s})<\uptau(W_{s}^{i})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime},j\in\mathcal{K}_{i}}\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H(\rho^{i,j}_{s})<\varepsilon_{k}\}}

and since
\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}L_{\infty}(\rho^{i},W^{i})<\infty
a.s. it follows from (
8.7
) that

\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime},j\in\mathcal{K}_{i}}\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H(\rho^{i,j}_{s})<\varepsilon_{k}\}}=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}L_{\infty}(\rho^{i},W^{i}),\quad\text{a.s.}

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We can now proceed with the proof of Proposition
8.1
.

Proof of Proposition
8.1
.

Recall that under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
, the process
(\rho,\overline{W})
takes values in
\overline{\Theta}_{x}
. Therefore, by the Markov property under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
proved in Proposition
5.4
and Lemma
8.2
, it follows that under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
and for every fixed
s>0
, the process

\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\int_{s}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}u\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{u})<H(\rho_{u})<\uptau(W_{u})+\varepsilon_{k}\}},\quad t\geq s,

converges uniformly as
k\to\infty
towards a continuous function.
This fact paired with the duality property established in Corollary
5.3
yields that under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
, for every fixed
s>0
, the process

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\int_{(\sigma-s)\vee 0}^{(\sigma-t)\vee 0}{\mathrm{d}}u\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{u})<H(\rho_{u})<\uptau(W_{u})+\varepsilon_{k}\}},\quad t\geq s,

converges also uniformly as
k\to\infty
towards a continuous function. These two facts combined prove the a.e. convergence (
8.4
) under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
. Finally, the special Markov property under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
follows by the Markov property and
[
37
, Theorem 3.8]
