by similar arguments to the ones employed in the proof of Theorem
7.5
. For this reason we only sketch the main steps. An application of the Markov property of Proposition
5.4
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
at time
t>0
followed with the special Markov property
[
37
, Theorem 3.8]
yield that, on the event
\{\sigma>t\}
and conditionally on
(L_{t}(\rho,W),L_{\sigma}(\rho,W))
,
the measure:

\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{L_{s_{i}}(\rho,W)>L_{t}(\rho,W)\}}\delta_{(L_{s_{i}}(\rho,W),\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})}

(8.12)

is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{[L_{t}(\rho,W),L_{\sigma}(\rho,W)]}(\ell){\mathrm{d}}\ell\,\mathbb{N}_{x,r}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W})
independent of
\mathrm{tr}(\rho,W)
. The result now readily follows by taking the limit as
t\to 0
, noting that the first statement of the proposition ensures that
\lim_{t\to 0}L_{t}(\rho,W)=L_{0}(\rho,W)=0
a.e.
∎

8.2
An auxiliary approximation of the exit local time

The purpose of this section is to extend the notion of exit local time under the excursion measure
\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}
. Note that since under
\mathbf{N}^{*}_{x}
the set
\{s\geq 0:\uptau(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})\}
is empty, the functional
(L_{t})_{t\geq 0}
introduced in (
4.5
) under
\mathbf{N}^{*}_{x}
is identically equal to
0
, and therefore not suited for our purposes.
At this point, we recall from the discussion at the end of Section
4.1
that for any given
(\uprho,\omega)\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
we interpret the process
L_{\Gamma_{t}(\uprho,\omega)}(\uprho,\omega),

t\geq 0
as
L(\uprho,\omega)
in the time-scale of
\mathrm{tr}(\uprho,\omega)
. Since
\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}
is the law of
\mathrm{tr}(\rho,W)
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,0}
,
extending the notion of exit local time under
\textbf{N}^{*}_{x}
boils down to finding under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
a continuous non-decreasing functional of
\mathrm{tr}(\rho,W)
which coincides with
L_{\Gamma}(\rho,W)
. To this end we rely on an alternative approximation of
L
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,0}
given in Proposition
8.3
. Specifically, if for every
{\mathrm{w}}\in\mathcal{W}_{E}
and
\delta,\upsilon\geq 0
, we set:

\uptau_{\delta,\upsilon}({\mathrm{w}}):=\inf\{t\in[\delta,\zeta_{\mathrm{w}}]:\penalty\ d_{E}({\mathrm{w}}(t),x)\leq\upsilon\}

with the usual convention
\inf\varnothing=\infty
, then we have:

Proposition 8.3
.

There exists
sequences
(\delta_{k})_{k\geq 0}
,
(\upsilon_{k})_{k\geq 0}
and
(\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime\prime})_{k\geq 0}
of positive numbers converging towards
0
as
k\to\infty
such that, under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
, the following convergence holds a.e.

\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\sup_{t\geq 0}\Big|L_{t}(\rho,W)-\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime\prime}}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau_{\delta_{k},\upsilon_{k}}(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\uptau_{\delta_{k},\upsilon_{k}}(W_{s})+\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime\prime}<\uptau(W_{s})\}}\Big|=0.\quad

(8.13)

Before proving this result let us discuss its main implications. First, note that the approximation (
8.13
) does not depend on
\Lambda
or on
r\geq 0
. Hence, the same result holds under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r^{\prime}}
for every
r^{\prime}\geq 0
for the same sequences
(\delta_{k})_{k\geq 0}
,
(\upsilon_{k})_{k\geq 0}
and
(\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime\prime})_{k\geq 0}
. These are fixed from now on. With this remark in mind, for
(\uprho,\omega)\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
we define a functional
L^{*}(\uprho,\omega)=(L^{*}_{t}(\uprho,\omega):t\geq 0)
, by setting

L^{*}_{t}(\uprho,\omega):=\liminf_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime\prime}}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau_{\delta_{k},\upsilon_{k}}(\omega_{s})<H(\uprho_{s})<\uptau_{\delta_{k},\upsilon_{k}}(\omega_{s})+\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime\prime}<\uptau(\omega_{s})\}},\quad t\geq 0.

As a first consequence of Proposition
8.3
we obtain the following result.

Corollary 8.4
.

We can find a measurable subset of full
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
measure at which we have

L^{*}_{t}(\rho,W)=L_{t}(\rho,W),\quad t\geq 0,

(8.14)

and

L^{*}_{t}(\emph{tr}(\rho,W))=L_{\Gamma_{t}(\rho,W)}(\rho,W),\quad t\geq 0.

(8.15)

In particular, in a measurable subset of full
\mathbf{N}^{*}_{x}
measure, the process
(L^{*}_{t}:t\geq 0)
is continuous, non-decreasing and null at
0
.

Proof.

The identity (
8.14
) is an
immediate consequence of the approximation (
8.13
), while (
8.15
) plainly follows from (
8.13
)
and a change of variable. The last claim is a consequence of (
8.15
) and the fact that
\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}
is the distribution of
\mathrm{tr}(\rho,W)
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
.
∎

In other words, Corollary
8.4
entails that for
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
-a.e.
(\uprho,\omega)
, the functional
L^{*}(\uprho,\omega)
agrees with
L(\uprho,\omega)
on
(\uprho,\omega)
, and when taken at
\mathrm{tr}(\uprho,\omega)
coincides with
L_{\Gamma}(\uprho,\omega)
. These relationships translate as follows when working with the excursions away from
x
.

Corollary 8.5
.

Under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
and
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, for every
u\in\mathcal{D}
, we have

(L^{*}_{t}(\rho^{u,*},W^{u,*}):t\geq 0)=(L_{\Gamma_{t}}(\rho^{u},W^{u}):t\geq 0).

(8.16)

Proof.

We start proving the statement under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, and in this direction we consider the set

\Omega_{0}:=\big\{(\uprho,\omega)\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E}):\penalty\ L^{*}(\mathrm{tr}(\uprho,\omega))\neq L_{\Gamma(\uprho,\omega)}(\uprho,\omega)\big\}.

Recalling that by definition, for every
u\in\mathcal{D}
, the truncated path
\mathrm{tr}(\rho^{u},W^{u})
is precisely
(\rho^{u,*},W^{u,*})
, we can write:

\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(\sum\limits_{u\in\mathcal{D}}f\big(A_{\mathfrak{g}(u)}\big)\mathbbm{1}_{L^{*}(\rho^{u,*},W^{u,*})\neq L_{\Gamma}(\rho^{u},W^{u})}\Big)=\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(\sum\limits_{u\in\mathcal{D}}f\big(A_{\mathfrak{g}(u)}\big)\mathbbm{1}_{(\rho^{u},W^{u})\in\Omega_{0}}\Big),

where
f
is an arbitrary non-negative measurable function on
\mathbb{R}
. Now an application of Theorem
7.3
paired with (
8.15
) yields that the right hand side in the last display is null. This proves that (
8.16
) holds a.e. for every
u\in\mathcal{D}
under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
. The same argument holds under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
applying Corollary
7.4
instead of Theorem
7.3
.
∎

Finally, Corollary
8.4
enables us to strengthen the special Markov property
stated in Proposition
8.1
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
. More precisely, since
L_{\sigma}=L_{\infty}^{*}(\mathrm{tr}(\rho,W))
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
, Proposition
8.1
and Corollary
8.4
yield the following.

Corollary 8.6
.

Under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
and conditionally on
\emph{tr}(\rho,W)
, the point measure (
8.5
) is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{[0,L_{\sigma}(\rho,W)]}(\ell){\mathrm{d}}\ell\,\mathbb{N}_{x,r}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,\overline{W})
.

The rest of the section is devoted to the proof of Proposition
8.3
. In this direction, we start with a technical lemma.

Lemma 8.7
.

For every fixed
y\neq x
and
\delta,\upsilon\geq 0
,
\varepsilon>0
set:

J_{\delta,\upsilon,\varepsilon}(y):=\mathbb{N}_{y}\Big(\sup_{t\geq 0}\Big|L_{t}(\rho,W)-\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau_{\delta,\upsilon}(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\uptau_{\delta,\upsilon}(W_{s})+\varepsilon<\uptau(W_{s})\}}\Big|\Big).

The following properties hold:

\mathrm{(i)}

J_{\delta,\upsilon,\varepsilon}(y)\leq 2
;

\mathrm{(ii)}

\limsup_{\upsilon\to 0}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}J_{0,\upsilon,\varepsilon}(y)=0
.

Proof.

Both points are direct consequences of classic estimates on functionals of Lévy snakes. For simplicity, we omit the dependence on
(\rho,W)
in our functionals when there is no risk of ambiguity. Fix
y\in E\setminus\{x\}
as well as
\delta,\upsilon\geq 0
,
\varepsilon>0
, and remark that:

J_{\delta,\upsilon,\varepsilon}(y)\leq\mathbb{N}_{y}(L_{\sigma})+\varepsilon^{-1}\mathbb{N}_{y}\big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau_{\delta,\upsilon}(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\uptau_{\delta,\upsilon}(W_{s})+\varepsilon<\uptau(W_{s})\}}\big).

By (
8.3
) we already know that
\mathbb{N}_{y}(L_{\sigma})\leq 1
, and to obtain an upper bound for the second term in the right-hand side of the last display we recall from
[
37
, Lemma 2.4]
that for every non-negative measurable function
\Phi
on
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{{E}}
, we have

\mathbb{N}_{y}\big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\penalty\ \Phi\big(\rho_{s},\eta_{s},{W}_{s}\big)\big)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}a\,\exp\big(-\alpha a\big)\cdot E^{0}\otimes\Pi_{y}\Big(\Phi\big(\mathcal{J}_{a},\widecheck{\mathcal{J}}_{a},({\xi}_{t}:t\leq a)\big)\Big).

(8.17)

In particular, we have

\varepsilon^{-1}\mathbb{N}_{y}\big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau_{\delta,\upsilon}(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\uptau_{\delta,\upsilon}(W_{s})+\varepsilon<\uptau(W_{s})\}}\big)=\varepsilon^{-1}\Pi_{y}\big(\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}a\,\exp(-\alpha a)\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau_{\delta,\upsilon}(\xi)<a<\uptau_{\delta,\upsilon}(\xi)+\varepsilon<\uptau(\xi^{a})\}}\big)\leq 1,

letting
\uptau_{\delta,\upsilon}(\xi):=\inf\{t\geq\delta:\penalty\ {\mathrm{d}}_{E}(\xi,x)\leq\upsilon\}
under
\Pi_{y}
.
This completes the proof of (i) and we now turn our attention to (ii). Still for fixed
y\in E\setminus\{x\}
, note that
J_{0,\upsilon,\varepsilon}(y)
is bounded above by:

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}_{y}\Big(\sup_{t\geq 0}\Big|L_{t}-\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau_{0,\upsilon}(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\uptau_{0,\upsilon}(W_{s})+\varepsilon\}}\Big|\Big)+\varepsilon^{-1}\mathbb{N}_{y}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{H(\rho_{s}),\uptau(W_{s})\,\in\,(\uptau_{0,\upsilon}(W_{s}),\uptau_{0,\upsilon}(W_{s})+\varepsilon]}\Big).

On one hand, the first moment formula (
8.17
) gives:

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-1}\mathbb{N}_{y}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{H(\rho_{s}),\uptau(W_{s})\,\in\,(\uptau_{0,\upsilon}(W_{s}),\uptau_{0,\upsilon}(W_{s})+\varepsilon]}\Big)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-1}\Pi_{y}\big(\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}a\,\exp(-\alpha a)\mathbbm{1}_{a,\uptau(\xi^{a})\,\in\,(\uptau_{0,\upsilon}(\xi),\uptau_{0,\upsilon}(\xi)+\varepsilon]}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\Pi_{y}\big(\uptau(\xi)\leq\uptau_{0,\upsilon}(\xi)+\varepsilon\big),

and since
\xi
has continuous sample paths, this last term converges to
0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. On the other hand, (4.11) in
[
17
]
yields that for every
0\leq\upsilon<{\mathrm{d}}_{E}(x,y)
, there exists a continuous non-decreasing process
L^{\upsilon}=(L^{\upsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
known as the exit local time from the open set
D_{\upsilon}:=\{z\in E:\penalty\ {\mathrm{d}}_{E}(x,z)>\upsilon\}
, such that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{N}_{y}\Big(\sup_{t\geq 0}\Big|L_{t}^{\upsilon}-\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau_{0,\upsilon}(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\uptau_{0,\upsilon}(W_{s})+\varepsilon\}}\Big|\Big)=0.

(8.18)

Hence, to derive (ii) it suffices to establish that

\lim_{\upsilon\to 0}\mathbb{N}_{y}\big(\sup_{t\geq 0}|L_{t}^{\upsilon}-L_{t}|\big)=0.

(8.19)

It is enough to show that for every sequence
(\upsilon_{k})_{k\geq 0}
of positive real numbers smaller than
\mathrm{d}_{E}(x,y)
and strictly decreasing towards
0
, we can find a subsequence
(\upsilon_{k}^{\prime})_{k\geq 0}
along which (
8.19
) converges to
0
. Let us fix any such sequence
(\upsilon_{k})_{k\geq 0}
and note that then,
(D_{\upsilon_{k}})_{k\geq 0}
is a non-decreasing sequence of open sets containing
y
with
\cup_{k\geq 0}D_{\upsilon_{k}}=E\setminus\{x\}
. Since the closure of
D_{\upsilon_{k}}
is included in
E\setminus\{x\}
, by
[
37
, Lemma 3.6]
we can find a subsequence
(\upsilon_{k}^{\prime})_{k\geq 0}
along which
\sup_{t\geq 0}|L^{\upsilon_{k}^{\prime}}-L|\to 0
as
k\to\infty
,
\mathbb{N}_{y}
-a.e. and to conclude that this convergence holds in
L_{1}(\mathbb{N}_{y})
we need a domination argument. To this end, remark that the term
\sup_{t\geq 0}\big|L^{\upsilon_{k}^{\prime}}-L\big|
is
\mathbb{N}_{y}
-a.e. bounded above by
L^{\upsilon^{\prime}_{k}}_{\sigma}+L_{\sigma}
, which converges towards
2L_{\sigma}
as
k\to\infty
. Moreover, by continuity of
\xi
under
\Pi_{y}
and (
8.3
), we have

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{N}_{y}(L_{\sigma}^{\upsilon_{k}^{\prime}})=\lim_{k\to\infty}\Pi_{y}\big(\exp(-\alpha\uptau_{0,\upsilon_{k}^{\prime}}(\xi))\big)=\Pi_{y}\big(\exp(-\alpha\uptau(\xi))\big)=\mathbb{N}_{y}(L_{\sigma}).

Now, an application of the general dominated convergence theorem yields that
\mathbb{N}_{y}(\sup_{t\geq 0}|L_{t}^{\upsilon_{k}^{\prime}}-L_{t}|)\to 0
as
k\to\infty
.
∎

We are now in position to prove Proposition
8.3
.

Proof of Proposition
8.3
.

Recall the definition of the pointed measure
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
given in (
6.4
) and observe that, by an absolute continuity argument, it suffices to establish (
8.13
) under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
. In this direction, for
\delta,\upsilon\geq 0
and
\varepsilon>0
we set:

\displaystyle\texttt{I}_{1}(\delta,\upsilon,\varepsilon):=\sup\limits_{t\in[0,U]}\Big|L_{U}(\rho,W)-L_{t}(\rho,W)-\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{t}^{U}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau_{\delta,\upsilon}(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\uptau_{\delta,\upsilon}(W_{s})+\varepsilon<\uptau(W_{s})\}}\Big|,

\displaystyle\texttt{I}_{2}(\delta,\upsilon,\varepsilon):=\sup\limits_{t\in[U,\sigma]}\Big|L_{t}(\rho,W)-L_{U}(\rho,W)-\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{U}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau_{\delta,\upsilon}(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\uptau_{\delta,\upsilon}(W_{s})+\varepsilon<\uptau(W_{s})\}}\Big|.

Notice that under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
, the variable

\sup\limits_{t\geq 0}\Big|L_{t}(\rho,W)-\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau_{\delta,\upsilon}(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\uptau_{\delta,\upsilon}(W_{s})+\varepsilon<\uptau(W_{s})\}}\Big|

is dominated by
2\texttt{I}_{1}(\delta,\upsilon,\varepsilon)+\texttt{I}_{2}(\delta,\upsilon,\varepsilon)
. Moreover, by Corollary
5.3
we have under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
the following identity in distribution:

\big(\rho_{U+t},\eta_{U+t},\overline{W}_{U+t}:\penalty\ t\geq 0\big)\overset{(d)}{=}\big(\eta_{((U-t)\vee 0)_{-}},\rho_{((U-t)\vee 0)_{-}},\overline{W}_{((U-t)\vee 0)_{-}}:\penalty\ t\geq 0\big),

with the usual convention that the left limit at time
0
is just
0
. Thus, since under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
we have that
H(\rho)=H(\eta)
a.e., it is plain that
\texttt{I}_{1}(\delta,\upsilon,\varepsilon)
and
\texttt{I}_{2}(\delta,\upsilon,\varepsilon)
have the same distribution under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
. Given our previous observations, the convergence (
8.13
) under
\mathbf{N}_{x,0}^{\bullet}
can be reduced to establishing that we can find
\big(\delta_{k},\upsilon_{k},\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime\prime}\big)_{k\geq 0}
as in the statement of the proposition such that:

\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\texttt{I}_{2}\big(\delta_{k},\upsilon_{k},\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime\prime}\big)=0,\quad\mathbf{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}\text{--a.e.}

(8.20)

We are going to deduce (
8.20
) thanks to the Markov property and Lemma
8.7
. For each
K>1
, let us write
\Omega_{K}
for the event
\{\uptau(W_{U})>K^{-1},\langle\rho_{U},1\rangle<K\}
. In order to verify (
8.20
), we claim that it suffices to prove that for every
K>1
, we have

\limsup\limits_{\delta\to 0}\limsup\limits_{\upsilon\to 0}\limsup\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbf{N}_{x,0}^{\bullet}\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{K}}\texttt{I}_{2}(\delta,\upsilon,\varepsilon)\big)=0.

(8.21)

Actually, (
8.21
) implies that on the event
\Omega_{K}
, the
convergence (
8.20
) holds along a subsequence that depends on
K
. Since this holds for arbitrarily large
K
, by a diagonal argument we can find a deterministic subsequence
(\delta_{k},\upsilon_{k},\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime\prime})
converging towards
(0,0,0)
as
k\to\infty
and satisfying (
8.20
) on the event
\{\uptau(W_{U})>0,\langle\rho_{U},1\rangle<\infty\}
. It readily follows from Lemma
6.1
that this event is of full
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
measure, and thus (
8.20
) holds.

Let us then prove (
8.21
). Consider the connected components
(a_{i},b_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
of the set
\{t\geq 0:\,H(\rho_{U+t})-\inf_{s\in[U,U+t]}H(\rho)>0\}
and as usual write
(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
for the associated subtrajectories. The Markov property paired with the discussion following (
8.2
) yields that the point measure

\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\delta_{(H(\rho_{a_{i}}),\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})}

is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\rho_{U}({\mathrm{d}}h)\,\mathbb{N}_{\overline{W}_{U}(h)}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W})
.
By Lemma
8.2
combined with (
8.1
) and the fact that
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
is supported on
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
, we infer that under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}
we must have

\texttt{I}_{2}(\delta,\upsilon,\varepsilon)\leq\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbbm{1}_{H(\rho_{a_{i}})\leq\uptau(W_{U})}\sup_{t\geq 0}\Big|L_{t}(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})-\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{\delta_{i},\upsilon}(W_{s}^{i})<H(\rho_{s}^{i})<\tau_{\delta_{i},\upsilon}(W_{s}^{i})+\varepsilon<\uptau(W_{s}^{i})\}}\Big|,

where we write
\delta_{i}:=\delta-H(\rho_{a_{i}})
, if
H(\rho_{a_{i}})\leq\delta
, and
\delta_{i}:=0
otherwise.
Thus, with the notation of Lemma
8.7
, we get that the term
\mathbf{N}_{x,0}^{\bullet}\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{K}}\texttt{I}_{2}(\delta,\upsilon,\varepsilon)\big)
is bounded above by:

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}_{x,0}^{\bullet}\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{K}}\cdot\Big(\int_{0}^{\delta}\rho_{U}({\mathrm{d}}t)J_{\delta-t,\upsilon,\varepsilon}(W_{U}(t))+\int_{\delta}^{\uptau(W_{U})}\rho_{U}({\mathrm{d}}t)J_{0,\upsilon,\varepsilon}(W_{U}(t))\Big)\Big).

(8.22)

Then, by Lemma
8.7
-(i) we deduce that (
8.22
) is dominated by:

2\,\mathbf{N}_{x,0}^{\bullet}\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{K}}\cdot\rho_{U}([0,\delta])\big)+\mathbf{N}_{x,0}^{\bullet}\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{K}}\cdot\int_{\delta}^{\uptau(\overline{W}_{U})}\rho_{U}({\mathrm{d}}t)J_{0,\upsilon,\varepsilon}(W_{U}(t))\Big).

Note that
\Omega_{K}
has finite
\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}_{x,0}
measure since by Lemma
6.1
we have
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet}(\uptau(W_{U})>K^{-1})<\infty
. Now, the convergence (
8.21
) follows applying the reverse Fatou lemma and Lemma
8.7
, noting that Lemma
6.1
entails that
\rho_{U}(\{0\})=0
,
\mathbf{N}_{x,0}^{\bullet}
-a.e. This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

8.3
The spinal decomposition with respect to the exit local time

In this section, we characterise the law of the spinal decomposition (
6.5
) under a pointed version of
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
at a point sampled
according to the measure
{\mathrm{d}}L
. In this direction, for each
r\geq 0
we consider the following pointed measure

\mathbf{N}^{\bullet,L}_{x,r}:=\mathbf{N}_{x,r}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W}){\mathrm{d}}L_{s}

on
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}^{2}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Recall the notation
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}_{\mu,\nu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}
from Section
6
and that we write
U:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
for the identity function. Recall as well that
\uptau
under
\mathcal{N}_{x,r}
stands for the first return to
x
of
\xi
.

Proposition 8.8
.

Fix
r\geq 0
. For every non-negative measurable function
\Phi
on
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}^{\bullet,L}_{x,r}\big(\Phi\big(\rho_{U},\eta_{U},\overline{W}_{U}\big)\big)=E^{0}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{x,r}\Big(\exp\big(-\alpha\cdot\uptau\big)\Phi\big(\mathcal{J}_{\uptau},\widecheck{\mathcal{J}}_{\uptau},(\bar{\xi}_{t}:\penalty\ t\in[0,\uptau])\big)\Big).

(8.23)

Under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet,L}
, conditionally on
(\rho_{U},\eta_{U},\overline{W}_{U})
the
processes
(\rho,\overline{W})^{U,\leftarrow}
and
(\rho,\overline{W})^{U,\rightarrow}
are independent and their conditional distributions are as follows:

•

the process
(\rho,\overline{W})^{U,\leftarrow}
is distributed as
(\eta,\overline{W})
under
\mathbb{P}_{\eta_{U},\rho_{U},\overline{W}_{U}}^{\dagger}
;

•

the process
(\rho,\overline{W})^{U,\rightarrow}
is distributed as
(\rho,\overline{W})
under
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{U},\overline{W}_{U}}^{\dagger}
.

Note that the only difference with Proposition
6.2
comes from the law of
(\rho_{U},\eta_{U},\overline{W}_{U})
.

Proof.

The same arguments applied in the proof of Proposition
7.2
allows to infer from (
8.23
) the second statement of the proposition - using the strong Markov property [Proposition
5.4
] and the duality established in Corollary
5.3
. Since this type of arguments have already been detailed in the proof of Proposition
7.2
, we only prove (
8.23
). Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\Phi
is continuous and bounded above by
1
.
Noting that
\uptau(W_{U})>0
,
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet,L}
-a.e., and that
\uptau>0
,
\mathcal{N}_{x,r}
-a.e., by the monotone convergence theorem it suffices to establish that we have

\mathbf{N}_{x,r}^{\bullet,L}\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{U})>\delta\}}\Phi\big(\rho_{U},\eta_{U},\overline{W}_{U}\big)\big)=E^{0}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{x,r}\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau>\delta\}}\exp(-\alpha\cdot\uptau)\Phi\big(\mathcal{J}_{\uptau},\widecheck{\mathcal{J}}_{\uptau},(\bar{\xi}_{t}:\penalty\ t\in[0,\uptau])\big)\Big),

(8.24)

for every
\delta>0
. To this end, note first that by Proposition
6.1
, for every
k\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\delta<\uptau(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\uptau(W_{s})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}\Phi(\rho_{s},\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s})\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}E^{0}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{x,r}\Big(\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}a\,e^{-\alpha a}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\delta<\uptau<a<\uptau+\varepsilon_{k}\}}\Phi(\mathcal{J}_{a},\widecheck{\mathcal{J}}_{a},\big(\overline{\xi}_{t}:\penalty\ t\in[0,a])\big)\Big).

(8.25)

Since
\mathcal{N}_{x,r}(\uptau>\delta)<\infty
, an application of the dominated convergence theorem yields that the term in the right-hand side of the previous display converges towards the right-hand side of (
8.24
).
Now, it remains to show that

\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{s})>\delta\}}\Phi\big(\rho_{s},\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s}\big)\Big)=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\delta<\uptau(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\uptau(W_{s})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}\Phi\big(\rho_{s},\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s}\big)\Big).

(8.26)

Since by Proposition
8.1
we have:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{s})>\delta\}}\Phi\big(\rho_{s},\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s}\big)=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\delta<\uptau(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\uptau(W_{s})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}\Phi(\rho_{s},\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s}),\quad\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\text{-a.e.},

(8.27)

by the general dominated convergence theorem it suffices to prove (
8.26
) for
\Phi
identically equal to
1
. Note that Fatou’s Lemma and (
8.25
) already give that
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{s})>\delta\}}\big)
is bounded above by
\mathcal{N}_{x,r}(\uptau>\delta)<\infty
. Finally, by the special Markov property of Corollary
8.6
, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\delta<\uptau(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\uptau(W_{s})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{s})>\delta\}}\Big)\cdot N\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H(\rho_{s})<\varepsilon_{k}\}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\uptau(W_{s})>\delta\}}\Big)\cdot\frac{1-\exp(-\alpha\varepsilon_{k})}{\alpha\varepsilon_{k}},

where in the second equality we used (
6.2
) and the convention
(1-\exp(-\alpha\varepsilon_{k}))/(\alpha\varepsilon_{k})=1
if
\alpha=0
. The convergence (
8.26
) with
\Phi\equiv 1
now follows taking the limit as
k\to\infty
in the last display.
∎

We will now make use of Proposition
8.8
to identify the distribution of
L_{\sigma}
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
. For
\lambda\geq 0
and
y\neq x
we set
u_{\lambda}(y):=\mathbb{N}_{y,0}(1-\exp(-\lambda{L}_{\sigma}))
. By
[
37
, Proposition 4.7]
, the function

\widetilde{\psi}(\lambda):=\mathcal{N}\Big(\int_{0}^{\uptau}{\mathrm{d}}h\penalty\ \psi\big(u_{\lambda}(\xi_{h})\big)\Big),\quad\text{ for }\,\lambda\geq 0,

(8.28)

is the characteristic exponent of a Lévy tree - in the sense that it is the Laplace exponent of a Lévy process satisfying conditions (A1) – (A4). In particular, it is of the form

\widetilde{\psi}(\lambda)=\widetilde{\alpha}\lambda+\widetilde{\beta}\lambda^{2}+\int_{(0,\infty)}\widetilde{\pi}({\mathrm{d}}\ell)\big(\exp(-\lambda\ell)-1+\lambda\ell\big),\quad\lambda\geq 0,

(8.29)

for some constants
\widetilde{\alpha},\widetilde{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and a Lévy measure
\widetilde{\pi}
on
(0,\infty)
satisfying
\int\widetilde{\pi}({\mathrm{d}}\ell)\penalty\ (\ell\wedge\ell^{2})<\infty
. Moreover
[
37
, Corollary 4.9]
gives that

\widetilde{\beta}=0\quad\quad\text{ and }\quad\quad\widetilde{\alpha}=\mathcal{N}(1-\exp(-\alpha\uptau)).

(8.30)

Note that
\widetilde{\psi}
does not have Gaussian component.

Proposition 8.9
.

The image measure of
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
by
L_{\sigma}
, restricted to
(0,\infty)
, is
\widetilde{\pi}({\mathrm{d}}\ell)
.

In other words, the distribution of
L_{\sigma}
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}(\cdot\cap\{L_{\sigma}>0\})
is precisely
\widetilde{\pi}({\mathrm{d}}\ell)
. We stress that the proposition provides no information about the measure of the event
\{L_{\sigma}=0\}
. This set might have positive
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
-measure in general.

Proof.

Since the Laplace exponent
\widetilde{\psi}
has no Brownian component, this is equivalent to proving that

\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\big(\exp(-\lambda L_{\sigma})-1+\lambda L_{\sigma}\big)=\widetilde{\psi}(\lambda)-\widetilde{\alpha}\lambda,

(8.31)

for every
\lambda\geq 0
. In this direction, note that

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\big(\exp(-\lambda L_{\sigma})-1+\lambda L_{\sigma}\big)

\displaystyle=\lambda\cdot\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}\,\Big(1-\exp(-\lambda\int_{s}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{u})\Big)\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda\cdot\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{s},\overline{W}_{s}}^{\dagger}\Big(1-\exp(-\lambda\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{u})\Big)\right),

(8.32)

where in the last equality we used the strong Markov property under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
. In order to compute the last expression, recall that the point measure (
8.2
) under
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}_{\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}
is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\mathbb{N}_{\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}(h)}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W})
. Lemma
8.1
and the formula for the Laplace transform of integrals with respect to Poisson point measures yields that for any
(\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\overline{{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\dagger}\big[\exp(-\lambda L_{\sigma})\big]{=}\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\uptau({\mathrm{w}})}\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\,\mathbb{N}_{{\mathrm{w}}(h)}\big(1-\exp(-\lambda L_{\sigma})\big)\Big){=}\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\uptau({\mathrm{w}})}\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\,u_{\lambda}\big({\mathrm{w}}(h)\big)\Big).

Getting back to (
8.32
), since
(\rho,\overline{W})
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,0}
takes values in
\overline{\Theta}_{x}
, we infer from the previous display and Proposition
8.8
that:

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\big(\exp(-\lambda L_{\sigma})-1+\lambda L_{\sigma}\big)

\displaystyle=\lambda\cdot\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}\,\Big(1-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\uptau(W_{s})}\rho_{s}({\mathrm{d}}h)\,u_{\lambda}\big(W_{s}(h)\big)\Big)\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\lambda\cdot E^{0}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{x,r}\Big(\exp(-\alpha\uptau)\Big(1-\exp\big(-\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{\uptau}({\mathrm{d}}h)\,u_{\lambda}(\xi_{h})\big)\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\lambda\cdot E^{0}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{x,r}\Big(\exp(-\alpha\uptau)-\exp\big(-\int_{0}^{\uptau}{\mathrm{d}}h\,\psi(u_{\lambda}(\xi_{h})\big)/u_{\lambda}(\xi_{h})\big)\Big),

where in the last equality we used that
J_{\infty}
under
P^{0}
is the jump measure of a subordinator with Laplace exponent
\psi(\lambda)/\lambda-\alpha
. Finally, noting that the last term in the previous display writes:

\lambda\cdot E^{0}\otimes\mathcal{N}\Big(1-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\uptau}{\mathrm{d}}h\,\psi(u_{\lambda}(\xi_{h})\big)/u_{\lambda}(\xi_{h})\Big)\Big)-\lambda\cdot E^{0}\otimes\mathcal{N}\big(1-\exp(-\alpha\uptau)\big),

the desired equality (
8.31
) now follows immediately by applying identity (4.21) in
[
37
]
.
∎

9
The excursion process and the tree coded by the local time

The goal of this section is to prove that the genealogy of the excursions as well as their respective boundary sizes can be encoded in a random real tree. In the process, our study will shed light on the law of the excursion process
\mathcal{E}
under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, thereby complementing the statement of Theorem
7.5
which solely concerned the law of
\mathcal{E}
under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
. In this direction, recall
the definition of
A
given in (
7.2
) and for every element
(\uprho,\overline{\omega})=(\uprho,\omega,\resizebox{559403}{466169}{$\curlywedge$})
of
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
write
\widetilde{H}(\uprho,\overline{\omega})
for the process defined by the relation

\widetilde{H}_{t}(\uprho,\overline{\omega}):=\widehat{\text{\resizebox{559403}{466169}{$\curlywedge$}}}_{A^{-1}_{t}(\uprho,\overline{\omega})},\quad t\geq 0.

(9.1)

By convention,
\widetilde{H}(\uprho,\overline{\omega})
is set as the path identically equal to
\widehat{\text{\resizebox{559403}{466169}{$\curlywedge$}}}_{0}
if the convergence in (
7.2
)
does not hold
18
18

18

When working under measures associated to Lévy snakes, this will not occur outside of a negligible set, but we give a general definition in order to have a comprehensible framework.
uniformly in compacts intervals (with a limit instead of a liminf). As usual, when there is no risk of confusion we will drop the dependence on
(\uprho,\overline{\omega})
. The process
\widetilde{H}
was studied under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
and
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
in
[
37
]
and for latter use we recall some of its main properties.

First, recall from (
7.3
) that

\text{supp }{\mathrm{d}}A=[0,\sigma]\setminus\mathcal{C}^{*},\quad\text{ under }\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}\text{ and }\mathbb{N}_{x,0},

(9.2)

where
\mathcal{C}^{*}
stands for the constancy intervals of
\widehat{\Lambda}
. As a consequence, the process
\widetilde{H}
under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
and
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
has continuous sample paths, and Theorem 5.1 in
[
37
]
(see as well the discussion right-after) states that
\widetilde{H}
is distributed as the height process of a
\widetilde{\psi}
-Lévy process and of a
\widetilde{\psi}
-Lévy excursion respectively. Hence, under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
and
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, the real tree
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{H}}
is well-defined, and it is distributed as a forest of
\widetilde{\psi}
-Lévy trees and as a
\widetilde{\psi}
-Lévy tree respectively, in the sense of Section
2.2.1
. Heuristically, since the constancy intervals of
A
coincide precisely with those of
\widehat{\Lambda}
, the tree
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{H}}
is obtained from
\mathcal{T}_{H}
by identifying each component
\mathcal{C}_{u}
for
u\in\mathcal{D}
in a single point, say
[u]
. The height (or distance to the root) of
[u]
in
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{H}}
is then given by
\widehat{\Lambda}_{u}
, the value of the local time in the corresponding excursion component. The tree
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{H}}
codes the genealogy of the excursion components, in the sense that if
u\succeq u^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{T}_{H}
, we still have
[u]\succeq[u^{\prime}]
in
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{H}}
. This heuristic discussion can be made precise by making use of the notion of “subordination of trees by continuous non-decreasing functions”, introduced and studied in
[
27
]
, when combined with results from
[
37
, Section 5]
. We refer to
[
27
]
for related work and applications to Brownian geometry, as well as to
[
9
]
for connected results in the context of superprocesses. More precisely,
we let
\widetilde{d}
be the pseudo-distance in
\mathcal{T}_{H}
defined by the relation

\widetilde{d}(a,b):=\widehat{\Lambda}_{a}+\widehat{\Lambda}_{b}-2\cdot\widehat{\Lambda}_{a\curlywedge b},\quad a,b\in\mathcal{T}_{H}.

For any
a,b\in\mathcal{T}_{H}
, we shall write
a\approx b
if and only if
\widetilde{d}(a,b)=0
or equivalently, if
\widehat{\Lambda}_{a}=\widehat{\Lambda}_{b}=\widehat{\Lambda}_{a\curlywedge b}
. Note that in particular, for any
a,b\in\mathcal{C}_{u}
we have that
a\approx b
by Lemma
4.5
. It readily follows that
\approx
is an equivalence relation on
\mathcal{T}_{H}
, and by
[
37
, Theorem 5.1]
and the discussion therein the metric space
(\mathcal{T}_{H}/\approx,\tilde{d})
pointed at the equivalence class of the root of
\mathcal{T}_{H}
is a pointed
\mathbb{R}
-tree isometric to
(\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{H}},d_{\widetilde{H}},p_{\widetilde{H}}(0))
.
19
19

19

In the sense that there exists an isometry between both metric spaces preserving the roots.

Before stating our main result and its implications we first need to introduce some notation. For every
(\uprho,\overline{\omega})=(\uprho,\omega,\resizebox{559403}{466169}{$\curlywedge$})
in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
, we partition the collection of debuts points
\mathcal{D}(\uprho,{\omega})
in the two following sets

\mathcal{D}_{+}(\uprho,{\omega}):=\{u\in\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega):\penalty\ L_{\sigma}^{*}(\uprho^{u,*},\omega^{u,*})>0\},\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\mathcal{D}_{0}(\uprho,{\omega}):=\{u\in\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega):\penalty\ L_{\sigma}^{*}(\uprho^{u,*},\omega^{u,*})=0\},

and we introduce the point measure:

\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\uprho,\overline{\omega}):=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{D}_{+}(\uprho,\omega)}\delta_{A_{\mathfrak{g}(u)}(\uprho,\overline{\omega}),\,L_{\sigma}^{*}(\uprho^{u,*},\omega^{u,*})}.

(9.3)

Recall that by convention
\mathcal{D}(\uprho,\omega)=\emptyset
when
(\uprho,{\omega})
does not belong to
\mathcal{S}_{x}
. Theorem
7.5
, Proposition
8.9
and classic properties of Poisson measures yield that under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, the measure
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}
is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(t){\mathrm{d}}t\widetilde{\pi}({\mathrm{d}}\ell)
, where we recall from Section
8.3
that
\widetilde{\pi}
is the Lévy measure of
\widetilde{\psi}
. Recalling the form of
\widetilde{\psi}
from (
8.29
) and the fact that
\int\widetilde{\pi}({\mathrm{d}}\ell)\penalty\ (\ell\wedge\ell^{2})<\infty
, under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
we can construct a
\widetilde{\psi}
-Lévy process by setting

\widetilde{X}_{t}\coloneqq\widetilde{\alpha}t+\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{(c)}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}\ell)\penalty\ \ell,\quad t\geq 0,

(9.4)

for
\widetilde{\alpha}
as in (
8.30
) and where
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{(c)}
stands for
the compensated Poisson measure
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{(c)}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}\ell):=\mathcal{E}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}\ell)-{\mathrm{d}}s\widetilde{\pi}({\mathrm{d}}\ell).
By considering the first excursion of
\rho
with duration greater than
\varepsilon
and the discussion at the end of the proof in
[
37
, Proposition 4.1]
, classic arguments yield that the process
\widetilde{X}
still makes sense under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
.

Theorem 9.1
.

Under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
,
the process
\widetilde{X}
is distributed as a
\widetilde{\psi}
-Lévy excursion. Moreover, under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
and
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, the height process of
\widetilde{X}
is precisely
\widetilde{H}
.

In particular, Theorem
9.1
implies that under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
and
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, the height process
\widetilde{H}
is a functional of
\mathcal{E}
. At the end of the section we will address the “inverse” problem, which consists in characterising the distribution of
\mathcal{E}
conditionally on
\widetilde{H}
.

Let us briefly discuss the main ingredients involved in
its proof. We start by proving Theorem
9.1
under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, we then derive the results under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
by standard excursion theory arguments. If under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
we write
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
for the
\widetilde{\psi}
-Lévy process associated with
\widetilde{H}
through Lemma
2.2
and (
2.26
), proving the statement of Theorem
9.1
under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
is equivalent to establishing that
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{X}
are indistinguishables under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
. Since
\widetilde{\psi}
has no Brownian component,
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
is a purely discontinuous Lévy process and therefore it suffices to show that the respective jump processes
\Delta\widetilde{X}^{\prime}_{t}
,
\Delta\widetilde{X}_{t}
for
t\geq 0
coincide. The key now is that since
\widetilde{H}
is the height process of
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
, we know from
[
18
]
that the jumps of
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
are coded in the so-called fractal masses at the branching points of
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{H}}
, see
[
18
, Theorem 4.7]
and the discussion right after, while on the other hand, the jumps of
\widetilde{X}
are by construction given by the variables
L_{\sigma}(\rho^{u},W^{u})
, for
u\in\mathcal{D}_{+}
. We will show that these two collections coincide by making use of the exit formula of Corollary
7.4
.

These arguments rely in preliminary results under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
for
r\geq 0
that we will now address. We start with a brief study of the process
(A_{t})_{t\geq 0}
under the measure
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
, for
r\geq 0
, and in this direction the following identity will be often used in combination with (
9.2
):

\{t\geq 0:\penalty\ \widehat{\Lambda}_{t}=0\}=\{t\geq 0:\penalty\ H(\rho_{t})=0\}=\{t\geq 0:\penalty\ \rho_{t}=0\},\quad\text{ under }\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}\text{ and }\mathbb{N}_{x,0}.

(9.5)

The first equality is a consequence of Lemma
3.1
while the second was already discussed in (
2.17
). Recall the notation
(\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k})_{k\geq 0}
for the sequence used in the definition of
A
given in (
7.2
).

Lemma 9.2
.

Fix
r\geq 0
. Under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
and outside of a negligible set, the convergence

A_{t}(\rho,\overline{W})=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k}}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\int_{r}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}z\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{z}(\overline{W}_{s})<H(\rho_{s})<\tau_{z}(\overline{W}_{s})+\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime}\}}

(9.6)

holds uniformly in compacts intervals. In particular, the process
(A_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is continuous and non-decreasing. Furthermore, the support of the measure
{\mathrm{d}}A
coincides with the complement of the constancy intervals of the process
\widehat{\Lambda}
.

Proof.

Recall that
(\rho,\overline{W})
is a snake path under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
. It follows from (
7.1
) that, under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
and outside of a null set, for any subtrajectory
(\rho^{\prime},\overline{W}^{\prime})
of
(\rho,\overline{W})
corresponding to some interval
[a,b]
for
0\leq a<b<\infty
, the process
A^{\prime}:=A(\rho^{\prime},\overline{W}^{\prime})
is well defined, where the
\liminf
in the definition of
A(\rho^{\prime},W^{\prime})
can be replaced by a limit, and with the convergence holding uniformly on compact intervals. Moreover, it is plain that
A_{t}(\rho^{\prime},\overline{W}^{\prime})=A_{(a+t)\wedge b}(\rho,\overline{W})-A_{a}(\rho,\overline{W})
, for
t\geq 0
. By the latter identity and (
9.2
), we deduce as well that the support of the measure
{\mathrm{d}}A(\rho^{\prime},\overline{W}^{\prime})
is precisely the complement of the constancy intervals of
\widehat{\Lambda}^{\prime}
. This analysis applies in particular to every
(\rho^{u},\overline{W}^{u})
for
u\in\mathcal{D}
, and an application of Theorem
7.3
then shows that the statement of the lemma holds at least for some
r\geq 0
. To extend it for every
r\geq 0
, it suffices to remark that, since the distribution of
(\rho,W,\Lambda-r)
under
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}
is precisely
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, if any of the previously mentioned properties failed for some
r^{\prime}
it would fail for every
r\geq 0
.
∎

As an immediate consequence of Lemma
9.2
, the process
\widetilde{H}
is continuous under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
for
r\geq 0
. In particular, we can consider the associated real tree
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{H}}
which, as usual, is rooted at
p_{\widetilde{H}}(0)
. In the next lemma, we establish the relation between the fractal masses at the branching points of
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{H}}
and the excursion-boundary sizes
L_{\sigma}(\rho^{u},W^{u})
, for
u\in\mathcal{D}
. In this direction, let
\widetilde{v}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be the unique function defined by the relation

\int_{\widetilde{v}(a)}^{\infty}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\lambda}{\widetilde{\psi}(\lambda)}=a,\quad\text{for }a>0.

(9.7)

In particular
\widetilde{v}
is continuous and we also have
\widetilde{v}(a)\to\infty
as
a\to 0
since
\widetilde{\psi}
verifies (A4).

Lemma 9.3
.

Fix
r\geq 0
. Under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
and for every
\varepsilon>0
, let
{\textbf{\emph{m}}}(\widetilde{H},\varepsilon)
be the number of connected components of
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{H}}\setminus\{0\}
having at least a point at distance
\varepsilon
from the root
p_{\widetilde{H}}(0)
. Then the following convergence holds

L_{\sigma}(\rho,W)=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\frac{{\textbf{\emph{m}}}(\widetilde{H},\varepsilon)}{\widetilde{v}(\varepsilon)},\quad\mathbf{N}_{x,r}\text{-a.e.}

(9.8)

Proof.

Since
(\rho,W,\Lambda-r)
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,r}
is distributed as
(\rho,W,\Lambda)
under
\mathbf{N}_{x,0}
it suffices to prove the result for
r=0
. We argue under
\mathbf{N}_{x,0}
and as usual, to simplify notation we omit the dependence on
(\rho,W)
and
(\rho,\overline{W})
in our functionals. Let us write
(s_{i},t_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
for the connected components of
\{H(\rho_{s})>\uptau(W_{s}):\penalty\ s\geq 0\}
and let
(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
be the associated subtrajectories. By the special Markov property of Proposition
8.1
, conditionally
L_{\sigma}
, the point measure
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\delta_{(L_{s_{i}},\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})}
is a Poisson point measure with intensity
L_{\sigma}\cdot\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
.
In particular, if
L_{\sigma}=0
the set
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{H}}\setminus\{p_{\widetilde{H}}(0)\}
is empty (since in that case
\widehat{\Lambda}
is constant) and there is nothing to prove. In what follows we argue on the event
\{L_{\sigma}>0\}
. Using again the special Markov property of Proposition
8.1
followed by (
9.5
) under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, we get that the collection
\Lambda^{i},i\in\mathcal{I}
, are precisely the subtrajectories associated with the connected components of
\{t\geq 0:\widehat{\Lambda}_{t}>0\}
. Now the characterisation of the support of
{\mathrm{d}}A
obtained in Lemma
9.2
gives:

{\textbf{{m}}}(\widetilde{H},\varepsilon)=\#\big\{i\in\mathcal{I}:\penalty\ \sup_{t\geq 0}\widehat{\Lambda}_{t}^{i}\geq\varepsilon\big\},

for every
\varepsilon>0
. Since by (
9.2
) the processes
\Lambda
,
\widetilde{H}
under
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}
differ by a time-change, the quantities
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}(\sup\widehat{\Lambda}>\varepsilon)
and
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(\sup\widetilde{H}>\varepsilon)
coincide, and we infer from our previous discussion that conditionally on
L_{\sigma}
, the random variable
{\textbf{{m}}}({\widetilde{H}},\varepsilon)
is Poisson with intensity
L_{\sigma}\cdot\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(\sup\widetilde{H}>\varepsilon)
. Since
\widetilde{H}
under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
is the height process of
\widetilde{\psi}
-Lévy excursion,
[
17
, Corollary 1.4.2]
gives that the continuous, unbounded function
(\widetilde{v}(a):a>0)
is precisely
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(\sup\widetilde{H}>a)
for
a>0
. In particular, standard properties of Poisson measures yield that conditionally on
L_{\sigma}
, the process
P_{t}:=\textbf{m}(\widetilde{H},e^{-t})
for
t\geq 0
is a counting process with continuous predicable compensator given by
\nu_{t}:=L_{\sigma}\cdot\widetilde{v}(e^{-t})
for
t\geq 0
. It is classical that the time-changed process
(P_{\nu^{-1}_{t}}:t\geq 0)
is a standard Poisson process, see e.g.
[
20
, Corollary 25.26]
, and we derive the convergence (
9.8
) by the strong law of large numbers for Poisson processes.
∎

We are now in position to prove Theorem
9.1
.

Proof of Theorem
9.1
.

Recall that under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
and
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, we write
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
for the Lévy process associated with
\widetilde{H}
through Lemma
2.2
and (
2.26
). As discussed above, we start by proving the result under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, and to do so it suffices to establish that the jump processes
\Delta\widetilde{X}^{\prime}_{t}
,
\Delta\widetilde{X}_{t}
for
t\geq 0
coincide. We will then derive the statement under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
by standard arguments.

Since we already know that
\widetilde{X}
and
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
have the same distribution, it suffices to check that for every jump-time
t\geq 0
of
\widetilde{X}
, there exists
0\leq t^{\prime}\leq t
such that
\Delta\widetilde{X}_{t}\leq\Delta\widetilde{X}_{t^{\prime}}^{\prime}
. Recall from the construction (
9.4
) in terms of the point measure
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}
that the jump-times of
\widetilde{X}
are precisely the family
A_{\mathfrak{g}(u)}
, for
u\in\mathcal{D}_{+}
, and that the respective jumps are given by
L_{\sigma}(\rho^{u},\overline{W}^{u})
, for
u\in\mathcal{D}_{+}
. We now relate these to the jumps of
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
by exploiting classical results concerning the fractal masses at branching points on Lévy trees. To this end, first note that by straightforward arguments, a.s. for every
u\in\mathcal{D}_{+}
we have:

\widetilde{H}_{(A_{\mathfrak{g}(u)}+t)\wedge A_{\mathfrak{d}(u)}}(\rho,\overline{W})=\widetilde{H}_{t}(\rho^{u},\overline{W}^{u}),\quad\text{ for }t\geq 0.

To simplify notation we denote the process in the last display by
\widetilde{H}^{u}
. For every
u\in\mathcal{D}_{+}
, let
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{H}^{u}}
be the tree coded by
\widetilde{H}^{u}
and write
0_{u}
for its root. We let
\textbf{m}(\widetilde{H}^{u},\varepsilon)
be the number of connected components of
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{H}^{u}}\setminus\{0_{u}\}
having at least a point at distance
\varepsilon>0
from the root
0_{u}
. By the exit formula
(
7.19
) and Lemma
9.3
, it follows that
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
a.s. for all
u\in\mathcal{D}_{+}
we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\frac{\textbf{m}(\widetilde{H}^{u},\varepsilon)}{\widetilde{v}(\varepsilon)}=L_{\sigma}(\rho^{u},\overline{W}^{u}).

Still under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, for each point
[u]\in\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{H}}
with
u\in\mathcal{D}_{+}
we let
\widetilde{M}([u],\varepsilon)
be the number of connected components of
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{H}}\setminus[u]
. Since
(\rho^{u},\overline{W}^{u})
is the subtrajectory of
(\rho,\overline{W})
associated with
[\mathfrak{g}(u),\mathfrak{d}(u)]
, it is plain that we have
\widetilde{M}([u],\varepsilon)\geq\textbf{m}(\widetilde{H}^{u},\varepsilon)
for every
u\in\mathcal{D}_{+}
a.s. Hence, we deduce from the last display that

\limsup_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\frac{\widetilde{M}([u],\varepsilon)}{\widetilde{v}(\varepsilon)}\geq L_{\sigma}(\rho^{u},\overline{W}^{u}),

for every
u\in\mathcal{D}_{+}
,
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
a.s. Since
\widetilde{v}(\varepsilon)\to\infty
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, we get that a.s. for every
u\in\mathcal{D}_{+}
, we have
\widetilde{M}([u],\varepsilon)\rightarrow\infty
as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
at least along a subsequence, which gives that the point
[u]\in\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{H}}
is a point of infinite multiplicity for
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{H}}
. An application of Theorem 4.7 in
[
18
]
(see as well the discussion right-after) ensures that the left-hand side in the previous display converges a.s. towards
\Delta\widetilde{X}^{\prime}_{t_{u}}
, where
t_{u}=\inf\{t\geq 0:p_{\widetilde{H}}(t)=[u]\}
. Finally, since
p_{\widetilde{H}}(\{A_{g(u)}\})=[u]
, it must hold that
t_{u}\leq A_{g(u)}
. This proves that under under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, the processes
\widetilde{X}
,
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
are indistinguishables and therefore
\widetilde{H}
is the height process of
\widetilde{X}
.

Let us now explain how to derive from this the analogous result under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
. Still under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, consider the connected components
(a_{i},b_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
of
\{t\geq 0:\rho_{t}\neq 0\}
and for each
i\in\mathcal{I}
write
(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})
for the subtrajectory associated with
[a_{i},b_{i}]
. Hence,
(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
are the excursions of the Lévy snake
(\rho,\overline{W})
away from
(0,x,0)
. Next, for every
i\in\mathcal{I}
, let us write
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{i}:=\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})
,
\widetilde{H}^{i}:=\widetilde{H}(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})
and
\widetilde{X}^{i}
for the process defined as in (
9.4
) replacing
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}
by
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
. Now, we claim that the point measure:

\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\delta_{-\widetilde{X}_{A_{a_{i}}},\widetilde{X}^{i},\widetilde{H}^{i}}

(9.9)

is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(\ell)\mathrm{d}\ell\widetilde{N}(\mathrm{d}X,\mathrm{d}H)
, where
\widetilde{N}
is the excursion measure above the minimum of a
\widetilde{\psi}
-Lévy process (with associated local time minus the running infimum, see Section
2.2
) and
\widetilde{N}(\mathrm{d}X,\mathrm{d}H)
stands for the corresponding distribution of the Lévy and height excursion process. Before proving the claim let us explain why the desired result follows from it. Fix
\varepsilon>0
, and consider
(\rho^{j},\overline{W}^{j})

the first excursion among
(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
satisfying
\sup\widetilde{H}^{i}>\varepsilon
. Then, the distribution of
(\rho^{j},\overline{W}^{j})
is precisely
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(\cdot\penalty\ |\sup\widetilde{H}>\varepsilon)
. Therefore, the law of
(\widetilde{X}^{j},\widetilde{H}^{j})
is
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}({\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{X},{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{H}|\sup\widetilde{H}>\varepsilon)
and by the claim, this distribution coincides with
\widetilde{N}(\mathrm{d}X,\mathrm{d}H\penalty\ |\penalty\ \sup\widetilde{H}>\varepsilon)
. Since this holds for every
\varepsilon>0
and by (
9.5
) we have
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(\sup\widetilde{H}=0)=0
, we derive that under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, the process
\widetilde{X}
is a
\widetilde{\psi}
-Lévy excursion and

\widetilde{H}
is the associated height process. It remains to establish the claim. In this direction, remark that, by the support characterization (
9.2
) and (
9.5
), the points
a_{i},b_{i}
,
i\in\mathcal{I}
, do not belong to
\{\mathfrak{g}(u):\penalty\ u\in\mathcal{D}(\rho,W)\}
and they are points of both left and right increase for
A(\rho,\overline{W})
. Further, since the convergence (
7.2
)
holds uniformly in compacts intervals (with a limit instead of a liminf), we must have

A_{(a_{i}+t)\wedge b_{i}}(\rho,\overline{W})=A_{a_{i}}(\rho,\overline{W})+A_{t}(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i}),

(9.10)

for every
i\in\mathcal{I}
and
t\geq 0
. It is now straightforward to deduce from our previous observations that

\widetilde{X}^{i}_{t}=\widetilde{X}_{(A_{a_{i}}+t)\wedge A_{b_{i}}}-\widetilde{X}_{A_{a_{i}}},\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{H}^{i}_{t}=\widetilde{H}_{(A_{a_{i}}+t)\wedge A_{b_{i}}},

(9.11)

for every
i\in\mathcal{I}
and
t\geq 0
.
Since
\widetilde{X}
and
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
are indistinguishables and
\widetilde{H}
is the height process of
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
, it follows from excursion theory for Lévy processes that to obtain the claim it suffices to show that
(A_{a_{i}},A_{b_{i}})
,
i\in\mathcal{I}
, are the excursion intervals of
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
above its running infimum. By (
2.17
) this is equivalent to establishing that the intervals

(A_{a_{i}},A_{b_{i}})
,
i\in\mathcal{I}
, are the connected components of
\{t\geq 0:\penalty\ \widetilde{H}_{t}>0\}
. However, this follows from definition (
9.1
), the support characterization (
9.2
) and (
9.5
). This completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

We conclude this section by identifying the conditional distribution of
\mathcal{E}
knowing
\widetilde{H}
under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
and
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
. Note that in particular, this characterises the distribution of
\mathcal{E}
under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
since we already know by Theorem
9.1
that
\widetilde{H}
under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
is the height process of a
\widetilde{\psi}
-Lévy excursion. First, by Theorem
9.1
paired with Lemma
2.2
and (
2.26
), we know that
\widetilde{X}
, its jump measure
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}
, and
\widetilde{H}
are functionals of each-other. Hence, it suffices to characterise the law of
\mathcal{E}
conditionally on
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}
under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
and
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
. Since
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}
is the image of
\mathcal{E}
under the map
(a,\uprho,\omega)\mapsto(a,L_{\sigma}^{*}(\uprho,\omega))
, the description can now be obtained by standard methods.

In this direction, by Proposition
8.9
and classic disintegration theorems, there exists a
\widetilde{\pi}
-a.e. uniquely determined family of probability measures
(\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*,\ell})_{\ell>0}
on
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
such that the function
\ell\mapsto\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*,\ell}
is Borel measurable, for every fixed
\ell>0
the measure
\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*,\ell}
is supported on
\{L^{*}_{\sigma}=\ell\}
, and we have

\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}=\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}(\,\cdot\cap\{L_{\sigma}^{*}=0\})+\int_{(0,\infty)}\widetilde{\pi}(\mathrm{d}\ell)\,\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*,\ell}.

Finally, let us write
\mathcal{D}_{\widetilde{X}}
for the set of jump-times of
\widetilde{X}
; recall these are is in one-to-one correspondence with the elements of
\mathcal{D}_{+}
by the map
u\mapsto A_{\mathfrak{g}(u)}
. If
u\in\mathcal{D}_{+}
with
s=A_{\mathfrak{g}(u)}
, we write
(\rho^{(s)},W^{(s)}):=(\rho^{u,*},W^{u,*})
.

Corollary 9.4
.

Under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
and conditionally on
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
, the point measures

\mathcal{E}_{+}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}\uprho,\mathrm{d}\omega):=\mathbbm{1}_{L_{\sigma}^{*}(\uprho,\omega)>0}\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}\uprho,\mathrm{d}\omega)\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{E}_{0}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}\uprho,\mathrm{d}\omega):=\mathbbm{1}_{L_{\sigma}^{*}(\uprho,\omega)=0}\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}\uprho,\mathrm{d}\omega)

are independent, and their conditional distributions are as follows:

(i)

The conditional distribution of
\mathcal{E}_{+}
given
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
is characterised by the relation:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0,x,0}\Big[g(\,\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}\,)\exp\big(-\langle\mathcal{E}_{+},f\rangle\big)\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{0,x,0}\Big[g(\,\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}\,)\prod_{z\in\mathcal{D}_{\widetilde{X}}}\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*,\Delta\widetilde{X}_{z}}\Big(\exp\big(-f(z,\rho,W)\big)\Big)\Big],

(9.12)

which holds for every non-negative measurable function
f
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
.

(ii)

The point measure
\mathcal{E}_{0}
conditionally on
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}
is a Poisson point measure with intensity measure given by

\mathbbm{1}_{[0,A_{\sigma})}{\mathrm{d}}t\otimes\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*}(\,\cdot\cap\{L_{\sigma}^{*}=0\}).

Furthermore, the previous statements hold if we replace
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
by the excursion measure
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
.

In other words, point (i) gives that conditionally on
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}
, the excursions
(\rho^{(z)},W^{(z)})_{z\in\mathcal{D}_{\widetilde{X}}}
are independent, and their respective distribution is given by
\mathbf{N}_{x}^{*,\Delta\widetilde{X}_{z}}
. Under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, the variable
A_{\sigma}
is a.s. infinite, and point (ii) then yields that under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
the point measure
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is independent from
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}
. Note that under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
and
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, the variable
A_{\sigma}
is
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}
measurable since it is the lifetime of
\widetilde{H}
.

Proof.

The statements under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
follow immediately form Theorem
9.1
and classic properties of Poisson point measures. The result under the measure
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
follows by standard arguments as the ones employed in the proof of Theorem
9.1
. We leave the details to the reader.
∎

10
Consistency with Abraham-Le Gall’s excursion theory

We conclude this work verifying the consistency of our excursion theory with the one developed by Abraham and Le Gall
[
2
]
for Brownian motion indexed by the Brownian tree. In this section, we assume that
\psi(\lambda):=\lambda^{2}/2
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, that the Polish space
(E,{\mathrm{d}}_{E})
is
\mathbb{R}
equipped with its euclidean metric, and we set
x:=0
. For
(y,r)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
we let
\Pi_{y,r}
be the law of a one-dimensional Brownian motion and its local time at
0
started from
(y,r)
. The local time is unique up to a multiplicative constant, that we fix here according to the following approximation:

\mathcal{L}_{t}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,1_{\{|\xi_{s}|\leq\varepsilon\}},\quad\Pi_{y,r}\text{-a.s.}

Straightforward computations give that assumptions

(

H
0

′

)

,

(

H
1

)

and

(

H
2

)

are satisfied, which allows us to consider the measures
\mathbb{N}_{y,r}
for
(y,r)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
. We write
\mathbf{N}_{0}^{*}
for the corresponding excursion measure at
0
. In this setting, under
\mathbb{N}_{y,r},\mathbf{N}_{0}^{*}
and for every
s\geq 0
, the measure
\rho_{s}
is just the Lebesgue measure restricted to
[0,H(\rho_{s})]
, and
H(\rho)
under
\mathbb{N}_{y,r}
is distributed as a non-negative Brownian excursion. Since the processes
H(\rho)
and
\zeta
are indistinguishable, in this case
\rho_{t}
is a functional of
\overline{W}_{t}
. Hence, the exploration process is of no use, and for this reason in
[
2
]
the results are stated solely in terms of
\overline{W}
(which is now a Markov process). However, in order to keep the same notation and framework as in the rest of the manuscript, we will still work with the pair
(\rho,\overline{W})
.

The tree-indexed process
(\widehat{W}_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{T}_{H(\rho)}}
under
\mathbb{N}_{0}
is the so-called Brownian motion indexed by the Brownian tree, and the work
[
2
]
was devoted to the study of its excursions away from
0
. In
[
2
]
, the authors established that there exists a unique measure
\mathbb{N}^{*}_{0}
on
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{R}})
verifying

\mathbb{N}_{0}^{*}\big(\Phi(\rho,W)\big)=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\varepsilon^{-1}\mathbb{N}_{\varepsilon,0}\big(\Phi\circ\mathrm{tr}(\rho,W)\big),

for every non-negative bounded continuous function
\Phi
vanishing in

\big\{(\uprho,\omega)\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{R}}):\penalty\ \sup_{s\geq 0}|\widehat{\omega}_{s}|<\delta\big\},

for some
\delta>0
. Then, the following measure was considered:

\mathbb{M}_{0}:=\frac{1}{2}\big(\mathbb{N}_{0}^{*}+\check{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{*}\big),

where
\check{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{*}
is the image of
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{*}
by the mapping
(\uprho,\omega)\mapsto(\uprho,-\omega)
. The measure
\mathbb{M}_{0}
was named the excursion measure away from

0
of Brownian motion indexed by the Brownian tree. Their analysis often relies on the following master formula. For every non-negative measurable functions
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\Phi:\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{R}})\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we have

\mathbb{N}_{0,0}\Big(\sum_{u\in\mathcal{D}}g(\widehat{\Lambda}_{u})\Phi(\rho^{u,*},W^{u,*})\Big)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}\ell\,g(\ell)\mathbb{M}_{0}(\Phi).

(10.1)

As we shall see, (
10.1
) is a special case of the exit formula proved in Theorem
7.3
and this fact will allow us to relate the measure
\mathbb{M}_{0}
with
\mathbf{N}_{0}^{*}
.

Proposition 10.1
.

The measures
\mathbf{N}_{0}^{*}
and
\mathbb{M}_{0}
are identical.

Proof.

Let
g
and
\Phi
be as in (
10.1
), and consider in the exit formula (
7.9
) the functional
\Phi\circ\mathrm{tr}(\uprho,\omega)
for
(\uprho,\omega)\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{R}})
. Theorem
7.3
entails that the left hand-side of (
10.1
) equals:

\mathbb{N}_{0,0}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}\mathrm{d}A_{s}\penalty\ g(\widehat{\Lambda}_{s})\cdot\mathbf{N}_{0,{\widehat{\Lambda}_{s}}}\big(\Phi\circ\mathrm{tr}(\rho,W)\big)\Big).

Recalling the definition of
\widetilde{H}
given in (
9.1
), we infer by a time-change that the previous displays is given by:

\mathbb{N}_{0,0}\Big(\int_{0}^{A_{\sigma}}\mathrm{d}s\penalty\ g(\widetilde{H}_{s})\cdot\mathbf{N}_{0,{\widetilde{H}_{s}}}\big(\Phi\circ\mathrm{tr}(\rho,W)\big)\Big).

Under
\mathbb{N}_{0,0}
, the process
\widetilde{H}
is the height process of a
\widetilde{\psi}
-Lévy excursion

[
37
, Theorem 5.1]
and by (
8.30
) we must have
\widetilde{\alpha}=0
. Since
A_{\sigma}
is the lifetime of
\widetilde{H}
, an application of (
6.2
) then gives that the last display writes:

\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}\ell\,g(\ell)\cdot\mathbf{N}_{0,\ell}\big(\Phi\circ\mathrm{tr}(\rho,W)\big).

Getting back to (
10.1
), the desired result follows by recalling the fact that, for any
\ell\geq 0
, the measure
\mathbf{N}_{0}^{*}
is the push-forward measure of
\mathbf{N}_{0,\ell}
by the map
(\uprho,\omega)\mapsto\mathrm{tr}(\uprho,\omega)
.
∎

Making use of the re-rooting formula
[
2
, Theorem 28]
and the scaling property of
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{*}
(which are not available for general spatial motions), a notion of exit local time was introduced in
[
2
]
. A more precise version, which we now recall, was later proved in the work
[
28
]
. Specifically,
[
28
, Corollary 37]
states that, if for every
(\uprho,\omega)\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{R}})
, we set:

\widetilde{L}^{*}_{t}(\uprho,\omega)=\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{|\widehat{\omega}_{s}|\,\leq\,\varepsilon\}},

then, under the excursion measure
\mathbb{M}_{0}
, this convergence holds a.e. uniformly in compact intervals, where the
\liminf
can be replaced by a limit. In particular, the process
\widetilde{L}^{*}(\rho,W)=(\widetilde{L}^{*}_{s}(\rho,W),s\geq 0)
under
\mathbb{M}_{0}
is a continuous, non-decreasing functional of
(\rho,W)
with Stieltjes measure
{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{L}^{*}
supported in the set
\{t\geq 0:\widehat{W}_{t}=0\}
. As usual, we omit its dependence on
(\rho,W)
when there is no risk of confusion.

Proposition 10.2
.

Under
\mathbf{N}_{0}^{*}
, the processes
\widetilde{L}^{*}
and
L^{*}
are indistinguishables.

Proof.

For every
(\uprho,\omega)\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{R}})
and
t\geq 0
, we set

\widetilde{L}_{t}(\uprho,\omega):=\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{|\widehat{\omega}_{s}|\,\leq\,\varepsilon,\,H(\uprho_{s})\leq\uptau(\omega_{s})\}}.

(10.2)

Since
\mathbf{N}_{0}^{*}
is the push-forward measure of
\mathbf{N}_{0,0}
by the map
(\uprho,\omega)\mapsto\mathrm{tr}(\uprho,\omega)
, we infer from Lemma
10.1
and
[
28
, Corollary 37]
that under
\mathbf{N}_{0,0}
outside of a negligible set, the time-changed process

\varepsilon^{-2}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{|\widehat{W}_{\Gamma_{s}}|\leq\varepsilon\}},\quad\text{ for }t\geq 0,

(10.3)

converges uniformly in compact intervals as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. This in turn implies that the convergence in (
10.2
) holds under
\mathbf{N}_{0,0}
uniformly in compact intervals (with a limit instead of a liminf), and that the limit of (
10.3
) as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
is precisely

(\widetilde{L}_{\Gamma_{t}}(\rho,W):t\geq 0)
. Thus, to complete the proof, it suffices to show that
L(\rho,W)
and
\widetilde{L}(\rho,W)
are indistinguishable under
\mathbf{N}_{0,0}
. Since
L(\rho,W)
and
\widetilde{L}(\rho,W)
are continuous with
L_{0}(\rho,W)=\widetilde{L}_{0}(\rho,W)=0
, it is enough to prove that
L_{\sigma}(\rho,W)-L_{s}(\rho,W)=\widetilde{L}_{\sigma}(\rho,W)-\widetilde{L}_{s}(\rho,W)
for Lebesgue almost every
s\in(0,\sigma)
or in other words, that under the pointed measure
\mathbf{N}_{0,0}^{\bullet}
we have

L_{\sigma}(\rho,W)-L_{U}(\rho,W)=\widetilde{L}_{\sigma}(\rho,W)-\widetilde{L}_{U}(\rho,W).

(10.4)

Observe that
\widetilde{L}(\rho,W)
and
L(\rho,W)
are constant on each connected component of the set
\{s\geq 0:\penalty\ \uptau(W_{s})<H(\rho_{s})\}
. Moreover, Lemma
6.1
gives
\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}_{0,0}(\uptau(W_{U})=H(\rho_{U}))=0
. Hence, it suffices to establish the equality on the event
\{H(\rho_{U})<\uptau(W_{U})\}
, and for the rest of the proof we work under the measure

\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}_{0,0}(\,\cdot\,\cap\{H(\rho_{U})<\uptau(W_{U})\}).

Now, for every
\delta>0
, set
T_{\delta}:=\inf\{s\geq U:\penalty\ H(\rho_{s})\leq\delta\}
. Remark that since
H(\rho_{s})>0
for
s\in(0,\sigma)
, the continuity of
\widetilde{L}(\rho,W)
and
L(\rho,W)
implies that
\widetilde{L}_{T_{\delta}}(\rho,W)\to\widetilde{L}_{\sigma}(\rho,W)
and
{L}_{T_{\delta}}(\rho,W)\to{L}_{\sigma}(\rho,W)
as
\delta\to 0
. Therefore, it suffices to show that (
10.4
) holds with
\sigma
replaced by
T_{\delta}
, for any
\delta>0
. To this end, fix an arbitrary
\delta>0
and let
(a_{i},b_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
be the connected components of the set
\{t\geq 0:\,H(\rho_{U+t})-\inf_{s\in[U,U+t]}H(\rho_{s})>0\}
. As usual we write
(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}=(\rho^{i},W^{i},\Lambda^{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
for the corresponding subtrajectories. By (
8.1
), (
10.2
), Lemma
8.2
and the Markov property given in Proposition
5.4
, the proof of (
10.4
) with
\sigma
replaced by
T_{\delta}
boils down to establish that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbbm{1}_{\delta<H(\rho_{a_{i}})<\uptau(W_{U})}\Big|L_{\infty}(\rho^{i},W^{i})-\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{a_{i}}^{b_{i}}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{|\widehat{W}_{s}^{i}|\leq\varepsilon,H(\rho^{i}_{s})\leq\uptau(W_{s}^{i})\}}\Big|=0,\quad\text{a.e.}

(10.5)

In this direction recall that by the Markov property of Proposition
5.4
and the discussion following (
8.2
), the point measure

\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\delta_{(H(\rho_{a_{i}}),\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})}

is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\rho_{U}({\mathrm{d}}h)\,\mathbb{N}_{\overline{W}_{U}(h)}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W})
. In particular, it follows from
[
28
, Proposition 34]
that:

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Big|L_{\infty}(\rho^{i},W^{i})-\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{|\widehat{W}_{s}^{i}|\leq\varepsilon,H(\rho^{i}_{s})\leq\uptau(W_{s}^{i})\}}\Big|=0,\quad\text{ a.e.}

for every
i\in\mathcal{I}
such that
\delta<H(\rho_{a_{i}})<\uptau(W_{U})
. The desired result now follows since, by continuity, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that there are only finitely many indices
i\in\mathcal{I}
, with
\delta<H(\rho_{a_{i}})<\uptau(W_{U})
, verifying
\inf|\widehat{W}^{i}|<\varepsilon_{0}
.
∎
